PREFACE Mastery Units in Applied Arithmetic, as the name indicates, is an organization of the work in arithmetic in units and includes provision for teaching and for assuring complete mastery of each unit by the student. It is intended for high school classes, and may be used as the basis of a course in the commercial department or for any high school class pursuing a curriculum that includes the mastery of arithmetic. 

Each unit in this book is introduced by a Preview Test to challenge the student's previously acquired skill, to reveal to both student and teacher some of the topics in the unit upon which the individual student should do intensive work for mastery, and to help the teacher to make assignments of work with respect to individual differences in ability and achievement. 

Each unit closes with a Mastery Test and a Thought Test to reinforce the work that has been done in the classroom and to disclose such weak spots as may still exist. The Mastery Tests and 

Thought Tests will enable teachers to differentiate in the further practice to be required of individual students on the topics presented in the successive units. 

An inspection of the Table of Contents will show the kinds of arithmetical work contained in each unit and the general scope of the topics covered in the ten units that constitute the book. In the preparation of this material the strong points in the earlier books by the same author have been retained. Teachers will find here an abundance of practice exercises and of problem material rich in its industrial content. The problem material, drawn from actual situations in industry and commerce, is introduced early in the book, thus serving to break up what in many books has been a dull grind of abstract mechanical exercises devoid of interest to the student. Although the book is definitely arranged on the topical plan, so as to develop specific arithmetical skills, this use of ample problem material rich in its social situations is sustained throughout the ten units. iii

iv 

PREFACE 

Practice exercises are based upon accuracy as the absolute requirement, with speed as the accompanying measure of quantity. 

Accuracy is the prime essential; but business men require sufficient speed to meet reasonable quantitative standards. This twofold requirement is met in this book by placing time limits on many of the practice exercises and tests. By using these time limits, the teacher can base the score upon accuracy and at the same time give recognition to speed as the subordinate but highly important element in full success. 

In the introduction of topics that are unfamiliar to the student, demonstration problems are used as examples to make clear the arithmetical procedure that is involved in the work. Paragraphs of discussion coordinate with the demonstration examples. 

Following these are the practice examples and the problem material, which serve to convert the learner's knowledge of theory into skills and to give permanence to the mastery of procedure.

The material in the various units has been enriched by providing brief treatments of such topics as Air Transportation, Home Budgeting, Building and Loan Associations, and other subjects not usually found in similar texts. Census figures, postal rates, import duties, and other statistical material have also been revised to conform to the most recent authentic data. The treatment of the Income Tax is based upon legislation enacted by 

Congress in 1934.

Many business men, executives and specialists in various fields of industrial activity, have contributed to the preparation of Mastery Units in Applied Arithmetic by offering valuable suggestions and by making constructive criticisms. To all these it is due to make grateful acknowledgment, and this is done with genuine pleasure. Special mention should be made of the assistance rendered by Mr. Robert Alfred Lile, C.P.A., of Russell 

Brown and Company, Certified Public Accountants, Little Rock, Arkansas, and by Associate Professor Stanley Pugh, M. A., LL.D. (Dixie University), Department of Business Administration,

East Texas State Teachers College.

MASTERY UNITS IN APPLIED ARITHMETIC Unit One IMPROVING COMPUTING SKILL 

Accuracy is the first essential in the use of arithmetic in all forms of business life. Next to accuracy in rank of importance is speed. 

The successful worker should be able to produce absolutely correct work and to do this rapidly. In order to ascertain your degree of accuracy and your rate of speed in working with whole numbers, write the results of the 

Preview Tests that you find on the next three pages.

Mastery implies personal struggle. No one else can do your part for you. First think through; then work through. The more you do mentally and without help, the stronger you become. 1

CHAPTER I ADDITION PRACTICE 

First Step. The first step in learning to add is to master all possible combinations in the addition of two numbers of one digit each. Without reversing the order, or using zero, there are 45 of these, as shown in the following table: 1 1 1 1 1 71 1 2 2 2 2

ADDITION PRACTICE 

There are 100 combinations in simple addition. These are the 45 given on the previous page, 45 formed by using the same numbers in reverse order, and 10 found by combining zero with the 9 digits and with itself.

These 100 combinations are given below arranged in the order of difficulty as shown by recent extended investigations, the most difficult being placed first.

TEACHER'S NOTE. It is not sufficient for the student to be able to compute accurately with figures that are written or printed. He should be taught to concentrate upon what he hears as well as upon what he sees. Read the foregoing numbers aloud, calling upon the students in turn for the sums. 

Do not permit the use of pencils nor allow books to be open.

ADDITION PRACTICE Horizontal Addition 

It is sometimes necessary to add columns horizontally in preparing statistical reports, comparative statements, etc. Add the following numbers horizontally: 1. 624+312+386= 327+874+472= 436+692+386= 2. 327+268+321: 726+432+729= 232+455+622= 3. 741+638+841= 783+392+396= 417+467+782=

Business Computations 

1. John Blackwell, a salesman for the Grand Rapids Furniture

Making Change 

The mental processes of addition and subtraction are so closely related that it is difficult to determine which is employed in the process of making change. Certainly the oral process is one of addition. The cashier or salesman, returning change out of $1.00 offered in payment of a bill of 19¢:

Hands customer package and says "19" 

Hands customer 1¢ and says "20" 

Hands customer 5¢ 

Hands customer 25¢ 

Hands customer and says "250" and says "504" 50¢ and says "$1.00" 

1. One dollar is tendered in of each of the following bills. payment 

State what change is returned to the customer: 14¢, 24¢, 32¢, 27¢, 42¢, 38¢, 51¢, 36¢, 47¢, 64¢, 72¢, 17¢, 21¢, 12¢, 26¢, 35¢, 81¢, 67¢, 71¢, 48¢, 54¢. 

2. State what is returned in each case, if a two-dollar bill is offered in payment of each of the following amounts: $1.41, $1.22, $1.12, $1.16, $1.24, $1.38, $1.62, $1.46, $1.28, $1.76, $1.39, $1.73, $1.64, $1.83, $.97, $.86, $.37. 

3. A $5.00 bill is offered in payment of each of the following amounts: $1.04, $2.09, $3.10, $1.76, $3.11, $4.49, $3.54, $2.97, $1.98, $3.58, $1.07, $4.02, $3.16, $4.51, $1.83, $2.19, $3.29. What change is returned in each case? 

4. A $10.00 bill is offered in payment of each of the following bills: $3.27, $4.52, $5.79, $7.38, $5.95, $3.85, $8.46, $9.12, $7.25, $1.29, $1.76, $.84, $3.81, $4.73, $6.44, $2.28, $6.54, $3.70. What change is returned in each case?

Complement Method 

A complement of a number is the amount which if added to it will make one unit of the next higher order. Thus the complement of 6 is 4, since added they make 10; the complement of 36 is 64; the complement of 475 is 525.

When a number to be subtracted is mixed in with a number of amounts which are to be added, a convenient method is to add the complement of the number and then deduct 1 from the next higher order in the result. 

398+686-475=398+686+525 (the complement of 475) — 1000 = 609

COMPLEMENT METHOD 

23 This method is valuable in connection with work listed on an adding machine which consists largely of addition, with an occasional subtraction. 

Subtraction is accomplished on the adding machine by adding the complemen、 of the number to be subtracted, and deducting 1 from the next higher order. 

Arrange the following for horizontal addition as in the illustration, but in place of the number to be subtracted write its complement, and change the sign from to +. Add, and deduct 1000 from the total.

SUBTRACTION PRACTICE 

Proof of Subtraction. Certainty of the accuracy of any result can be had by subjecting your work to a proper proof. Prove your work in subtraction by adding subtrahend and difference. 

If your result agrees with the minuend, your work is accurate. 

How a Banker Proves His Depositors' Ledger. When the balance of each account has been obtained, the work is proved as follows: 

Add the total of the first balance column and the total of the deposits column. Subtract the total of the total checks column. The result should be the total of the final balance column.

The fact that multiplication is a short process for finding the sum of a number of equal addends can be illustrated as follows: 16 16 16 16 64 "4 times 16" means that 16 is to be taken 4 times. result is secured by addition as by multiplication.

Invoices and Statements 

An invoice is a written list of the items of a sale. It should show in its heading the names of the parties to the sale, and the date. 

Its body should show the name, quantity, and price of each article, the total selling price of each item and the total of the sale. Filling in the totals of the various items is called extending the invoice; finding the total of the invoice is called footing it. 

1. On Jan. 2, 1934, Wenly & Cowling bought of Beecher, Peck, and Lewis, Detroit, Mich.: 2 boxes assorted visiting cards @ 75¢; 

200 Thanksgiving napkins @ 20¢ a hundred; 1000 napkins @ 75¢ a hundred; 3 doz. writing pads @ 40¢; 2 doz. single oak slates @ 684; 2 gr. rules @ 94¢; 6 doz. note books @ 35¢; 5 doz. pads drawing paper @ 38¢; 4 doz. paint balls @ $1.86. Terms: Net cash. 

2. Butler Bros., Chicago, Ill., sold to John Dallas & Son, Fargo, N. Dakota, on Jan. 3, 1934: 4 doz. work baskets @ 894; 3 doz. match safes @ 874; 5 doz. cloth sprayers @ 45¢; 6 doz. towel bars @78¢; 5 doz. flour sifters @ 84¢; 3 doz. strainers @ $1.84; 2 doz. percolaters @ 89¢; 4 doz. 8 x 10-inch drip pans @ 65¢; 3 doz. wash baskets @ $10.20. Terms: 1/20 n/60.* *1% off for cash in 20 days, or net price (without discount) in 60 days.

MULTIPLICATION PRACTICE Postal Rates 

Problems involving mailing expense, while very simple, are important and teach facts which everyone should know. Letters, packages, and money may be sent by mail to all parts of the United States and to foreign countries. 

Classes of Mail

Mail matter is divided into four classes arranged in the order of the cost of mailing, as follows:

First Class: Written matter, sealed or unsealed 

Rate: 3¢ per oz. or fraction, or 2¢ if for local delivery by carrier; U. S. postal cards, 1¢.

Second Class: Newspapers and magazines 

Rate: 14 for each 2 oz. or fraction, to 8 oz.

Third Class: Merchandise and printed matter 

Rates: (1) 1¢ for each 2 oz. or fraction, to 8 oz. incl. (2) Books, catalogs, seeds, etc., 1¢ for each 2 oz. or fraction, to 8 oz. incl.

Fourth Class: (Parcel Post) Merchandise and printed matter weighing more than 8 oz.

Parcel Post Rates 

For the purpose of fixing rates, each mailing point is assumed to be the center of a system of zones surrounding it. The rate is based upon the distance, and all points within a given zone have the same rate. The first zone (radius 50 mi.) is a circle with the shipping point at the center; and the other zones are broad rings, each lying outside the next smaller one. The distances are shown in the table of parcel post rates on the next page. 

The destination to which a parcel is mailed is said to be in a certain zone. Parcels weighing more than 8 oz., up to 70 lb., may be sent to any one of the eight zones. The maximum length of a package plus the maximum girth must not exceed 100 inches.

POSTAL RATES 

41 The table on this page shows the parcel post rates on packages weighing not more than 11 pounds. The complete table used by a postmaster shows the rates on all packages up to 70 pounds. 

The cheapest rate is on matter intended for delivery at or from the office where it is mailed. This is called the local rate. The next lowest rate is for matter mailed to points in the first and second zones.

4. A merchant mailed a 6 lb. package to one customer and a 

3 lb. package to another customer in a certain city in the fifth zone from his post office. How much would he have gained or lost in expense for postage, if he could have shipped both purchases in one larger carton, the two smaller empty cartons weighing the same as the one larger empty carton?

MULTIPLICATION PRACTICE Special Forms of Service. In connection with the handling of the mail, the government provides for insuring packages, registering valuable letters and packages, and transmitting merchandise C.O.D. It also provides for the special delivery of mail by messengers. Schedules of rates charged for these special forms of service have been made, and for a moderate fee any one of them may be included in the mailing of matter. 

Money Orders. If it is desired to pay money to a person without actually sending money by mail or otherwise, this can be done by the use of a post office money order. The use of money orders and the table of rates charged for this form of transferring funds are presented under Exchange in this text book.

Postal Savings Department. The government gives the people a simple form of savings bank service by accepting small deposits of money and paying a low rate of interest. The conditions governing this service are explained under Banks and Banking.

Air Mail. The newest feature of the postal service is the air mail. Mail is carried by airplane on regular routes connecting the most important cities in the 

United States. The rate on air mail is 6¢ for each ounce or fraction of an ounce. By this means an important piece of mail for which there is urgent need in a distant city, may be sent to its destination in time for use on the same day or the following day. Mail has been carried from Los Angeles to New 

York City in approximately 13 hours.

Air Mail Problems 

1. What will it cost to send by air mail from St. Louis to

New Orleans two letters, one weighing 1 oz. and one weighing 

2 oz., the delivery of the letters in New Orleans being by the regular carriers at the usual time for mail delivery? 

2. A lawyer in Chicago is sending an important document to his partner in New York City for use in court. The document weighs 3 oz. and special delivery service in New York will cost 

10¢. What will be the cost for air mail postage including the special delivery fee?

CHAPTER IV DIVISION PRACTICE 

The fact that division is a short method of finding how many times one number contains another can be proved by a series of subtractions, thus: 

How many times does 109 contain 35? 109 35 74 35 39 35 Subtracting 35 as many times as possible, we find that it is contained in 109 three times, with a remainder of 4. The process here illustrated is called continuous subtraction, and is said to be required frequently in civil service examinations. The same result may be secured more quickly by division.

2. 83 contains 12 

4. 976 contains 120 

6. 739 contains 150

Division is the reverse of multiplication and calls for a knowledge of the same tables. When the divisor is a number with the multiplication table of which you are familiar, use the short division method; if not, use the long division method.

Dividing by 10, 100, 1000, etc. 70÷ 10=7 700 100=7 7000÷1000=7 You have learned that to multiply by 10, 100, or 1000 it is only necessary to annex to the multiplicand as many ciphers as there are ciphers in the multiplier. It follows that to divide by 10, 100, or 1000 it is only necessary to take off the ciphers. Rule: 

To divide by 10, 100 or any number consisting of 1 followed by ciphers, point off in the dividend as many places as there are ciphers in the divisor. Thus to divide 7000 by 1000, point off three places in the dividend. 

The result is 7.

UNIT REVIEW Thought Test I 

(Time, 20 minutes)

Record your answers on a separate paper. Number them to correspond to the numbers in the test. Do not copy the questions. 

1. If you sold a customer several bills of goods and the customer made several payments, how could you determine the amount still due? 

2. Henry Williams made six different deposits in a bank at various times and has issued ten different checks drawing money out of the bank. How could you tell the balance in the bank, and what convenient arrangement would show him the facts? 

3. If you know the balance in the bank at the end of May, the amount deposited and the amount withdrawn during the month of May, how can you find the balance in the bank at the beginning of May? 

4. If you know the total cost of six articles and the individual cost of five of them, how would you find the cost of the remaining one? 

5. James Bronson had a certain amount of cash at the beginning of April. You know how much he received during April and how much he has at the end of April. How can you find the amount he spent during April? 

6. Williams Brothers bought a certain amount of goods during the year 1927. At the end of the year you know the cost of the goods unsold. How will you find the cost of the goods that they sold? 

7. During the year 1928, Hannan & Co. made a profit from selling goods and a profit from renting part of their store. They were at an expense in hiring clerks, advertising their goods, paying for fuel, light, etc. If you knew the amounts in each of the above items, how would you find the net profits? 

8. If you knew the amount of money with which James Kenny began business and knew how much he had gained and how much he had lost, how would you find what he is worth now? 

9. If you knew the distance from St. Louis to New Orleans and knew how far a gallon of gasoline would run an automobile, and knew the cost per gallon, how would you estimate the cost of gasoline in running from one of these cities to the other?

CHAPTER V GETTING READY FOR FRACTIONS Factoring 

The factors of a number are the numbers which when multiplied together make the number. Thus 3 and 5 are the factors of 15. 

Factoring a number is the process of determining its factors. 

A composite number is one that is composed of two or more integral factors (besides itself and 1). Thus, 15 is a composite number.

A prime number is a number which has no factors besides itself and 1. Thus 13 is a prime number. Prime factors are therefore factors which cannot themselves be factored. Thus, 4 and 5 are factors of 20, but are not its prime factors. The prime factors of 

20 are 2, 2, and 5. When the word factors is used, prime factors are usually understood.

ORAL EXERCISE 1. Name the prime numbers from 1 to 100. 

2. Name the composite numbers from 1 to 100, giving at least two factors for each.

Explanation: In naming the factors of a number you must name numbers which when multiplied together produce it, naming them all. It would be improper to say that 3 and 5 are the factors of 30 because factors (from facio = to make) are makers, and 3 and 5 multiplied together do not make 

30. The factors of 30 are 2, 3, and 5. 

3. Name the prime factors of 

18 24 16 20 28 44 52 27 45 63 81 72 36 50 12 48 54 32 60 56 66 78 40 76 90 30 98 75 70 96 88 45 80 99 53

GETTING READY FOR FRACTIONS Divisibility of Numbers. A knowledge of the following facts regarding divisibility of numbers will prove of great assistance in factoring. 

All even numbers are divisible by 2. 

A number is even if its last digit is 2, 4, 6, 8, or 0. 

A number is divisible by 3 if the sum of its digits is divisible by 3. 

Thus we can tell at a glance that 471 is divisible by 3, because 4+7+1=12, which is divisible by 3.

A number is divisible by 4 if its two digits at the right are ciphers or express a number which is divisible by 4. 

Thus we can tell at a glance that 1324 is divisible by 4, because 24 is divisible by 4.

A number is divisible by 5 if its right hand figure is 0 or 5. 

Thus, 5, 10, 15, 20, etc., are divisible by 5.

All even numbers the sum of whose digits is divisible by 3, are divisible by 6.

Thus, 354 is seen to be divisible by 6 because it is an even number and because 3+5+4=12, which is divisible by 3. 

This is a combination of the two facts of divisibility first stated. If a number is divisible by 2 and is also divisible by 3, it is clear that it is divisible by 6.

A number is divisible by 8 if its three digits at the right are ciphers or express a number which is divisible by 8. 

Thus, 14,128 is seen to be divisible by 8, because 128 is divisible by 8. 

A number is divisible by 9 if the sum of its digits is divisible by 9. 

Thus, 2439 is seen to be divisible by 9, because 2+4+3+9=18, which is divisible by 9.

All numbers ending in O are divisible by 10. 

Thus, 1280 is divisible by 10.

A number is divisible by 11 if the sum of the digits in the even places equals the sum of the digits in the odd places, or if the difference between these two sums is 11 or a multiple of 11. 

Thus, 72,919 is seen to be divisible by 11 because 9+9+7=25, 1+2=3, and 25-322, which is a multiple of 11.

GREATEST COMMON DIVISOR 

55 A number is divisible by 25 if its last two digits are ciphers or express a number which is divisible by 25. 

Thus, 1925 is divisible by 25.

A number is divisible by 125 if its last three digits are ciphers or express a number which is divisible by 125. 

Thus, 12,625 is divisible by 125.

EXAMPLE. Find the prime factors of 2730. 

2)2730 Applying the methods for determining divisibility, find some one prime factor, preferably the smallest possible (in this 3)1365 case, 2). Divide by this factor. Find another prime factor 

5)455 and divide in the same way. 

Continue until no further division can be performed. When the last quotient cannot be factored, but is itself a prime number, it and the other prime numbers which were used as divisors, are the prime factors of the number. In this case the prime factors of 2730 are 2, 3, 5, 7, and 13. 7)91 13 

Proof: 

2X3X5X7X13=2730

Greatest Common Divisor 

A common divisor of two or more numbers is a number which will divide each of them an integral number of times. Thus, 6 is a common divisor of 18 and 24.

To find the greatest common divisor of two or more numbers, first separate each into its prime factors. The product of all common prime factors is the greatest common factor or greatest common divisor.

GETTING READY FOR FRACTIONS 

EXAMPLE. Find the greatest common factor of 192, 144, and 168.

Reasoning Method 

2)192 2)144 2)168 2)96 2)72 2)84 2)48 2)36 2)42 2)24 2)18 3)21 2)12 3)9 2)6 3 3 By inspection of the above it is apparent that 2, 2, 2, and 3 are common to all three of the above numbers. The greatest common divisor of the three numbers, therefore, is 2X2X2X3=24. 

Formula Method 

2)192-144-168 

2)4836- 42 Arrange the numbers which are to be factored, as shown, and divide by some number which will divide each of them. Continue doing this until no number can be found which will divide each of the remainders. 

Multiply together the numbers which have been used as divisors. The product (in this case 2×2×2×3) is 

867 the greatest common divisor.

Least Common Multiple

A multiple of a number is any number that is determined by taking the given number an integral number of times. The multiple will therefore contain the given number without a remainder. 

Thus, 15, 25, 50, 75, etc., are multiples of 5, and each will contain 5 without a remainder.

LEAST COMMON MULTIPLE 

57 A common multiple of two or more numbers is a number that is a multiple of each.

Thus, 72 is a common multiple of 2, 3, 6, 9, etc., and contains each without a remainder.

The least common multiple of two or more numbers is the smallest number that is a multiple of each. 

Thus, 18 is the least common multiple of 2, 3, 6, and 9. 

EXAMPLE. Find the least common multiple of 4, 8, 6, and 15.

Reasoning Method 

The prime factors of 4 are 2 and 2. 

The prime factors of 8 are 2, 2, and 2. 

The prime factors of 6 are 2 and 3. 

The prime factors of 15 are 3 and 5.

The least common multiple must contain every factor that is contained in each of the four numbers. Inspection of the above shows that these are a 2, a second 2, a third 2, a 3, and a 5. The least common multiple is therefore 2×2×2×3X5=120. 

Formula Method 

2)4-8-6-15 2)2-4-3-15 3)1-2-3-15 1-2-1-5 Write the four numbers as shown and divide by any prime number that is seen to be a factor of any two of the numbers. Dividing by 2, we get 2, 4, and 

3 for quotients in the first three instances. In the fourth instance, we find that 2 is not an exact divisor, so we bring down the 15. Continue this process until all the numbers on the bottom line are prime to each other. Then multiply together all divisors and the quotients remaining on the bottom line, thus: 

2×2×3×2×5=120, least common multiple. It is not necessary to use 1 as a factor, so that has been omitted. It will be seen that the factors of the least common multiple are the same as by the reasoning method first shown.

GETTING READY FOR FRACTIONS Cancellation 

Cancellation is the omission of the same factor from both dividend and divisor. Sometimes cancellation may be used to shorten the work of division.

EXAMPLE. Divide 15X20X32 by 12X24X6. 

First arrange the numbers so that the factors of the dividend will be above a horizontal line and the factors of the divisor will be below the line. 

This is a fraction. The problem is to eliminate equal factors from dividend and divisor, which, as previously noted, will change the fraction to lower terms without changing its value. 15X20X32 12X24X 6 (a) (a) It is evident by inspection that the 15 above the line and the 12 below are each divisible by 3. 

Cancel the 15 and the 12, writing in place of them the quotients secured by dividing by 3. 2 5 5 15X20X32 5 5 4 15X20X32 (b) (c) 

12×24× 6 

12×24× 6 (d) 5 5 4 15X20X32 

12X24× 6 1 1 43 1 (b) Now note that the 20 above the line and the 4 below the line are each divisible by 4. Cancel and divide. (c) Next note that 32 and 24 are divisible by 8, and cancel. (d) Next note that 4 and 6 are divisible by 2, and cancel. 

Since no further cancellation can be performed, it is evident that the fraction is in its lowest terms. Multiply the factors of the dividend together, and multiply the factors of the divisor together, thus: 5X5X2 -50=58 1X3X3

CHAPTER VI Practice Involving Common and Decimal Fractions 

A fraction is one or more of the equal parts of a unit. 

A fractional unit is one of the equal parts of a unit, as one third, one fourth. 

Assuming $1 as the unit, 50¢, 25¢, 10¢, 20¢, 80¢, 1¢ are what parts of it?

The term unit, as applied in commercial calculations, may mean a group of similar things such as 1 dozen, 1 gross, 1 hundred, 1 thousand, or 1 ton. Prices are often based upon such units. When such units are used in figuring costs, they are called cost units.

Assuming 1 dozen as the unit, what fractional parts of 1 dozen would the following numbers be: 1, 3, 4, 6, 8, 9%

Assuming 1 hundred as the unit, what fractional parts of 100 would the following numbers be: 1, 10, 20, 25, 50, 75%

Fractions may be written in either of two forms, the common fraction form or the decimal fraction form. 

The common fraction form is that in which the numerator is written above, and the denominator below, a horizontal line, as in. The numerator and denominator are the terms of the fraction. The decimal fraction form is that in which the digits of the numerator are written at the right of a point, called the decimal point, and the denominator is not written, but is considered to be 1 with as many ciphers annexed as there are places to the right of the decimal point. Remember this in reading decimals.

Thus in the fraction .7, the numerator is 7 and the denominator is 10 as indicated by the fact that there is only one decimal place. The indicated denominator of any decimal is either 10 or some power of 10. 

Common fractions and decimal fractions are merely different forms for expressing fractional values. 59

A common fraction is an indicated division. The fraction indicates the division of 3 by 4. When the division is performed, we have .75, the decimal form of the fraction. It is read, 75 hundredths. The numerator is 75, and the denominator is 1 with as many ciphers annexed as there are decimal places to be read.

61 A proper fraction is a fraction the value of which is less than 1, i.e., the numerator of which is smaller than the denominator, as . 

An improper fraction is one having a numerator greater than its denominator, and whose value is greater than 1, as . 

A mixed number is an integer and a fraction expressed as one number, as 11.

An improper fraction can always be expressed as an integer or as a mixed number.

Interchangeability of Common Form and Decimal Form Fractions are expressed in two forms, the common form and the decimal form.

In the common form any number may be used as a denominator. 

In the decimal form, the denominator is always 10 or some power of 10. 

Any common fraction has its equivalent decimal form, and any decimal fraction has its equivalent common fraction form. 13 Thus, may be written .5, or .5 may be written.

CHANGING THE FORM 

To change a fraction from a common form to a decimal form, divide the numerator by the denominator. 

EXAMPLE. Change to its decimal form. 

.875 8)7.000 63 Divide the numerator, 7, by the denominator, 8, and continue to so divide until there is no remainder, annexing as many ciphers at the right of the decimal point in the numerator as required. Point off as many places in the quotient as there are decimal places in the dividend. 

If the division should continue indefinitely, carry the decimal as far as desired and either omit the remainder, write it in the common form, or if it is more than half of the last decimal unit, increase the last decimal unit by 1.

EXAMPLE. Change to a decimal form. 

This division would continue indefinitely; therefore the .6666 decimal is carried to four places and the remainder is either 3)2.0000 written in the common form as shown, or omitted (giving.6666 as the result) or counted as 1 whole number in the last decimal order, which would give .6667 as the result. Such fractions are called interminate fractions.

Least Common Denominator 

Fractions having the same denominator (which is called a common denominator) can be added without change of their terms. If fractions which are to be added have not a common denominator, they must be expressed in terms of some common denominator before they can be added.

Thus, and cannot be added until they are expressed as and, respectively, or expressed with some other common denominator. 

The least common denominator of several fractions is the least common multiple of their denominators. 

Fractions having a common denominator are called similar fractions.

EXAMPLE. Change,,, and to similar fractions having the least common denominator. 

The l.c.m. of 3, 5, 6, and 2 is 30. 38; }=3%; &=3%; 1=18.

Addition of Fractions 

Since the denominator indicates the size of the fractional unit, and the numerator indicates the number of such units, it is clear that if two fractions have the same denominator they are added by adding the numerators.

To add common fractions express each in terms of the least common denominator of the fractions. Add the numerators, and write the total over the common denominator.

EXAMPLE. Add 1, 2 and 1.

To Add Fractional Units. 

When the fractions added are fractional units, it is most convenient to use the product of their denominators for their common denominator.

When two fractions are thus changed to similar fractions, the new numerator of each fraction is always the same figure as the old denominator of the other fraction. Thus, and =}. 

Hence the rule: To add two fractional units, multiply together the old denominators to obtain the new denominator, and add the old denominators to obtain the new numerator.

To Add Decimals 

EXAMPLE. Add .05, .8, .015, .3675, .004. .05 .8 .015 .3675 .004 1.2365 

WRITTEN 

In the addition of decimals, the decimal points should be kept under each other, and each decimal order under the corresponding order above. That is, tenths under tenths, hundredths under hundredths, etc.

By arranging as suggested above, the sum will be given in its common denominator automatically. Why is this so? 

1. Add 123.786, 24.5203, 1035.001, 5.20079, 1436.5, 1002.2001, 34.6901, and 35.0078. 

2. Add 153.472, 15.3472, 1534.72, 1500.3472, 1500.3472, 200.002, 521.052, and 634.0207. 

3. Add two hundred four and two hundred four thousandths, two hundred and four thousandths, two hundred four thousandths, six hundred and fifty four ten-thousandths, one hundred fifty and one hundred forty-one thousandths, three hundred fifty three and four hundred sixty three ten-thousandths. 

4. Add three thousand one hundred forty-three and two hundred sixty-seven thousandths, four hundred sixty-five hundred-thousandths, five thousand one and one thousand five ten thousandths, four hundred sixty five thousand and eighty-nine thousandths, one hundred one and one hundredth, three thousand four hundred and sixty five ten-thousandths.

69 1. 2. 3. 4. 5. 6. 538 364 237 4734 2461 4891 624 7152 469 608 37156 605 2718 5891% 521층 ​391 4101 9517 309 202 183 295 706 878 When there are several addends, some being expressed in the common fraction form and some in decimal form, it is necessary to express all in the same form before adding. 

EXAMPLE. Add 163, .123, 9, 6.24. 16.666 .123 9.6 6.24 32.630

In this case it is more convenient to change the common fractions to decimals. Change each common fraction to its decimal form, carrying any common fractions that will not reduce to pure decimals to as many places as the longest decimal has, with the remainder annexed as a common fraction. Thus the following 16 is changed to .6663, because the next number, having three decimal places, is the longest decimal. Write the numbers so the decimal points will be under each other and add. The result is 32.630.

Write tenths below tenths, hundredths below hundredths, etc. If there are fewer places in minuend than in the subtrahend add a cipher, or ciphers. Subtract as in ordinary subtraction. Place a decimal point in the answer just beneath the decimal points in the minuend and subtrahend.

When common fractions and decimal fractions are mixed. 

EXAMPLE. From .775 take 3. 

.775 .6 .175 

First, change to a decimal (=.6). Then write .6 under .775, with the decimal point directly under the one above and each order under its corresponding order. Subtract as in integers. The result is .175.

Sometimes it is shorter to change the decimal fraction to a common fraction, and sometimes it is shorter to change the common fraction to a decimal fraction. Each problem should be examined to determine which is the better plan of procedure.

MULTIPLICATION OF FRACTIONS Multiplication of Fractions 

75 Since the numerator of a fraction indicates the number of fractional units in it, a fraction is multiplied by multiplying its numerator without changing its denominator. 

Thus is doubled by multiplying its numerator by 2. x2= or 

Since the denominator of a fraction indicates the size of the fractional units of which the fraction consists, a fraction is multiplied by dividing its denominator without changing its numerator. 

Thus is multiplied by 2 by dividing its denominator by 2. X2= 

To multiply an integer by a common fraction. 

EXAMPLE. Multiply 16 by . 

16X= 16X4 ==12 Multiply the integer by the numerator of the fraction, and divide by the denominator. It makes no difference whether the fraction be the multiplier or the multiplicand, as these may be interchanged without affecting the result. 

Apply the principle of cancellation to problems in multiplication of fractions when possible.

To multiply mixed numbers.

Example. Multiply 26 by 183. 

2631 18 82/ 13 208 26 49012 

First multiply the fraction by the fraction: 

Write as a partial product. Next multiply the whole number in the multiplicand by the fraction in the multiplier. ×26=83. Write 83 as a partial product. Next multiply the fraction in the multiplicand by the whole number in the multiplier, and write the result, 13, as a partial product. Next multiply the whole numbers and write the partial products as shown. Add all partial products. The result is 4901.

If either the multiplicand or the multiplier is an integer, without a fractional part, two of the above steps will be eliminated. 

Similarly, if either is a fraction without an integral part, two of the. steps will be eliminated.

In case of mixed numbers when the integers are small, it is sometimes easier to express both multiplier and multiplicand as improper fractions and multiply. 

Decimal fractions and common fractions are interchangeable in form. The form of either may be changed to the other if the change will make solution easier.

Written Review Problems 

1. Two men do a certain piece of work at a contract price of $178.50. One works 36 days and the other works 15 days. If their work is paid for at the same rate, how much shall each receive? 

2. A tomato can is 33 in. in diameter and 4 in. high. What must be the inside width, length, and height of a box to contain 4 doz. cans if they are put in 4 rows of 6 cans each, on the bottom, and the box contains 2 tiers? 

3. A tailor bought a piece of goods containing 411 yd. If 

3 yards are required to make a suit, how many yards will remain after 11 suits have been made from the goods? 

4. In a certain factory the employees work 74 hours a day for five days in the week and work 3 hours on Saturday. If the rate of pay of one of the employees is 45¢ an hour, how much should he receive for one full week? How much for a week in which he is absent Friday and Saturday? 

5. In the State of Ohio the state levy for highway improvement for 1914 was .3 mill. If a farmer owned 193.8 acres of land assessed at $42.50 per acre how much tax would he pay for this purpose? 

6. A woman bought a turkey weighing 11½ lb. @ 26½¢ per lb. 

How much change should she receive out of a $5.00 bill offered in payment?

7. The following bill was purchased at a grocery store: 

31 lb. steak at 26; 6 lb. ham at 224; 12 lb. lard at 17; 50 lb. sugar at 

7½¢; and 1 doz. cans corn at 2 cans for 25¢. How much change should be returned out of a $10 bill? 

8. Each section of a radiator is 2 in. thick. What will be the length of a radiator having 17 such sections? Suppose each section of this radiator contains 34 sq. ft. of radiation; how many square feet of radiation does the radiator contain? 

9. A boiler contains 92.8 sq. ft. of grate surface and burns 9 lb. of coal per hour per sq. ft. of grate surface. How much will it burn in 24 hours? 

10. How much coke will three fires burn in 27 days, allowing 

7½ hr. per day and 8 lb. of coke per hour for each fire? 

11. A steam piston has an area of 78.15 sq. in. If the cylinder is filled with steam at a pressure of 150 lb. per sq. in., what is the total force exerted on the piston? 

12. Navy brass is composed of .62 copper, .01 tin and .37 zinc.

The weight of a certain brass casting is 2785 lb. How much of each kind of metal was used to produce the casting? 

13. Henry Lane is bankrupt and a statement of his affairs shows the following condition: Cash...

ASSETS LIABILITIES 

$ 139.50 Notes and Accounts Payable. $20,000

Accounts and Notes Receivable 1028.00 

Real Estate..... .....6600.00

DIVISION OF FRACTIONS Division of Fractions 

Dividing a fraction by an integer.

81 Since the numerator of a fraction indicates the number of fractional units of which it consists, it is clear that a fraction is divided if its numerator is divided without changing its denominator. 

Thus is divided by 2 by dividing its numerator by 2. +2= 6÷2 Since the denominator of a fraction indicates the size of the fractional units, it is clear that a fraction is divided if its denominator is multiplied without changing the numerator. 

Thus & is divided by 2 by multiplying its denominator by 2. ÷2 8X2 

Hence, to divide a fraction by an integer, divide its numerator or multiply its denominator by that integer.

ORAL. In solving these problems, divide the numerator if it contains the divisor an integral number of times; if not, multiply the denominator. 1. 욕​÷2 6. 8÷2 11. 18÷3 16. ÷3 

7. ÷3 12.÷5 17. 27÷9 3. 13÷3 8. 공​÷7 13.÷3 18. 3÷3 4. ÷3 9.÷4 14. 2÷16 19. 45÷3 

10. 18÷4 15. 8÷2 20. 8÷7 

EXAMPLE. 

4).3728 .0932 

Divide .3728 by 4. 

The numerator is .3728, and the denominator 10,000.

Divide the numerator by 4. Point off in the result the same number of places as in the dividend, thus observing the principle that in the division of fractions by dividing the numerator the denominator remains unchanged.

Division of Decimals 

(a) Proceed to divide as in whole numbers, annexing decimal ciphers to the dividend if needed to complete the division. (b) In placing the decimal point in the quotient, note: .09).54 9).54 .06 .9)1.89 (a) If the dividend and the divisor have the same number of decimal places and the division is exact, the quotient is a whole number. (b) If the divisor is a whole number, the quotient has the same number of decimal places as the dividend. (c) If the dividend and the divisor do not have the same number of decimal places, subtract the number of decimal 2.1 places in the divisor from the number of decimal places .09)18.9 in the dividend to find the number of decimal places in = the quotient. If you need to annex decimal ciphers to the dividend in order to increase the number of decimal places and determine the location of the decimal point, you may do so. Annexing decimal ciphers does not change the value.

Number your answers on a separate paper. Do not copy the questions. 

1. If you know the sum of two fractions and know what one of them is, how can you find the other fraction? 

2. If you know what part of a farm is planted in corn and how many acres are in corn, how can you find the number of acres in the farm? 

3. If you know how many days it takes Henry to do a certain piece of work and how many days it takes John to do the same work, how can you find the amount they will both do in one day? 

4. If you know how much each of three persons invests and know the profits of their business, how can you determine the amount of the profits each should have? 

5. If the denominators of two fractions are the same but the numerators are different, which is the larger fraction?

8. If the numerators of two fractions are the same but the denominators are not the same, which is the larger fraction?

11. If you know the number of students in school today and what part of those enrolled are absent, how can you find the number of students enrolled? 

12. If you know the number of days it takes for two men to plow a certain field and the number of days it would take for one of them to plow it, how can you find the number of days in which the other man ought to plow it?

Unit Three

SOME SPECIAL WAYS OF USING NUMBERS 

Arithmetic presents many opportunities for doing number work by using short cuts. A few of these short cuts and their practical uses in problems in industry are shown in Unit Three. 

This unit also shows how to represent the relation of large numbers to one another by means of graphs that speak directly to the mind through the eye. In this way you can compare numbers that are so large you cannot comprehend them. 

To test your ability to do these things, see how much of the work in the Preview Tests you can do without error. This will show you on what parts of Unit Three you need most practice and will enable you to economize time and work. The short cuts in arithmetic are intended to be used as time and work savers. 333 93

CHAPTER VII ALIQUOT PARTS 

An aliquot part of a number is a number which will divide it without a remainder. Thus, 2 is an aliquot part of 4; 2 is an aliquot part of 10; 33 is an aliquot part of 100; 1663 is an aliquot part of 1000.

Aliquot parts of 100 are more commonly used than any other. 

Aliquot parts of $1.00 form the basis of an important short-cut in multiplication that is much used by billing clerks. The following illustrative example will show its application. 

Although any number which will divide another number an integral number of times is an aliquot part of it, the use of aliquot parts in arithmetic is usually confined to those mixed numbers which are themselves difficult to handle, but which when regarded as aliquot parts of 10, 100, or 1000 are easy to handle such as 12, 163. 

EXAMPLE. What is the cost of 184 gallons of oil at 12 per gal.? 

12 of $1.00. 

184 gal. at $1 cost $184. $184÷8 $23. Ans. 

Since 12 is of $1.00, 184 gallons at 12 cost of what they would cost at $1.00, or of $184, which is $23.

Find the cost at $1.00, and divide by the number of times that $1.00 contains the price per article. 

Aliquot Parts of 100

Memorize the following important aliquot parts of 100: 14 10 12 12 16 50 33512 25 20 1623 121/ 10 Construct tables similar to the above, showing the important aliquot parts of 10 and 1000. 97

ALIQUOT PARTS 

Halves, Fourths, Eighths, and Sixteenths 

50¢ is what part of $1.00? When articles are purchased at 50¢ each, how does the number of dollars of cost compare with the number of articles?

Answer the same questions, changing the amount under consideration to 25¢; 121; 61.

ORAL. Find the cost of each of the following: 

1. 20 lb. @ 50¢.

3. 37 lb. @ 50¢. 

4. 49 lb. @ 25¢. 

5. 27 lb. @ 50¢.

Interchangeability of Price and Quantity Price and quantity may be interchanged without affecting the result. Thus if a problem be stated "Find the cost of 17 yd. at 

50" it may be solved as though it read, "Find the cost of 50 yd. at 17¢,” for the answer would be the same in either case; viz. $8.50.

This agrees with the general principle of multiplication that changing the order of the factors does not affect the result.

Compounding Aliquot Parts 

Many calculations are shortened by compounding aliquot parts, that is, adding two or more simple aliquot parts instead of using the aliquot parts multiplication table. Multiply 438 by 372. (a) of 43800=10950 of 43800= 5475 (b) of 43800=10950 of 10950= 5475 of 43800=16425 16425 of 100 plus of 100

Instead of treating 37 as of 100, it is treated as (Method a) or as of 100 plus of of 100 (Method b).

Sometimes it will be easier to subtract than to add. 

EXAMPLE. Multiply 438 by 871. 

43800 5475 

In this case 87 or 50+25+12. is treated as 100-12 instead of of 100 

From 100×438 subtract of itself. 38325

COMPOUNDING ALIQUOT PARTS

ALIQUOT PARTS To Divide by an Aliquot Part EXAMPLE. Divide 81625 by 12. 

81625÷100=816.25 8X816.25 6530 12 is of 100. The result of dividing by 

12 is therefore 8 times as great as the result of dividing by 100.

To divide by a number which is an aliquot part of 100, point off two places and multiply by the number which represents the number of times the divisor is contained in 100.. 

The rule for dividing by an aliquot part of 10, or of 1000, or any other number which consists of 1 followed by ciphers, is similar to the above, but the number of places pointed off differs.

Applications of Aliquot Parts to Commercial Transactions 

Commodities sold by the ton (2000 lb.) or by the thousand. 

EXAMPLE. Find the cost of 1750 ft. of hemlock sheathing at $21.50 per M.

ORDINARY METHOD SHORT METHOD 

$21.50 1.750 107500 15050 2150 $21.50 (cost 1 M) 

5.375 (cost M) 

16.125 (cost M) $37.625 (cost 12 M) $37.62500 

EXPLANATION OF THE SHORT METHOD. The advantage of the short method is that much can be done mentally without writing. It is at once apparent that 1750-12 M. The cost of M ($5.375) is written just below $21.50 and subtracted, giving $16.125 as the cost of M. To this $21.50 is added without rewriting it, giving a total of $37.63 for the cost of 12 M. Note that the number of figures written by the short method is only 18, as against 30 by the ordinary method.

A still shorter solution, but one which would not apply to every problem so easily as it does to this one, is as follows: $43 5.37 $37.63

It is at once apparent that 1750 ft. =2M ft. less of itself. 

Multiply $21.50 by 2 mentally and write the result, $43.

Then deduct from $43 one-eighth of itself. 

NOTE. It is impossible to give arbitrary rules to cover all the various possible applications of aliquot parts in time-saving methods. The alertminded student will be able to make use of many devices, of which the illustrations here shown are only suggestive. Two students, equally alert mentally, might solve such a problem as the above in different ways, both good.

ALIQUOT PARTS Miscellaneous Aliquot Parts 

Aliquot parts may be based upon other numbers than 10, 100, or 1000. For instance, when the price unit is a dozen or a gross, short methods of multiplication may be used which involve aliquot parts of 12 or 144. Similarly when the unit of measurement of distance is 320 rods (1 mile), or the unit of measurement of time is 60 days, short methods of multiplication may be used involving the use of aliquot parts of 320 and of 60. 

There are many cases in which aliquot parts can be used besides those in which the unit of cost or of measurement is 10, 100, 1000, 12, 144, 320, or 60. 

These seven cases, however, sufficiently indicate the general principle. 

ORAL 

1. What part of 12 is 6? 9? 3? 1? 7? 10? 2? 4? 5? 8? 11? 

2. What part of 1 gross is 1 doz.? 3 doz.? 6 doz.? 9 doz.? 2 doz.? 

10 doz.? 8 doz.? 4 doz.? 5 doz.? 7 doz.? 11 doz.? 

3. What part of 144 is 72? 36? 108? 12? 84? 120? 24? 48? 60? 

96? 132? 

4. What part of 320 rods is 160 rods? 40 rods? 80 rods? 200 rods? 

120 rods? 240 rods? 

5. What part of 60 days is 30 days? 15 days? 10 days? 6 days? 

12 days? 45 days? 50 days? 

6. What part of 6 days is 3 days? 2 days? 1 day? 4 days? 

7. What two aliquot parts of 60 days, added, make 45 days? 

40 days? 21 days? 36 days? 

8. What aliquot part of 60 days, plus what aliquot part of itself, makes 33 days? 22 days? 45 days? 24 days? 

9. How many times 60 days is 180 days? 240 days? 90 days? 

210 days? 75 days? 

10. What aliquot part of 60 must be deducted from 60 to leave 

50? 45? 54? 48? 40?

4.40 4.40 $13.20 $13.20

The cost for 60 days=$4.60. 1.15 $8.05 

The cost for 30 days of $4.60=$2.30 

The cost for 15 days of $2.30 (or ≥ of $4.60)=$1.15. $4.60+$2.30+$1.15=$8.05.

CHAPTER VIII  

Pay Rolls

In figuring the wages of workmen paid by the hour it is useful to be familiar not only with the aliquot parts of $1.00 but with the aliquot parts of the day's pay. 

Suppose that a workman is paid on the basis of $5 a day, for a day of 6 hours. This is at the rate of 833¢ an hour. The pay roll clerk is probably familiar with the table of 833¢ up to $5. 

If the workman's time for the week is 36 hours, and his rate of pay 8314, the pay roll clerk may figure it as 6 days at $5 a day. 

EXAMPLE. Find the total hours and the total amount due each workman listed on the following time sheet:

Problems of the Farm 115 1. The freight rate on corn is 62.5¢ a hundred from Chicago to New York. The freight rate on dressed hogs is $1.42 a hundred. 

It requires 30 bushels of corn of 56 lb. each to grow a 300-lb. hog, dressing 225 lb. How much cheaper is it to ship the dressed hog than the corn? 

2. When the average price of corn was 87¢ in Nebraska and $1.23 in Massachusetts, how much more was the cost for feed to raise a 300-lb. hog in Massachusetts than in Nebraska? 

3. A farmer applies $2.40 worth of fertilizer to a field and the crop increases in value $16.39. On another plot of the same field he uses $20.90 worth of another fertilizer, and this crop increases in value $40.54. Considering only the profits on this year's crops, which is the more profitable fertilizer to use and how much? 

4. A farmer bought a car load of twenty steers at $5.75 a hundred, plus a freight charge of 7¢ a hundred, and eighteen months later sold them at $7.65 a hundred. The steers averaged 898 lb. when bought, and the average weight when sold was 1454 lb. If the cost of keeping the steers was $900, what was the gain? 

5. A farmer bought a herd of 149 steers averaging 982 lb. for which be paid at the rate of 5¢ a pound. The average feed for each steer was: 21.6 bu. of corn @ 50¢; 13 tons of silage at $3.75; 400 lb. mixed hay at $10.00 a ton; 320 lb. cottonseed meal at $30.00 a ton. 

The herd was then sold at $8.25 per hundred and they averaged 

1254 lb. What was the profit per steer, and the gross profit? 

6. A boy planted an acre of corn. The seed cost him 75; plowing, barrowing, discing, etc., cost $4.50; cultivation cost $3.50, husking he considered worth $2.50. His yield was 80 bu. worth 

85¢ a bu. What was his profit? What was the cost per bushel? 

7. The University of Minnesota has determined that the cost of raising and harvesting an acre of corn in a certain county was $21.77, and of an acre of barley $19.13. The corn yielded 42 bu. an acre and was worth 85¢ per bu. The barley yielded 37 bu. per acre, and was worth 92¢ per bu. What was the net profit per bushel from each crop? Which was the more profitable crop and how much per acre?

Farm Problems 8. A crop of 15 bu. of wheat per acre removes from the soil 

21lb. of nitrogen, 71 lb. of phosphorus, and 4-1 lb. of potassium. Ordinary barnyard manure contains 10 lb. of nitrogen, 6 lb. of phosphorus, and 12 lb. of potassium per ton. How much manure must be applied to replace the nitrogen used? How much to replace the phosphorus ? 

9. Find the net loss or gain on hogs for 19-. Value of hogs on hand Jan. 1, $345.60. Apr. 14, bought a pure bred hog for $38.50. May 6, paid for services of veterinary, $2.00. June 4, sold 24 pigs at $3.20 each. Sept. 8, bought 1 tons bran at $22. 

Sept. 20, sold 8 shoats averaging 156 lb. at $6.90 a hundred pounds. Nov. 10, sold 20 hogs averaging 278 lb., at $7.20 a hundred pounds. Dec. 8, sold one hog for $32. Inventory Dec. 

31, 32 hogs averaging 306 lb. at 9¢. Man labor charged, 630 hr. at 164; horse labor, 46 hr. at 11¢. Cost of feed, $803.50. 

10. A farmer pays 55¢ a rod for digging trenches, and $23.00 a thousand for tile. What will be the cost of tiling 84 rd., if the survey costs $4.30 and 16 tile are required for each rod? How much per rod will the tiling cost? 

11. It is estimated that a bushel of corn as feed makes an increase of 10 lb. weight per hog. If corn is worth 95¢ per bu. and hogs 141¢ per lb., is it better to sell the corn or feed it, and how much, in a field of corn of 50 A. averaging 64 bu. per A.? 

12. If the annual cost of maintenance of a dairy cow is $60.00, and she gives 

14,275 lb. of milk testing .04 lb. butter fat per lb. what is the cost per pound of butter fat, making no allowance for the value of the skim milk? What is the total amount received if the milk is sold on the basis of 42.3¢ per pound of butter fat? What should be received if the butter fat is made into butter (making more in weight) and sold at 40.7¢ per lb. ?

PROBLEMS IN BUSINESS 

117 14. A field of barley is 60 rods wide and 80 rods long. It yielded 1230 bu. What was the yield per acre? 

15. What will it cost to enclose a field 80 rd. square with a woven wire fence with a single strand of barbed wire on the top, posts to be 1 rd. apart? The posts cost 23¢ each; the barbed wire costs 3¢ a rod, and the woven wire 411¢ a rod. The staples cost $2.75; the digging of the post holes, $15.00; the setting of the posts, $12.50; and the stretching and fixing the wire, $10.50. 

What does the fence cost per rod completed? 

Problems in Business 

1. A contractor built a house for Jones, who agreed to allow him for his pay of all amounts paid for labor and materials. 

After paying the contractor, Jones found that the house had cost him $6489. How much was the contractor's pay? 

2. A dealer cut the price of a dining table one fourth. If the reduced price was $27.60, what was the price before reduction?

If this table was marked in the first place to produce a profit of half the cost, what profit is realized if it is sold for $27.60? 

3. In North Dakota a farm of 160 A. was purchased for $4500. The entire farm was sown in wheat, and produced 221 bushels per acre. The wheat was sold for 85¢ per bushel. The first year's crop was what fractional part of the cost of the farm? 

4. A cardboard carton is 31" x 61″ x 81", outside dimensions.

These cartons, when filled, are packed 3 doz. in a larger carton. 

What must be the inside measurements of the larger carton to contain three tiers of 12 each, of the smaller cartons packed flat? (Two answers.)

5. What fractional part of a cubic foot does each of the smaller cartons mentioned in problem 4 occupy? How many cubic feet do 36 cartons occupy? What outside dimensions can you suggest for cartons to hold 36 of the smaller boxes, allowing in. for the thickness of the material? 

6. A label is used on the end of the small cartons mentioned in problem 4 leaving a margin of inch around the label. What is the size of the label? 

Cost per Unit 

1. Thomas Plant, a farm hand, worked 291.5 hours in one month.

His wages were $60.00. His board was estimated at $20.00. What did he cost his employer per hour? If Plant worked 31.5 hours in plowing one field what did the man labor cost for plowing that field? 

2. In a certain high school a teacher gets $1500 for 10 months of service, each month consisting of 20 school days. He spends 6 hours each day in class work with classes averaging 25 students each. What is the average cost per student per hour for this teacher? 

3. What would be the teaching cost per student in a high school accommodating 1275 students, and maintaining a faculty of 52 teachers receiving the same pay and working the same number of hours as the teacher in Problem 2? 

4. An office building to cost $700,000 is to be erected upon a lot 60' x 100'. The ground was leased for 99 years at an annual rental of $12,000 for the first 10 years, $13,000 per year for the succeeding ten years and $14,000 per year for the remaining 79 years. If the value of the land is 25 times the average value of the lease, what is the cost of this property per front foot and per square foot, including the building? 

5. A tract of land containing 80 acres was purchased for $2,000 per acre and then subdivided into 640 lots each having a frontage of 30 ft., and a depth of 160 ft. What is the cost per lot in this sub-division, and the cost per front foot? How much land was used for streets and alleys? 

6. A freight locomotive consumes 212 lb. of coal per mile and a passenger locomotive consumes 109 lb. per mile. What will it

COST PER UNIT 

119 cost to run each of these locomotives 90 miles if the coal costs the railroad company on an average of $1.39 per ton? (Allow 2000 lb. per ton.) 

7. If a locomotive averages 31,417 miles per year and fuel costs 

10.6¢ per mile, how many tons of coal are used, if the coal costs $1.46 per ton of 2000 pounds? 

8. In a certain city the street railway company carried 240,940,- 

100 revenue passengers, and received $11,952,286 as passenger earnings. What was the average receipt per passenger? If the operating expenses of this railway amounted to $8,694,230, what was the cost per passenger? 

9. A roll of wrapping paper 24" wide and 800 ft. long, costs 5¢ per lb. and weighs 36 lb. It is used to wrap parcels in boxes 42" high and 5½" wide. The full width of the paper is used for each parcel. How many parcels may be wrapped from one roll of paper and what is the average cost per parcel if 11" is allowed for lapping over on the box? (Carry the result to the fourth decimal place.) 

10. A hardware dealer rents a store for $62.50 per month.

During March his sales were $2500, and during April they were $3250. The rent was what decimal part of his sales each month? 

The decimal rate for March was how much greater than the decimal rate for April? 

11. A board 1 in. x 6 in. x 16 ft. contains 8 board feet. Using boards of this size, a man made 3200 wheels each 1 in. thick and 

6 in. in diameter, in 32 hrs. He received 611¢ per hr. for his labor. If the lumber was purchased at $28.50 per thousand board feet, what was the average cost of each wheel? (Give result to fourth decimal place.) 

12. A company manufactured 150,000 of a certain article durin 1933. The cost of these articles was $38,500.00 for materials; $33,850.00 for labor. The company paid out $23,000 for general expenses during the year and estimated that its property had depreciated in value $7500. What was the cost per unit of manufacture for materials? for labor? How much must be charged against each unit to cover its share of expense? of depreciation? 

What was the total cost per unit?

Profit and Loss 1. A man renting a farm on half shares hired an extra man and team for 10 days at $6.24 per day to plow corn. He obtained an increased yield of 4 bu. per acre on 52 acres. If corn is worth 

81¢ per bushel, what was the profit on the hired man's labor? 

2. According to the statistics of a street railway company, it carried 224,033,000 revenue passengers during the year at an average cost of $.0388 per passenger. The average receipts per passenger were $.0441. At this rate what were the profits of the company? What would be the yearly profit on one car that would carry 185 passengers during each trip if it made 4 trips per day every day except Sunday? 

3. A dealer bought 400 sheep for $1175.50. He sold of them at $9.75 per head, and 3 of the remainder at $9.25 per head. 

What then remained he sold at $9 per head. Find the profit realized and the average profit per head realized. 

4. A butcher bought a steer that weighed 1460 lb., at $9.85 per cwt. If this steer dressed 56 lb. to the cwt., how many pounds of dressed meat will the butcher get? What will be the value of the dressed meat if it averages 244¢ per lb.? Disregarding labor and other costs not mentioned, what will be the butcher's profit? 

5. A second hand paper dealer bought 150 tons of old papers at a pound. The expense of getting this stock was $760.50. 

At a further expense of $104.50 he separated it into three lots of 50 tons each, which he sold at $20, $26.50, and $37.00 per ton respectively. What was his profit? 

6. If 40 acres of land yield 30,000 lb. seed cotton, and 1500 lb. of seed cotton make a bale of lint, what is the gross return from the crop if the lint sells for 12¢ a pound and the residue, which is seed, sells for $22.00 a ton? (Assume 1 bale=500 lb.) 

7. I was offered $3.50 per hundred for a load of seed cotton weighing 1580 lb. I refused this offer and had the cotton ginned, paying 40¢ per 100 lb. lint, and $1.20 for bagging and ties. The seed cotton ginned 450 lb. lint. I sold the lint for 91¢ a pound, and the seed at $1.55 per 100 lb. How much more did I receive for my cotton than if I had accepted the offer first stated?

CHAPTER IX GRAPHIC ILLUSTRATIONS 

A graph is a diagram by which some relationship is illustrated, usually by means of straight or curved lines. 

The following graph illustrates the variation between enrollment and attendance at a certain school for each day for one month. 900 800 700 600 500 

First Week Second Week Third Week Fourth Week 

The upper charted line represents the enrollment of the school, and the lower charted line, the attendance. 

What does each space between the light vertical lines represent? 

Between the heavy vertical lines?

What does each space between the light horizontal lines represent? Between the heavy horizontal lines?

What does the space between the two charted lines represent? 

When are the lines farthest apart and what does this indicate? 

Graphs may be used to illustrate temperature, rain fall, earnings and expenses, receipts and expenditures, fluctuations in prices, to compare one year's sales with another, or for a variety of other purposes. 121

UNIT REVIEW Unit Mastery Test VII 

Number your answers to correspond to the numbering in the test. 

Arrange your work clearly so as to show the process you follow in each problem. 

1. A coal dealer bought a car load of coal weighing 81,000 lb. at $5.90 per ton, 2000 lb. He sold the coal at $8 per ton at retail after paying 80¢ per ton for freight charges. If the cost of delivery averages 32¢ per ton, what will be his profit on the car load? 

2. A coal mining company shipped to a dealer 78,400 lb. of coal. The freight was 60¢ per ton and the cost of the coal at the mines was $2.35 per ton. If the cost of delivery was 25¢ per ton, what should be the retail price per ton in order to make the dealer's profit 80¢ per ton? 

3. It costs $22.80 to raise an acre of potatoes, exclusive of rent on the land. If the potatoes sell at 65¢ per bushel, what yield in bushels per acre must a tenant secure in order to make a profit of $6 per acre after paying $5 per acre rental and paying the expense of raising the crop? 

4. A farmer shipped to a dairy 198 cans of milk. He paid freight charges of 163 cents a can. The dairy bought the milk at $1.33 a can. What was the net amount of money the farmer received from this sale? If the dairy had paid the freight charges, how much should the farmer have received per gallon for the milk in order to make the same net amount of money? 

5. On January 1, 19, Jones had an investment of $12,- 

263.54. At the end of the year his assets amounted to $21,346.05, and his liabilities amounted to $12,564.59. What was his net investment at the end of the year? What was the amount of his gain or loss for the year? 

6. Smith's assets on January 1 were $13,456.27. His liabilities were $3429.68. During the year his gains were $10,564.59 and his losses were $5789.67. His liabilities at the end of the year were $4862.36. What were his assets at the end of the year?

UNIT REVIEW Thought Test III 

(Time, 20 minutes)

Number your answers to correspond to the numbering in the test. 

Do not copy the questions. 

1. If you know that the price of gasoline per gallon is a certain aliquot part of one dollar and know the number of gallons you need to buy, how can you find the number of dollars the gasoline will cost? 

2. If you know that the rate of pay per hour of a workman is composed of two of the aliquot parts of a dollar added together (as 75¢ plus 1634) and know the number of hours he has worked during the week, how can you find the amount of pay he should receive? 

3. When Jones came home from the factory Saturday evening, he brought his weekly pay. Knowing the amount of money and the aliquot part of a dollar that represents his pay per hour, how can he find the number of hours work with which he has been credited on the pay roll sheet for the week? 

4. In farms of medium size the fields are sometimes made 40 rods long or 80 rods long for convenience. In a 40-rod field every strip of land 4 rods wide contains an acre. If a rectangular field is 80 rods long and you are told the width of it in rods, how can you find the area in acres without multiplying the dimensions and then dividing their product by 160? 

5. A wholesale house wishes to ship a lot of drugs in uniform small cartons. Knowing the three dimensions of one of the small cartons, you are sent to the shipping room to select the case in which a given number of these cartons can be placed with least waste of space. How can you determine this by arithmetic? 

6. You are given the statistical data showing the altitude of each of the highest ten mountains in the world. How will you make clear to a small child their height and their relative heights in comparison with the highest building with which he is familiar? Describe clearly the device you plan to use.

Unit Four SKILL IN COMPUTING BY HUNDREDTHS 

In going forward in arithmetic, we narrow the field that we work, but we study the smaller parts more intensively. The specialist says that this is “learning more and more about less and less."

First we studied computing in whole numbers. Then we worked in fractions, including decimals-a special group of fractions whose denominators must be 10 or some power of 10.

Now in Unit Four we take up a still smaller group consisting of decimals that are hundredths. Wonders can be done with these little tools, if you know how to use them skillfully.

The Preview Test will help you to measure your expertness in this kind of work. Go through the test carefully to find how much of it is already part of your mastered skill and what parts need further practice and study. 129

WRITTEN TEST Preview Test in Percentage, Commission and Brokerage 

Make your work sheet show the process by which you solve each problem or example. Number your answers to correspond to the numbers in the test. Use aliquot parts method when helpful. 

1. Find 

18 % of 900 

24% of 718

CHAPTER X PERCENTAGE 

Percentage is an application of decimal fractions in which one one-hundredth is the unit used in computations. 

A common fraction may have any denominator; that is, the unit in computations may be any part of a thing, as. A decimal fraction must have for its understood denominator 10 or some power of 10, as in .001. But in percentage the unit is always equivalent to 100. Thus 1 per cent = 100• 

Per cent means for each hundred. One one-hundredth, in percentage, is called one per cent. The sign for per cent is "%. The per cent taken is the rate.

Six per cent (6%), which is equivalent to 18, or .06, means six hundredths, or six for each hundred, or six to each hundred.

Common fractions, decimal fractions, and quantities expressed as per cents are only different forms of expression. Any per cent can be expressed in either of the other forms. 

EQUIVALENT EXPRESSIONS 

Per Cent Decimal Fraction

Common Fraction 6% .06 or 100 50 51% 11 .05 or .055 or 100 200 

1% or .75% .002 or .0075 or 100 400 Any one of the above forms expresses a value which may be expressed in either of the other forms.

WRITTEN. Prepare a table with six columns headed respectively: Common Fraction, Decimal Fraction, Per Cent, Cents on 131

PERCENTAGE 

the Dollar, and Dollars. Fill in with the following, each on a separate line and each under the proper heading:, .6, 75%,, 621¢, $.50, 35¢, 80¢, 37%, 5%, 10%, .45, .70, .06, .08, 200, 100' 

180. Then fill in all blank spaces on each line with equivalents of the value expressed in one space on that line. 

To find a given per cent of a number multiply the number by the rate. You will point off two decimal places in the answer, of course, as the rate is always expressed in hundredths. 

EXAMPLE. Find 16% of 250. 

(a) (b) 250 250 .16 \ 1500 250 40.00 16% 1500 250 40.00 

EXAMPLE. Find 25% of 468. 

4)468 117 In this case 25% is recognized at once as 1, and the problem is solved by the aliquot parts method. 

In solving the following problems, use the aliquot parts method when desirable. Interchange the factors whenever this will make the work easier.

1. The cost of manufacturing a barrel of flour is $2.5365. The expenses of selling are $37,250 for 50,000 barrels. If selling expense is distributed in proportion to manufacturing costs, what must be the selling price per barrel to cover selling expense and to yield a profit of 10% of the selling price?

What amount of each will be necessary to make two tons of the composition metal?

000. The operating expenses were 39.5% of the gross revenue, and

6. A corporation has gross annual returns from a leasehold amounting to $136,500. Eight per cent of this sum is reserved each year to discharge a debt of $131,040. How many years will be required for the amount reserved annually to discharge this debt?

To find what per cent one number is of another 135 The relation of one number to another is most readily expressed as a common fraction. Thus the relation of 4 to 6 is most readily expressed as . This common fraction can then be changed to a decimal by dividing the numerator by the denominator. If it is desired to express this value in terms of percentage, the decimal should be carried to two places only (the remainder, if any, being written as a common fraction) and the per cent sign used instead of the decimal point. 

EXAMPLE. 5 is what per cent of 20? 

The relation of 5 to 20 is denominator gives the decimal .25.

AVERAGE 

To find a number, or base, when any per cent of it is known As a percentage is the result of multiplying some number by a rate expressed in hundredths, we have this equation: 

Rate (in hundredths) times Base Percentage As percentage, then, is a product, we can divide it by either of the two factors to find the other factor. Therefore, to find a number when any per cent of it is known write the per cent as a decimal and divide the given number or part by it. 

EXAMPLE. 168 is 8% of what number? .08) 168.00 2100 

Reasoning: If 168 is 8% of the number divide by 8 to find 1% or divide by .08 to find 100%.

Often it is convenient to use the aliquot parts method. 

EXAMPLE. 120 is 121% of what number? 

121% of anything. Therefore, 120 is of the required number, or base. 8x120 960 120 is 12% of 960

If the given number is more than 100% of the number required to be found, the process is the same. 

EXAMPLE. 80 is 125% of what number?

Robert sold a colt for $80, which was 25% more than it cost him. How much did it cost him? 

1.25) 80.00 64. 

The cost of the colt 100% 

The selling price ($80)=100% +25% (125%) of the cost. Write the per cent as hundredths and divide the selling price by it. The colt cost Robert $64.

CHAPTER XI COMMISSION AND BROKERAGE 

Commission is the performance of a service by one party, called an agent, or commission broker, for a principal, which service consists of effecting a sale or a purchase of merchandise or other property.

The compensation paid for this service is also called the commission. 

The commission or brokerage is generally a certain per cent of the amount of the sale or purchase, but it may be a certain amount for each unit of weight or quantity; as 2¢ per bu., or ¢ per lb.

A commission merchant, or broker, is a merchant who buys or sells property as an agent.

A commission merchant usually has possession of the property he sells. 

ST. LOUIS, MO., Sept. 5, 19

Account Sales of 150 bbl. BEN DAVIS APPLES For Account of A. R. DOWNER, OLD MISSION, MICH. 

BY M. A. FROST & CO.

COMMISSION AND BROKERAGE 

A broker usually does not have possession of the property he sells, but merely acts as an agent in bringing together the buyer and the seller. 

A guaranty is a charge made by the agent to cover his personal risk in guaranteeing the goods to be of a certain quality. 

This charge is generally estimated as a certain per cent of the amount of the sale or purchase.

Collection. The broker sometimes makes a separate charge for collecting the money for the sale. This charge for collection may be a per cent of the amount of the sale, or may be a flat sum. 

An account sales is a formal statement prepared by the agent, to show the quantity and prices of sales, the expenses that are properly charged to the principal, and the net proceeds of the sales. 

The net proceeds is the amount due the principal after all expenses have been taken from the amount of the sales or the amount of the collection.

An account purchase is a formal statement prepared by the agent to show the amounts of purchases for the principal, other expenditures made for the principal, and the agent's charges. 

The commission is based in each case upon the amount or quantity of the purchase.

GULFPORT, MISS., Nov. 15, 19 Purchase for Account of JEVNE & CO., CHICAGO BY COX AND SONS

Unit Five USING PERCENTAGE IN BUSINESS TRANSACTIONS 

In Unit Five we study some of the ways in which percentage is used in the business world. The mastery of this unit will help to make you certain that you are right when you compute interest on a note, find for how much a note may be discounted at the bank, make an invoice in which the seller of merchandise offers a succession of discounts, or perform any of the common operations in using percentage. No part of arithmetic is more useful to you than this work in Unit Five.

The Preview Test which follows will enable you to know exactly what operations in Percentage, as used in business, are already as familiar to you as the multiplication table, and in what parts of this group of exercises and problems you still need to study and practice for mastery. 151

CHAPTER XII INTEREST Simple KINDS OF INTEREST Compound FACTORS OF EVERY PROBLEM
