Interest. As commonly defined, interest is a compensation paid for the use of money.

Strictly speaking, interest is the use of money, without regard to what compensation was paid or whether any compensation was paid. The two uses of the word interest correspond to the two uses of the word rent. Neither interest nor rent is money, yet common speech sanctions the expressions "paid the interest," and "paid the rent," instead of "paid for interest" and "paid for rent." 

Principal. The money at use is called the principal.

Rate. The rate is the number of hundredths of the principal paid for the use of the principal for one year. The rate is usually expressed as a per cent.

Legal Rate. This is the rate fixed by state law to apply to any interest-bearing obligation in the absence of an agreement between debtor and creditor as to the rate. 

Maximum Rate. This is the highest rate that contracting parties are permitted by law to agree upon. 153

INTEREST 

NOTE. The legal rate and the maximum rate are two distinct things. Most states prescribe both by statute, though a few states fix no maximum. In a few states the maximum rate and the legal rate are the same per cent. Any rate not greater than the maximum rate may be fixed by contract. 

Usury is the charging of a rate of interest in excess of the maximum rate allowed by law.

In the states where usury is forbidden, the law usually prescribes some sort of punishment for those who charge usurious rates of interest. See Burgess'

Commercial Law. 

Kinds of Interest. Interest may be simple or compound.

Simple interest is interest upon principal only. 

If $1000 be at interest, drawing 6% simple interest payable annually, $60.00 interest falls due at the end of each year. This $60.00 should be paid annually but even if it is not paid it is not permitted itself to draw any interest. 

Compound interest is interest upon the original principal plus interest accruals periodically calculated. 

If $1000 be at compound interest, at 6% calculated annually, it would draw interest as follows: 

1st yr. 

2d yr. 

Int. on $1000.00 at 6% 

Int. on $1060.00 ($1000.00+$60.00) 

3d yr. Int. on $1123.60 ($1000.00+$60.00+$60.00+$3.60) =$60.00 63.60 = 67.42

Note that the first year's interest is upon principal only; the second year's interest is upon principal and interest; and the third year's interest is upon principal, interest, and interest on interest. This would be continued, further and further compounded, until the end of the time for which the obligation runs. No 1 

On the first day of

OCTOBER, 1922 

$300 Simple interest on unpaid interest. This is a kind of interest that is sometimes used and which is a compromise between simple and compound. While not permitting interest to be compounded by the method just described, it permits interest items, if unpaid when due, to draw simple interest until paid. An illustration of this is an interest-bearing interest coupon

THE GILT EDGE MAID MINING COMPANY 

will pay to the bearer hereof THREE($300) DOLLARS at the First National Bank of Chicago, Illinois, being six/6/months' interest then due upon its bond No dated April 1st 1922. 

John Ritbition

A COUPON SECRETARY 

attached to a bond. If not paid when due, it bears simple interest until paid.

FACTORS IN FIGURING INTEREST Methods of Solution 

155 There are three methods of solution commonly followed:

The 

Ordinary Method, the Exact Method, and the Bankers' Method. 

A different result is secured by each method. Before you can understand these it will be necessary for you to study carefully the following statements:

Three factors in figuring interest. Every calculation of interest involves three factors: (a) Counting the time (b) Expressing the time fractionally in terms of a year 

(c) The computation 

(a) Counting the time: The time is sometimes counted by calendar months and days, sometimes by "compound subtraction," and sometimes by exact days.

Months and Days: Count forward the number of whole calendar months indicated (as 9 mo. from Jan. 10 to Oct. 10), then count exactly the days remaining. This method is used on the New York Stock Exchange and other stock exchanges.

Compound Subtraction: This is the method you learned under denominate numbers. It gives the same result as the months and days method when the extra days (after whole months have been counted) do not include the 31st of any month. If the extra days do include the 31st of some month, it gives one day less than the month and day method. If the extra days include the end of February, it gives two days more than the month and day method. 

Exact Days: This method of counting time requires no explanation, as the term defines itself. If the time exceeds a year, whole years are counted as such. (b) Expressing the time fractionally in terms of a year. The time having been counted, it is necessary to know how many days are to be considered a year. Sometimes a year is considered to be 360 days, and sometimes it is considered to be 365 days. (c) The computation. Having counted the time, and having determined whether the basis of calculation is to be 365 days or 360 days, the figuring itself presents little difficulty.

RULE: The rule for figuring interest whatever method is used, is

Multiply together the principal, the rate, and the time expressed in years.

INTEREST 

Ordinary Method. Count the time by compound subtraction and figure the interest on the basis of 360 days to the year. Consider a year as 12 months of 30 days each. This is the legal method for ordinary business transactions. 

EXAMPLE. Find the interest on $1000 at 6% from Oct. 21, 1926 to Apr. 6, 1928. Year Month Day 1928 1926 10 21 1 15 

Int. for 1 yr. = $60.00 

5 mo. 15 da. = 165 da. $1000. 1 6 165 -X: = $27.50 100 360 

Total = $87.50

It is stipulated by law in many states that in calculations of interest a year is to be considered as 12 months of 30 days each. This applies to the calculation of interest and also to the counting of the time. 

Exact Method. Count time by exact days and treat these days as 365ths of a year. 

EXAMPLE. Find the interest on $1000 at 6% from Oct. 21, to Apr. 6, 1928. 

Oct. 21, 1926 to Oct. 21, 1927 = 1 yr. 1926 of a year

Days in April

Bankers' Method. 168 Count the time by exact days, and figure the interest on the basis of 360 days to the year. 

EXAMPLE. Find the interest on $1000 at 6% from Oct. 21, 1926 to Apr. 6, 1928. 

NOTE. Do not confuse the expressions "Exact time" and "Exact interest."

Counting time by exact days is not all of the process of finding exact interest, nor is this method of counting time confined to the finding of exact interest.

INTEREST Processes of Figuring Interest 

Given a method of counting time, and having determined whether 360 or 365 days is to be considered a year, the result of a problem will not vary, whatever process be used in the actual figuring. There are a great many processes of figuring in use.

In this book there will be only two of these processes explained. Learn one of these processes and stick to it. 

The Cancellation Process 

RULE. Multiply together the principal, the rate, and the time expressed in years. 

Express all three terms fractionally, and cancel.

THE SIXTY-DAY PROCESS The Sixty-day Process 

159 This is sometimes called the 60-day process and sometimes called the 6% process.

RULE. Point off two places in the principal, which will give you the interest for 60 days at 6%. Multiply this by the number of times 60 days is contained in the whole time. 

Increase or decrease the result so secured, according as the rate is more or less than 6%.

Time-saving applications of the sixty-day process of 1%, Note the

It is well to bear in mind that since the interest for 60 days is 1% of the principal, the interest for 6 days (1 of 60 days) is and can be found by pointing off 3 places instead of 2. 

The interest for 6 days at 6% is found by pointing off 3 places. 

The interest for 60 days at 6% is found by pointing off 2 places. 

The interest for 600 days at 6% is found by pointing off 1 place. 

The interest for 6000 days at 6% equals the principal.

It is also well to bear in mind that factors in multiplication can be interchanged without affecting the result. Thus 3X4-4X3. 

Applied to solutions of problems in interest, the interest on $100 for 60 days the interest on $60 for 100 days. Interchanging principal and time, find the interest on the following:

Finding the Rate

To find the rate, the principal, interest, and time being known, find the interest at 1% and divide the given interest by this amount.

EXAMPLE. I had $620 invested in a business. It earned for me $9.92 in 96 days. To what rate of interest was this equivalent? $620 1 96 ·X· X =$1.653, interest on $620 at 1%. 1 100 360 $9.92÷$1.653=6, the rate %.

NOTE. Be careful not to drop fractions from a number which is to be used as a divisor. Give the result to the nearest %

FINDING AN UNKNOWN ELEMENT Finding the Time 

163 To find the time, the other factors of the problem being known, find the interest on the principal at the rate stated, for one year, and divide the total interest by the interest for one year. result will be the number of years in the time.

Finding the Principal 

To find the principal, the other factors of the problem being known, find the interest on $1.00 at the rate stated and for the time stated. Divide the total interest by this amount. The result will be the number of dollars in the principal. 

EXAMPLE. What principal will produce $12.80 at 6% interest in 4 mo. 15 da.? $1.00 will yield $0.0225 interest in 4 mo. 15 da. at 6%. $12.80÷$.0225 = $568.89, the number of dollars in the principal.

Finding the Present Value 

It is often important to know the present value of an obligation maturing at some future time. How much money should be placed at interest at a given rate on some previous date, in order that principal and interest will together exactly equal the amount of the obligation on the day it is due? 

First find what one dollar will amount to, in the given time, at the rate specified. Divide the amount of the obligation by the sum so secured; the result will be the number of dollars required. 

EXAMPLE. What is the present value of a debt of $765 due in 4 mo., if interest is worth 6%? 

The interest on $1.00 for 4 mo. at 6% is $.02. $1.00 will therefore amount in 4 mo. at 6% to $1.00+$.02=$1.02. $765÷$1.02=750, the number of dollars required.

The principal found in the above problem is frequently called 

Present Worth and the interest, True Discount.

Problems in Cost of Interest

Use the ordinary method in solving the following problems. 

1. On Apr. 1, 1927, F. C. Keach gave his note to Myron Duckels for $964.50. The note was due in six months and bore 6% interest. 

How much was due on the day of maturity? 

2. On Jan. 10, 1927, H. C. Schoolcraft received D. C. Brown's 

90-day note for $560 at 5%. The note was dated Jan. 1, 1927. 

How much was due Aug. 21, 1928, no payments having been made?

An interest-bearing note if unpaid at maturity continued to bear interest at the rate stated in it. A non-interest-bearing note bears interest after maturity at the legal rate of the state in which it is to be paid. 

3. Brookman & Co. bought from the Yellow Pine Lumber Co. mdse. worth $2256.50, terms 90-day note without interest on day of shipment. The note was payable at Rock Island, Ill.

The lumber was shipped Nov. 24, 1927. The note was not paid until June 16, 1928. How much was then paid?

See table of statutory rates of interest at the end of the chapter. 

4. A bill of goods was sold on June 27, 1927 amounting to $783.17 on the following terms: 2/10, n/30, int. at 6% after maturity. How much would have paid the bill on July 7, 1927? On 

July 10, 1927? On July 27, 1927? On Feb. 28, 1928? 

5. Upon graduation from high school a young man enters an office at a salary of $12.00 a week. What sum of money would have to be invested at 6% to yield the same income? (Simple interest is understood unless compound interest is specified.) 

6. A man bought a farm of 160 A. in North Dakota at $35 per A.

He paid $1600 cash at the time of purchase and gave four notes for $1000 each, with interest at 6%, payable in 1 yr., 2 yr., 3 yr., and 

4 yr. respectively. If all these notes were met at maturity, what was the total cost of the farm, including interest? 

7. If a sum of money is loaned at 6% per annum, how long will it take the principal to double itself? at 5%? at 8%? at 7%? 

8. What is the interest on $1262.50 at 6% from Aug. 15, 1927 to Mar. 23, 1928, ordinary method? exact method? bankers' method? 

9. A sells furniture for cash. B sells on six months' time. If A sells a dresser for $37.50, what will B have to charge for the same

INTEREST AND PROFIT AND LOSS 

167 dresser, if the use of money in a trading business is worth 12% per annum? 

10. On Sept. 1, 1927, Mr. Thomas bought a cottage for $2500, paying $1000 at time of purchase and $100.00 a month thereafter until paid. Interest was charged upon unpaid balance at 6%. 

The monthly payment was applied first upon interest due, the balance being applied upon principal. Show how each month's payment was applied. What was the total cost of interest? 

11. Two men are negotiating a contract by which one agrees to give the other three notes for $2000 each with interest at 6%, one note to be paid in one year, one in two years, and one in three years. 

The debtor asks that the interest be paid on each note only when the note is paid. The creditor insists that all interest be paid annually. How much difference is there between them? 

12. A house which cost $6500 was purchased by paying $1500 cash at the time of sale and giving a mortgage, bearing interest at 6%, for the balance. The house accommodates two families, each of whom pays $30 per month rent. The annual expenses are: 

Taxes, 21.27 mills on each dollar of a valuation of $4000; insurance, $15.40; water tax, $12.50 for each family; and repairs, $60. What rate of interest is earned on the money invested by the purchaser? 

13. A young man, when he becomes 25 years of age, is to receive, by the will of his father, $10,000. He is now 22 years of age and desires to sell his inheritance. If the use of money is worth 6%, simple interest, what should he receive at present for his inheritance? 

Problems of Interest and Profit and Loss 1. A bill of goods amounting to $1247.83 is bought on the terms "2/10, n/30." The purchaser is short of cash but wishes to save the discount on the bill. He can get a loan at the Union Trust Co. for 30 da. at 6%, but is undecided. Will it be to his interest to obtain a loan sufficient to pay the bill? If so, how much? 

2. An investor bought a house for $3750 and rented it for $30 per month. During the first year he paid $21.74 for taxes, $18.67 for insurance, $12.50 for water tax, and $15.00 for repairs. At the

INTEREST 

end of one year he sold the house for $4000. What was his profit on the venture and to what rate of interest on his original investment was it equivalent? 

3. A hardware dealer bought certain articles for $5.52 each. 

Three months after the purchase he sold them for $7.50 each. 

What was the per cent of profit? To what rate of interest was this equivalent? 

4. An apartment house containing six apartments is for sale. 

Two apartments rent for $32.50 per month each, two for $30 each, and two for $25 each. The annual expenses are: Taxes on $9500 at $20.48 per M; water tax, $8 for each apartment; insurance, $27.50; repairs (estimated), $120; other expenses and decorations (estimated), $200. What price can an investor afford to pay to realize 9% net on the investment? 

5. A peddler bought a car load of watermelons containing 1225 melons at 9 per melon, borrowing the purchase money for 

30 days at 7%. There were 85 bad and broken melons and the remainder were divided into three grades. There were 240 1st grade, 650 2d grade, and 250 3d grade melons that retailed at 25¢, 20¢, and 15¢ respectively. Eight wagons and eight men were employed to peddle these melons and closed out the car load in two days. If a man and wagon were worth $5 per day was there any profit beyond wages, and if so how much, after paying $22.78 for freight? 

6. A country bank, having a surplus of funds, deposited $50,000 with a city bank. The latter agreed to pay interest at the rate of 

21% annually, payable at the end of the year. The city bank loaned this money for a year at 6% and received the interest semi-annually. The interest so received was loaned at 6%. Find the profit made by the city bank the first year. 

7. A grain dealer bought on Sept. 27, 1927, three car loads of corn, containing respectively 900 bu., 1000 bu., and 950 bu., at 

82 per bu. The corn was stored on Sept. 29 at a charge of per bu. per month or fraction thereof. On Jan. 17 following, the corn was sold at 972¢ per bu. There was a shrinkage of 

60 bu. and there were expenses amounting to $57.83. The profit realized from the transaction was equivalent to what rate of interest on original investment?

To find the interest on more or less than $1000.00, find the interest on $1000.00 for the given time and rate from the table. Divide this by 1000 and multiply the quotient by the number of dollars in the required amount. 

The table gives the interest for any number of days from 1 to 15. If the interest be required for 16 days, to the interest for 15 days add the interest for 1 day; and so on for other numbers of days.

CHAPTER XIII NEGOTIABLE PAPER 

A negotiable paper is a written promise or order for the payment of money which if properly made out and transferred under proper conditions* passes to the purchaser free of any defenses which may have existed against it in the hands of the original holder. 

Checks, notes, drafts, certificates of deposit, and bonds are examples of negotiable paper.

A full discussion of negotiable paper will be found in your commercial law text. It is sufficient for the purposes of this book to note that the intent of the laws concerning commercial paper is to create a medium of exchange that will circulate freely because people are not afraid to take it at its apparent value (face, less discount or plus interest, with a possible deduction for exchange or for collection). Speaking broadly, it can have no concealed defenses.

A check is an order on a bank, payable in cash on demand. To be of value it must be drawn by one having funds in the bank * These conditions relate to the proper endorsement and delivery. The holder, to be protected in his rights, must be an innocent holder, for value, and must have secured the paper in the usual course of business. 171

NEGOTIABLE PAPER 

subject to check sufficient to cover it. Savings accounts are not subject to check.

The parties to a check are the drawer, the drawee, and the payee. 

In the model the drawer is D. B. Stewart, the drawee is the Merchants' Exchange Bank, and the payee is J. B. Olmstead. 

Checks should be presented for payment promptly. Failure to present a check within a reasonable time makes the holder liable for loss in case of failure of the bank and subjects him to inconvenience and perhaps loss if, when he finally presents the check, there are not sufficient funds to the drawer's credit in the bank to pay it.

A PROMISSORY NOTE 

A negotiable promissory note is an unconditional promise in writing made by one person to another, signed by the maker, engaging to pay on demand or at a fixed or determinable future time, a certain sum of money. It may be payable to order or to bearer.

The parties to a note are the maker and the payee. In the above note the maker is George R. Davis and the payee is 

Samuel Stewart.

The note shown is interest-bearing. How much is it worth on 

Sept. 2? How much on Oct. 2? on Nov. 1? on Jan. 1? How much 4 mo. after maturity, if unpaid?

A TRADE ACCEPTANCE Trade Acceptances and Drafts 

Sight drafts and time drafts are used in collecting accounts that are due or overdue. A trade acceptance is a modification of the draft made by inserting in it an acknowledgment of debt arising from the purchase of goods by the drawee from the drawer of the acceptance. This form of negotiable paper, when accepted by the drawee over his signature, has the force of a promissory note. It may then be converted into cash by the drawer by discounting it at a bank. The trade acceptance is used in this way by many business houses in collecting time accounts before they are due.

A draft, or bill of exchange, is an unconditional order in writ ing, addressed by one person to another, signed by the person giving it, requiring the person to whom it is addressed to pay on demand or at a fixed, determinable future time a given sum of money, to some person named therein or to his order or to bearer. 

If payable on demand, or "at sight," it is called a sight draft. 

If payable a certain number of days, months, or years after sight it is called a time draft. If payable on a given future date it is called a time draft.

Sight drafts are used largely for making collections when the debtor lives at a distance from the creditor. They are usually drawn through a bank but not necessarily so. Drafts through a bank constitute a good collecting medium because the debtor may not wish his bank to know that he owes money and is slow to pay it.

BANK DRAFT 

A bank draft is a draft drawn by one bank upon another. It is widely used as a medium of remittance. 

If A of Chicago wishes to make a remittance to B of St. Louis he will go to some Chicago bank and buy a draft upon some St. Louis bank with which the 

Chicago bank has connections. A will then send the draft to B, who will cash it at the St. Louis bank upon which it is drawn. For the services the Chicago bank may charge a fee, which is called exchange.

FINDING THE DATE OF MATURITY Endorsement 

175 The transfer of negotiable paper is accomplished by endorsement and delivery. The endorsement is the signature of the payee, written usually on the back of the left end of the paper, either by itself or with accompanying words specifying to whom the paper is transferred.

If the payee writes his name only as his endorsement, the effect is to transfer the paper to any bearer. This is called an endorsement in blank. If he precedes his signature with the words "Pay to the order of John Doe" or "Pay to John

Doe or order" the effect of the endorsement is to transfer the paper only to John Doe or to some other person whom he may designate. This is called an endorsement in full. Other forms of endorsement are restrictive endorsements, qualified endorsements, and endorsement "without recourse," each of which has the effect indicated by its name. (Endorsement is also spelled indorsement.) 

Finding the date of maturity Demand or sight paper is payable on being presented for payment. Time paper is due a certain number of days, months, or years after sight or after date. If "after date," time begins to run at once. If "after sight," time begins to run after the date of acceptance.

If paper is not accepted when presented for acceptance, or if it is due and payment is refused, it is said to be dishonored. If dishonored, the fact of dishonor is often attested by a notary public, in a form called a protest. Endorsers are entitled to a formal notice of protest, and failure to properly protest paper and duly notify endorsers will often relieve endorsers from liability. 

In counting time to maturity, omit the day upon which the note is dated; then count forward the number of days, or months, or years in the time as stated; the last day of the period stated is the due date.

Thus to fix the date of maturity for a 60-day note dated March 15, count 

March 16 to 31 inclusive as 16 days. 16 days left in March plus 30 days in 

April 46 days. There are 60-46 or 14 days to be counted in May. The due date is then May 14.

The time when interest begins to run is indicated in the wording of the paper. If the paper is interest-bearing, interest begins to run from its date, or from the date of acceptance in case of a time draft payable a certain number of days after acceptance.

CHAPTER XIV BANK DISCOUNT 

The holder of paper not yet due may realize cash upon it by selling it. The buyer of such paper usually accepts it at a discount. 

Banks do a large business in discounting paper before maturity. 

For purposes of figuring discount, paper may be divided into two general classes-interest-bearing paper and non-interest-bearing paper.

Non-interest-bearing paper is worth at maturity only its face, and it is upon this amount that discount is figured. Interestbearing paper is worth at maturity its face plus interest for the time it has run, and it is upon the full amount of its principal and interest that discount is figured. Discount is figured in the same way as interest, but generally at a different rate. 

The value of any note or other written obligation, at any time, is the face of the paper plus interest if any, for the full period of the obligation, and minus discount. The discount must be based on the combined principal and interest. 

The period of time for which discount is figured is called the term of discount. It is usually counted in exact days.

273 304 184 215 243 274 153 184 212 243 122 153 181 92 123 151 182 61 92 120 151 181 212 242 273 304 334 365 30 31 62 90 121 151 182 212 243 274 304 335 365 334 365 30 304 335 365 31 273 304 334 365 31 273 303 334 365 243 273 304 335 61 92 122 153 183 62 92 123 153 61 92 122 30 61 91 365 31 61 This table gives the exact number of days from any day of any month to the corresponding day of any other month for any period of time not more than one year.

Thus, the number of days from April 27 to July 27 would be 91. This is found by locating April in the column to the left and following along the horizontal line to the right until the column headed July is reached. The number at the junction of these two columns is 91. This indicates that the time from 

April 27 to July 27 is 91 days. 

The time from Apr. 27 to Aug. 10 would be found as follows: The time from 

Apr. 27 to July 27 is 91 days. From July 27 to Aug. 10 is 14 days. 91 days+ 

14 days = 105 days.

EXAMPLE. A note for $350, dated Mar. 1, due in 60 days, was discounted Mar. 18 at 6%. Find the proceeds of the note. 

Mar. 1+60 days = Apr. 30, date of maturity. 

From Mar. 18 to Apr. 30=43 days, the term of discount. 

Interest on $350 for 43 days = $2.51, amount of discount. $350-$2.51 $347.49, proceeds of note.

CHAPTER XV TRADE AND CASH DISCOUNT 

Trade discount is a deduction from list or catalog price, made for the purpose of fixing the invoice price to the trade. It is usually expressed as a given per cent of the list price.

This method of fixing price is convenient when the seller wishes to quote different prices to different classes of customers, or wishes to make changes in price from time to time. Many other conveniences and economies of the method will be apparent to the thoughtful student. 

EXAMPLE. A merchant who sells to both wholesale and retail trade publishes a catalog with prices for the retail trade. He offers a 25% trade discount to wholesalers. What is his wholesale price on an article listed at $28.00? 

List price 

Trade discount (25%) 

Invoice price $28.00 7.00 $21.00

Series of Trade Discounts 

EXAMPLE. The merchant mentioned in the preceding example offers an additional trade discount of 10% to jobbers; that is, his price to jobbers is "list less 25 and 10%." What is his July price to jobbers on an article listed at $28.00, if as a special inducement during July he offers an additional 5%? 

List price 

1st trade discount (25%) $28.00 7.00 $21.00 

2d trade discount (10%) 2.10 $18.90 

3d trade discount (5%) 

Invoice price .94 $17.96

NOTE. The foregoing problem could have been solved as follows: 

100%-25% 75%; 75%-10% of itself = 67%; 67%-5% of itself = 64%; 

64% of $28=$17.96 or, 95% of 90% of 75% =64.125%;.64125×$28=$17.96. 184

PROBLEMS IN BILLING 

187 Cash discount is a deduction from invoice price made in consideration of payment within a specified time. 

The offer of a discount for cash is usually made in the terms of sale, printed or written as a part of the heading of an invoice. 

Thus "2/10 n/30 (2% 10 days, net 30 days)" means that the customer may deduct 2% if he pays cash in ten days, but if he fails to do this the bill is due without discount in 30 days. 

Sometimes the terms of sale contain the additional specification that the charge draws interest if not paid at maturity. 

Taking a discount for cash in advance of maturity is usually regarded as a purely financial transaction, not a trading transaction. Hence cash discount is not deducted, usually, in figuring costs. Some authorities favor the practice, however.

1. A dealer advertised an ice chest for $14.56. been selling for $20. The advertisement read, "To each person who presents this ad, a further discount of $1.00 will be allowed." 

Brown presented the advertisement and bought an ice chest. At what rate of discount did he buy it? 

2. A canvasser sold stereoscopic views at 12 each or $1.50 a dozen. As an inducement for large orders, he made it a rule to give two pictures extra with each $3.00 purchase. To what rate of discount was that equivalent?

Unit Six

HOW WE PAY FOR GOVERNMENT AND PROTECTION 

Everyone has something to say when you mention taxes. Some people also discuss the cost of insurance. These are live subjects in every community in the United States. Unit 

Six discusses the kinds of taxation used by the government, the principles underlying the levying of taxes, and the method by which the amount to be paid by an individual is determined. The Unit also gives careful attention to an explanation of the protection that insurance gives against losses caused by fire and losses caused by death. Examples make plain the methods adopted for the financial protection of society against such losses. 

As in earlier Units, the book provides a 

Preview Test by which you may measure your own already acquired knowledge of these subjects and may see where in this Unit you will need to do most work in order to gain mastery of the Unit. 193

WRITTEN TEST Preview Test in Taxation, Customs, and Insurance 

Make your work sheet show the process by which you solve each problem or example. Number the answers to correspond to the numbers in the test. 

1. What is a franchise tax? How does the franchise tax differ from the fees paid for licenses to operate automobiles on the highways? 

2. If the total rate (not including school tax) in your city is $2.95 on each $100 of assessed valuation, and Richardson's real estate is assessed at $4,000 and his personal property at $1,500. what is the amount of his tax for one year? 

3. In the income tax law of the United States, the persona) exemption for single men and women is $1,000, and for married men or for a man and his wife it is $2,500. The rate is 4%. 

What amount of tax will a single man pay, if his total income is $3,000 and he has no deductions or credits except the personal exemption? What will a married man pay, if he has an income of $3,000 and has no deductions or credits except the personal exemption? 

4. A merchant in New York imports a shipment of 750 gross of lead pencils, valued at 18¢ a dozen. The duty on lead pencils is 30% ad valorem plus 50 cents per gross. How much must the merchant pay in order to get this shipment? 

5. A shipment of linoleum, manufactured in Europe, was received in New York. The value was given as $1,600. What is the amount of duty to be paid by the importer, the rate on linoleum being 42% ad valorem ? 

6. If the rate of fire insurance on a certain type of building in your city is $1.65 per hundred, what will be the premium on a policy insuring the building for one year for $4,500? 

7. A building having a replacement value of $3,600 is insured for 80% of its value. In case of total destruction by fire, how much will the insurance company pay the insured owner? 

8. If the annual rate on $1000 ordinary life policy issued to a man at the age of 24 years is $19.66, what annual premium must he pay on a policy for $3,500?

CHAPTER XVI TAXATION 

A tax is a sum of money demanded by the Federal, state, or local government of those who live or own property under its protection. The most common kinds of taxes are property tax, income tax, sales or merchants' occupation tax, franchise tax, and corporation tax. There are also license fees, duties and customs on imports, internal revenue tax, estate and inheritance taxes, and other kinds.

A property tax is levied on real and personal property. 

An income tax is levied on incomes of individuals and corporations by the Federal government and many of the states. 

A sales or merchants' occupation tax is a specified rate to be levied on the amount of sales made by an individual or a corporation. The tax is paid by the purchaser to the merchant in the sale. The rate varies but is usually from 14 to 2¢ or 3¢ on a dollar.

A franchise tax is a sum paid for the privilege of doing business under special conditions often involving the granting of monopoly in a specified type of service. It is illustrated by the tax paid by a street car company for the use of the streets in a city.

A corporation tax is a tax levied on a corporation in the state in which it is chartered, without respect to the area in which it transacts business. 

License fees are taxes charged for the privilege of carrying on some specified type of business or engaging in some specified form of activity. 

License fees are charged for selling liquor, and for hunting and fishing in a specified territory, etc.

Duties are taxes imposed by the Federal government on many kinds of imported goods.

Internal revenue taxes are collected by the Federal government on some kinds of commodities produced or traded in as merchandise. 

Taxes on liquor and tobacco are examples of this kind of tax. 

Estate taxes and inheritance taxes are levied on the value of the estates. of deceased persons and on the value of property inherited from deceased persons.

It is customary to express the tax rate as mills on $1, as dollars on $100, or as dollars on $1000. Sometimes it is expressed as a per cent. When a tax rate is expressed as mills, it means that the given number of mills is the tax on each $1 of valuation. 195

TAXATION The Method of Taxation 

The methods of taxation followed by taxing bodies, whether the taxing body is the nation, state, county, town (township), or village, are very similar. A budget of appropriations is prepared, the total of which is the amount required for the expenditures of the ensuing year or period of years to be covered. Taxable properties (and persons) are listed. The rate of taxation is fixed by comparing the amount needed with the valuations of taxable properties (and persons).

TAXATION Income Tax 

The United States government and some of the state governments levy taxes upon incomes of individuals and corporations, in excess of specified amounts. The examples given below conform to the Federal Income Tax, as revised in the Revenue Act of 1934.

Gross income is the total income from business, profession, and invest ments. It also includes gains from the sale of property. Net income is the income remaining after making certain deductions, the most important of which are (a) business expenses, (b) interest paid, (c) taxes paid, (d) depreciation, and (e) charitable and other contributions. The law also allows as credits against the net income (1) personal exemption, $1,000 for a single person and $2,500 for head of family or man and wife, (2) $400 for each dependent, (3) dividends and interest from certain sources, and (4) earned income credit.

The normal income tax is a tax of 4% on the net income after making the allowed deductions and credits. The surtax is an additional tax on a graduated scale. It is levied on the "surtax net income."' The credits for these two kinds of net income are not identical. The personal exemptions and the credits for dependents apply to both; but the credits for dividends, interest, earned income, etc., apply only in computing the normal tax. The law allows as credit against normal tax an amount equal to 10% of a taxpayer's "earned net income,'' that is, income from salary or compensation for personal services. Income up to $3,000 is considered as all earned income; but if the earned income is over $14,000, only $14,000 may be so treated.

EXAMPLE (with both normal tax and surtax)

CHAPTER XVII UNITED STATES CUSTOMS 

Customs, or duties, are taxes upon commodities imported or exported. They are of two kinds, ad valorem and specific. 

An ad valorem duty is a duty levied "according to value" (from ad, according to, and valorem, value). It is a certain per cent of the value of the goods in the country of origin. 

Imported merchandise is appraised by the custom house officials, and the duty is based upon this appraisal of value. The value fixed is the price at which the merchandise is freely offered for sale in the country of origin, in usual wholesale quantities, on the date of shipment. 

The date of shipment, in case of goods originating from an interior point, is the date of starting from that point. Thus, on goods originating in Switzerland and shipped from Antwerp, the price fixed would be the price in Switzerland at the time of leaving the point of origin. 

Before imports are cleared, the importer "declares" their value to the custom house officials and pays a duty based on the declaration. The goods are then appraised and cleared if the declared value is O. K'd. by the appraisers. 

If not, further duties and penalties are imposed. 

Specific duty is a duty levied according to quantity, as measured by the yard, pound, bushel, gallon, or other unit of measure. 

The duty on a commodity may be ad valorem, or specific, or both. 

A tariff is a list or schedule of rates of duties. The word tariff is also used to mean the duties themselves, hence is synonymous with customs and duties.

Tare is a deduction from the gross weight of a shipment to allow for the weight of the box, bottle, barrel, crate, or other container in which it was shipped. 

Leakage and breakage are allowances for waste from leaking and breaking.

Goods can be cleared only at a "port of entry," where a government custom house is located. The goods imported must pass through the hands of the officials of the custom house, who assess and collect the duty. The coasts and borders of the United States are divided into districts each one of which has its port of entry. 202

TABLE OF CUSTOMS RATES 

203 Goods may enter the country at a point which is not a port of clearance but if so must be bonded at once for clearance at a regular port of entry. 

Goods not for immediate delivery or "consumption' and upon which duty is not paid at the time of entry, may be stored in a bonded warehouse for a period of three years or less. A bonded warehouse is a warehouse the owner of which is under bonds to the government not to deliver the goods, except for export or to another bonded warehouse, until the duty is paid. 

Invoices of goods imported are usually made out in the currency of the country from which they come. The par value of these currencies in exchange fluctuates, and on that account no table of standardized values can be made. In solving problems on the next page use the values suggested in a foot note.

CHAPTER XVIII INSURANCE 

Insurance is a guaranty of protection against loss arising from a specified cause. The money paid by the insured for the insurance is called the premium. The rate of insurance is usually expressed as a certain amount for each $100 of insurance, as $1.50 per hundred. It may be expressed as a per cent; 11%, meaning $1.50 per hundred. An insurance policy is a contract by which the insurer agrees, for a specified premium, to indemnify the insured to a stipulated amount in case of loss arising from the cause given in the policy. 

The most common kinds of insurance are fire, life, and accident insurance. There are many other kinds, including marine, automobile, employer's liability, live stock, crop, plate glass, rain, hail, tornado, health insurance, etc. An employer can be insured against loss on account of accidents to employees; a merchant can be insured against loss of profits on goods destroyed by fire. A merchant having stock stored in various places may have it all insured in a policy called a "fleet' policy. The same kind of policy may be used for insuring all the automobiles used by a corporation. In short, insurance can be obtained in almost any case in which there is a risk, by the person having an insurable interest. 

Fire Insurance 

A fire insurance policy is a contract by which the insurer, for a specified premium, agrees to indemnify the insured up to a certain amount in case of loss of property by fire. "Loss" means loss or damage. "Loss by fire" also covers damage by water or by chemicals used in extinguishing the fire, or other damage done in extinguishing the fire. The loss must be to the property specified in the contract of insurance.

A fire insurance policy covers only direct loss by fire, and not any indirect damages which the insured may contend that he has sustained, such as suspension of business activity.

Losses will not be paid in excess of the sum insured, nor in excess of the interest of the insured except by special agreement endorsed on the policy. 205

INSURANCE 

The insurance company is not liable, in case of total loss, beyond the sound value of the property. The sound value is the actual value on the day of the fire.

In no case is the company liable beyond what it would cost to replace or repair the property.

The company is liable for loss from fire caused by lightning (but not tornado) but not for damage from the lightning itself, except by special agreement. 

If a party who has an insurable interest but who is not an owner, is to be protected, a clause extending the insurance to cover the additional interest must be embodied in the policy. 

In case there are claims against persons other than the insurer, for damages on account of the fire, the insurer will exercise its right of subrogation—that is, that the insurer shall be substituted for the insured as the holder of such claims, so that the insured may not collect damages from two sources. 

By special agreement the insured may be permitted to carry other insurance, to work overtime, to make alterations and repairs, to store gasoline, and to do other things for which permission is denied in the body of the policy. If other insurance is carried all insurers will bear the loss pro rata, as will be explained more fully later. 

The "special agreements" referred to in the foregoing paragraphs are written on separate slips of paper called "riders” and attached to the policy. 

A rider may cover some feature not mentioned in the printed policy form, or it may be a waiver of some right of either party. The agreement on a rider supersedes any terms of the printed contract with which it conflicts. 

The 80% co-insurance clause, which will be described more fully later, and other similar stipulations, if required, are attached as riders. 

The foregoing particulars conform to the New York Standard Policy form, which is generally accepted, though subject in some states to statutory provisions not in effect in other states. 

Rates. Rates of fire insurance are fixed by rating bodies.

Sometimes these bodies are composed of representatives of the companies, as the Chicago Board of Underwriters, sometimes of persons appointed by the local or state government, sometimes of committees of both kinds, acting together either officially or in a mutually advisory capacity.

Rates are compiled by individuals or rating bureaus whose sole business is the measuring of fire hazards. Schedules of fire insurance rates compiled by such individuals or bureaus are often used. 

The rates are separately determined for each building and depend upon physical condition, exposures, moral hazards, nature of occupancy, facilities for extinguishment of fire, and other related considerations.

FIRE INSURANCE

Three-fourths Value Clause. Where provisions for protection from fire are inadequate, and when over-insurance is feared, insurance companies sometimes stipulate that the insurer shall in no event be liable for more than three-fourths of the sound value of each item of the property covered. What these items are is determined by the enumeration and valuation of each which is written in the body of the policy. 

WRITTEN. In each of the following cases the three-fourths value clause was in effect. How much did the insured receive in each case? 

1. Property worth $4000 and insured for $3000 was damaged $3500. 

2. Property worth $4000 and insured for $3000 was totally destroyed. 

3. Property worth $4000 and insured for $3000 was damaged $3000. 

4. Property insured for $3000 and worth $4250 was found to be damaged $500.

INSURANCE 

5. Property worth $4000 and insured for $4000 was totally destroyed. 

6. Property worth $3500 and insured for $4000 was damaged $3000. 

7. Walton insured his barn and contents all under one policy, for $12,500 at an average rate of 48¢. The items insured, as enumerated in the policy, were: Barn, $5000; horses and cattle, $3500; wagons and harness, $2250; hay and grain, $1750. The barn caught fire and the losses were as follows: Barn, $1500; horses and cattle, $1250; wagons and harness, $2000; hay and grain, $1750. How much money did the insurance company owe Walton? 

The Co-Insurance Clause. A large percentage of fire losses are only partial. If all policies were framed to permit the policy holder to collect in full for his partial loss, provided that loss were within the limits of the policy, the tendency would be for parties taking out insurance to take out only enough insurance to cover probable partial losses. In such a case, the policy holder would be insuring only a part of his whole property, but would be fully protected to all probable extent of damage. 

To encourage property owners to carry enough insurance, companies offer reduced rates to apply to policies which contain a special clause called a co-insurance or contribution clause. 

The co-insurance clause most frequently used is the 80% coinsurance clause. It is an agreement upon the part of the insured that he will carry insurance equal to 80% of the value of the property insured. All of this 80% that is not placed with insurance companies the insured agrees to carry himself. 

Thus an owner of property worth $10,000.00 agrees under the 80% coinsurance clause, that he will either carry $8000.00 worth of insurance or will himself be responsible for that part of the $8000.00 which he does not place with the insurance companies.

If this owner takes out only $6000.00 worth of insurance, he himself carries $2000.00, or one-fourth of the total of $8000.00 insurance agreed upon. In case of any loss he agrees to bear one-fourth of the loss. The company bears the remainder of the loss provided it does not exceed the face of the policy.

If this owner takes out $8000.00 insurance, or more, he is living up to his agreement fully and can collect the full amount of the loss, provided, of course, that it is not more than the amount of the policy.

FIRE INSURANCE 

209 WRITTEN. The policy in each of the first six problems following contains the 80% coinsurance clause. How much was due the insured in each case? 

1. A property worth $30,000, insured for $20,000, was damaged $6,000. 

2. A property worth $25,000, insured for $20,000, was damaged $3000. 

3. Household goods worth $8000, insured for $5000, were damaged $2500. 

4. Property worth $1000, insured for $800, was damaged $300. 

5. A stock of goods worth $2000, insured for $1200, was damaged $500. 

6. A barn worth $3000, insured for $2250, was damaged $750. 

7. Barnes Bros. insured a stock of goods worth $11,000, for $9000 under a policy containing a 90% coinsurance clause. In case of a loss of $600, how much should they receive? 

8. A policy containing a 90% coinsurance clause was issued for $4000 on a stock of goods worth $7500. How much should the company pay in case of a loss of $1200?

Risks Covered by Several Companies. A person may wish to insure his property in several companies, or a company may not wish to carry all the risk on a property. In such a case two or more policies, covering the same property, may be issued by different companies.

NOTE. Sometimes a single policy for a large amount will be issued by one company, which will then proceed, through its underwriting department, to reinsure the property in other companies for smaller amounts, thus dividing the risk. Such a case involves no problem, however, for the policy-holder. 

When there are two or more insurers each dealing directly with the policy-holder, and a loss occurs, each company will contribute to the loss its pro rata share. The policy-holder cannot collect more for his loss than he would have been able to collect had all his insurance been placed with one company. 

It is important that the written portions of all policies covering the same property should read exactly alike. If they are not concurrent, great confusion will result in case of adjustment of a fire loss.

INSURANCE WRITTEN 

1. A stock of goods worth $25,000 is insured in four companies, under policies containing the 80% coinsurance clause, for $8000, $5000, $3000, and $4000 respectively. If there is a fire loss of $4000, how much will each company pay?

5. A farmer insured his barn, which cost $3000, for $2500, under the provisions of the "three-fourths value clause." The 

Ohio Farmers Ins. Co. took a risk of $1500 and the Fireman's

Cancellation of Insurance. A policy of insurance may be canceled by either the insured or the insurer. 

The insurer must give five days notice of cancellation and must refund pro rata the unearned premium. 

Thus, if a policy is issued for one year and the premium is paid, and the insurer should cancel the policy after 4 months, the insurer must refund the premium for the other 8 months. The pro rata basis would require the insurer to refund (3) of the premium for one year.

FIRE INSURANCE 

211 The insured need not give notice to the insurer in advance in canceling the policy. In this case the insurer does not refund unearned premium on a pro rata basis, but instead charges what is known as a "short rate" which is the rate the insured would have had to pay had he bought short time insurance at the beginning. 

The difference between the premium paid at first and the short rate charge, is refunded to the insured. Time is counted by exact days. 

When insurance is canceled because of removal, and a new policy for the same amount taken out in the same company, short rates do not apply.

If a policy is canceled and a smaller policy issued on the same risk by the same company, short rates apply to the amount dropped but not to the amount continued.

Open Policies. An open policy is one in which the amount of the risk is left open and changes from time to time as the property increases or decreases in total value. The changes in value are recorded in the company's Open Policy Book. Premiums are paid on the amounts shown by this policy book as having been at risk from time to time, and settlements in case of loss are made on the basis of the showing of the policy book at the time of the fire.

Whenever the total value diminishes, the amount of insurance is reduced accordingly, and the cost is figured, short rate, on the amount so canceled. 

Charges cannot be made except as goods are taken out. The amount canceled is always taken from that which has been longest standing.

Life Insurance 

Life Insurance is guaranty of protection under a policy or contract by which the insurer, for a specified premium, undertakes to pay a given sum upon the death of a person named therein (the insured), or after the lapse of a certain number of years, the insured being still alive.

The premium is usually a series of annual payments in advance, but an advance payment may be made for any number of years, or the entire consideration may be a single payment in advance. 

The beneficiary is the person to whom the insurance is payable. 

There are many kinds of life insurance. The principal kinds are (1) ordinary life, also called straight life, or life, (2) limited payment life, and (3) endowment.

Ordinary Life. Under an ordinary life policy payments of premium are made annually in advance and the face of the policy is payable at death only.

LIFE INSURANCE 

215 

Limited Payment Life. Under this form of policy payments of premium are made annually for a stipulated number of years only; as 10, 20, or 30. Twenty-payment life is probably the most common of the limited payment policies. The face of the policy is payable at death, whether before or after the expiration of the specified period.

The term whole life policy includes both ordinary life and limited payment life. 

Endowment. Under this form of policy the method of paying premiums is the same as under the limited payment policy, payments being completed in a given number of years. Ten- and twenty-year endowment policies are both common. The face of the policy, however, under this plan, is payable at the end of the period specified in the contract, if the insured is then living. If the insured dies before the end of the specified period, the face of the policy is payable at once, and premium payments cease. 

Term Insurance is that form of life insurance whereby a life is insured for a given term of years or until the insured reaches a given age. The payments are usually made annually. It has no value beyond the period of time for which the premium is paid. In this respect it is similar to fire insurance.

Assessment Insurance is a form of life insurance under which the survivors of a mutual association are assessed to pay the claims for deaths of members, and the expenses of the organization. 

There are many other forms of life insurance and many different kinds of policies issued under each.

Theory of Life Insurance. All persons do not live to the same age. There is an average length of life, however, and there is an average death rate at each age. Compilers of insurance statistics and tables, called actuaries, prepare tables of mortality and tables of expectation of life at various ages, and upon these statistics they figure the premiums which the company should charge for insurance. The companies, of course, expect to lose in individual cases where the insured dies "before his time," but they collect additional premiums from those who live longer than the average. 

Reserves. Under the forms of insurance shown in the table of premiums, the insurer sets aside a part of each premium. This reserve is invested and allowed to accumulate to meet claims.

LIFE INSURANCE 

217 If the insured ceases to pay his premiums, he car receive from the insurer that part of its reserve which comes from his payments. 

The amount which he would thus receive is called the cash surrender value of his policy. Instead of the money, the insured at his option, may accept from the company whatever amount of fully paid up insurance the money will buy, or he may arrange that the full face of his policy will remain in force for whatever period of time the money will pay for.

Loan Value. A policy-holder can borrow on his policy. The amount which he can borrow, called the loan value of the policy, is in some companies as great as the cash surrender value. 

In most states there is some degree of regulation of life insurance companies by law, particularly in regard to the reserves to be created. The laws of some states require that statements concerning the cash surrender value, the loan value, the amount of paid up insurance its reserve will purchase, and the extension of the entire policy its reserve will purchase, be included in the policy itself, and be a part of the contract of insurance. 

Dividends. Some companies make their policy-holders participators in their earnings. In such a case, the policy-holder, when he receives notice of premium due, also receives notice of the amount of his share of the previous year's earnings. This dividend may be (1) withdrawn in cash, (2) applied to the payment of premiums, (3) used to purchase paid-up insurance, (4) left at interest.

From the foregoing it is clear that money invested in life insurance cannot be entirely forfeited even by failure to continue premium payments. The following table of non-forfeiture provisions shows the three alternatives which the policy-holder is offered by one well-known company in case of non-payment of premiums: (1) the time for which the policy will be extended, (2) the amount of paid-up insurance which he can get, (3) the cash surrender or loan value. Note that in this case the cash surrender value and the loan value are the same; this is not always true. The values shown are supposed to be based on the reserve. 

The table is not complete.

Unit Seven ANALYSIS OF EXPENSE IN INDUSTRY 

Large industrial corporations use very exact methods of computing profit and loss on trading and of ascertaining just what it costs to produce one unit of each kind of goods. In this manner they are able to know what departments are too expensive in proportion to their part in the business of the corporation, in what items additional expense would be justified, and in what respects the organization itself could be strengthened by changes in administrative and production control. In Unit Seven you will be interested in learning how this is done. Leaders in the business world need this knowledge and skill. 

A brief Preview Test gives you the opportunity to try your knowledge and skill in the work outlined above. Write the answers to the questions in the Test, and make a list of the kinds of work in this Unit upon which you find you are not sure of your ability. Then use the list to check your progress in mastery of the Unit. 223

CHAPTER XIX  

The prime cost, or net cost, of goods purchased is the original purchase price as shown by the invoice. 

The gross cost is the total cost after adding in-freight and cartage, insurance and storage en route, and any other direct charges entering into the cost of the delivered goods. 

When these direct charges can not be applied integrally to units of purchase, it is usual to find the ratio of direct charges to purchases and express this as a per cent of the purchase price.

The cost of goods sold is found by adding to the purchase price of the goods sold the per cent necessary to cover direct charges. 

To find the cost of goods sold during a given period, add to the inventory at the beginning of the period the amount of purchases, and deduct the inventory at the end of the period. Then add the per cent necessary to cover direct charges.

The gross sales is the total of sales at invoice price. 

The net returns from sales is the amount remaining after deducting from gross sales the returns and allowances, sales commissions, and shipping expense. Sometimes a deduction is made for bad debts.

The profit or loss on trading is the difference between the gross cost of goods sold and the net returns from sales. The per cent of profit or loss is the amount of profit or loss expressed as a per cent; it may be expressed as a per cent of the prime cost, or of the gross cost, or of the net returns, or of the selling price. 

The profit or loss on doing business is the profit or loss on trading less the cost of doing business.

HOW BUSINESS HOUSES FIGURE PROFITS 

What was the average weight of the five pigs?

What was the average daily cost of feeding? 229 The contest lasted 17 weeks. What was the weight of the first pig at the beginning of the contest? If feeding cost only half as much per day during the time before the start of the contest and if the pig was sold at $7.30 per hundred, what was the profit on the pig?

How Business Houses Figure Profits 

During the last few years business houses have quite generally adopted the method of figuring the per cent of profit on selling price, rather than the cost. Of course, the cost of doing business and the desired net profit enter into the actual wholesale cost. 

For example, a retail merchant buys gloves at $21.36 a dozen ($1.78 a pair). He sells them for $30.00 a dozen ($2.50 a pair). 

Where does he look for his profit? Obviously, to the selling price and not to the cost. This $8.64 represents his gross profit on the sale and since it is the $30.00 which pays the profit, it is very evident that the sale is the correct base upon which he shall figure his profit.

If I buy an article for $1.00 which includes parcel post (shipping) what will I have to sell it for to make a net profit of 15 per cent, after deducting 10 per cent for the cost of doing business?

If the sales price is to be used as the basis for figuring my profit, the article must be sold for $1.33. The method is as follows: 100% 

S 10% 15% 25% 75% 

The Selling Price 

Cost of doing business 

Desired net profit 

Selling Cost and Desired profit 

= Wholesale Cost 

75% or 34 of the price at which the article must be sold to make a net profit of 15 per cent, after deducting 10 per cent for the cost of doing business, equals $1.00 (100 cents). 

100=1.333. Therefore, $1.33 is the S. P. 

Therefore, the selling price of the article must be $1.33.

To find the price at which an article will have to be sold in order to cover the cost of doing business and at the same time realize the desired net profit, add together the per cent of cost of doing business and the desired per cent of net profit. Deduct this from 100 per cent. Divide the cost price by this result.

1. At what price must a publisher sell a school book that cost him 663 (manufacturing cost) in order to realize a net profit of 25% if he estimates that the selling expense is 20% and overhead 5%? 

2. At what price must a merchant sell hats that cost him $3.00 if the overhead expense is 15% and the selling expense 

20%?, to realize a profit of 25%? 

3. A certain article costs $1.00 wholesale including freight. 

What will it have to be sold for to bring a net profit of 10% after allowing 18% for the cost of doing business? 

4. What per cent profit must be added to the cost of an article so that 25% may be deducted from the asking price and a profit of 20% still realized on the cost? 

5. What must be asked for goods costing $20.00 in order that a discount of 20% may be allowed and a profit of 20% be maintained? 

6. Find the selling price of a hat costing $3.50 if store expense is 20% of cost and the merchant wishes to make 20% above cost and expenses. 

7. The manufacturing cost of an article is $1.50. At what price must this article be sold to yield the merchant a profit of 

20% if the salesman is allowed 25% of the selling price for making the sale?

Profit or Loss on Trading

As previously defined, profit or loss on trading is the difference between the gross cost of merchandise and the net returns from sales, without consideration of the general expenses of the business. 

This profit or loss may be stated as a per cent of prime cost, gross cost, net sales, or gross sales.

Cost of Doing Business 

The cost of doing business is an expression which defines itself. 

In a merchandising business it includes rent, salaries, light and heat, taxes, advertising, depreciation, and all other expenses except those that make up the gross cost of merchandise. 

Depreciation is the decrease in the value of property which takes place as it wears out, goes out of use, etc.

18 Depreciation

A very simple method of figuring annual depreciation is to find the difference between the value at the beginning of the life of the property and the value at the end, and divide by the number of years. Use this method in the following problems. 

1. If a frame building is estimated to be usable for forty years, what per cent of the original cost should be charged off each year? 

2. If a frame building cost $1500.00 and an amount equal to 

21% of the cost be charged off each year, what is the average amount invested in the building for 40 years? 

3. The duration of an ordinary patent is 17 years. If a firm paid $5000 for a patent, what amount should it charge off the cost each year? 

4. On a farm in Kansas 16,156 bushels of corn, 70 lb. to the bushel, was weighed as it was husked. In July following, it was sold and measured 14,896 bushels. What was the per cent of shrinkage? If it sold for 56¢ a bushel, what did it bring? 

5. A man paid $2100.00 for an automobile. His license and tax cost $16.00 Expenses for repairs for the year were $72.40; oil, $5.85; gasoline, 440 gallons at 16¢. He ran 5280 miles and his machine depreciated 28%. What was the cost per mile?

Manufacturing Costs include Materials, Labor, and Factory Ex pense. (See Fig. 1.) Each one of these includes direct costs and indirect costs. (See Fig. 2.)

Direct Costs (see Fig. 2) are the materials, productive labor, and direct expense consumed in manufacture, and chargeable specifically to some unit of manufacture. 

Indirect Cost (or overhead) is the cost of materials, labor, and expense not so chargeable.

The Prime Cost of any unit of manufacture is the cost of materials and labor charged directly to it. The Factory 

Gen'l O.H. Cost, or production cost, is the prime cost plus factory overhead. The Gross Cost is the factory cost plus a 

(See Figs. 2 and 3.) 

burden for general overhead expense.

Fixed Factory Charges, including such items as light and heat, and power, can be charged directly to the departments, in whole or in part, each department being charged with its fair proportion and the balance being charged to General Expense.

COSTS Distribution of Overhead 

Overhead expenses must be distributed in some fair proportion among the units of manufacture. In the following pages four well-known methods are briefly treated. 

Direct Labor Method 

Under this method the burden is distributed over labor costs. 

The rate of burden is the ratio of the overhead charge to the total of direct labor.

EXAMPLE. Find the factory cost of an article if the materials used cost $40 and the direct labor $55, factory overhead for the month being $800 and total direct labor $4000. 

WRITTEN 

$800÷$4000=20%, rate of burden $55.20 $11., burden on one article $40+$55+$11-$106, factory cost 1. A certain factory has been in operation for one year and during that time has paid $15,000 for productive labor and $3750 for factory overhead. What is its rate of factory burden to be added to direct labor cost?

Direct Labor Hour Method 

Under this method, the overhead expense is distributed among the different jobs in proportion to the time spent on each job in direct labor.

Prime Cost Method 

Under this method, overhead charges are distributed to the different jobs or articles in the ratio which the prime costs of the different jobs or articles bear to the total prime costs. 

EXAMPLE. During one month, a factory paid $2000 for productive labor and used material that cost $1500. The overhead expense for that month amounted to $875. The material for one desk cost $18 and the direct labor on it was 17 hr. at 75¢ per hour. 

What was the gross cost of production of the desk? 

WRITTEN 

$2000+$1500= $3500= total prime cost. $875÷$3500=25%, rate of burden. $18.00 cost of material for the desk. $12.75 cost of productive labor on the desk. $30.75 prime cost of the desk. $7.69 overhead charges (25% of $30.75). $38.44 gross cost of production. 1. The material used in one month by the Ideal Manufacturing 

Company cost $6392.85; amount paid for productive labor was $3570, of which $422.80 was for work done the preceding month (accrued productive labor); and overhead amounted to $932.90. 

The cost sheets for a certain job showed that requisitions had been honored for material amounting to $39.87, and that labor on the job was as follows: 71 hours at 333; 68 hours at 60; and 41½ hours at 44. Unused material valued at $8.40 was returned to the stores. Find the gross cost of the job.

Machine Rate Method 

Under this method a rate per hour of use is fixed upon for each machine, this rate being large enough to include all of the overhead charges.

To determine this rate, many elements must be considered; as, (a) Space occupied; (b) value; (c) rate of depreciation from wear and tear; (d) amount spent for repairs; (e) power used; (f) light and heat used; (g) oil and other supplies consumed; (h) indirect labor required; (i) supervision required; (j) time unused; and so on. These elements must be considered for each machine, and the cost items for each machine considered in their relation to the total cost of similar items for all machines. 

WRITTEN 

1. Miller and Long operate a machine shop containing four machines exactly alike, and a work bench, each occupying the same space. Their overhead charges for one year were: Rent, $480; insurance, $80; depreciation, $240; repairs, $100; power, light, and heat, $275; taxes, $50; general expense, $750 On rent and on general expense the work bench bears its fifth of the total burden,

COSTS 

but on the other items the cost of operating the work bench is only of the cost of operating a single machine. What is the cost per hour to operate one machine, and to maintain the work bench, if all are used full time 260 days per year and 8 hours per day?

Departmental Costs 

The Gross Cost of each unit of manufacture is ascertained by dividing the general expenses among the departments, then

DEPARTMENTAL COSTS 

247 dividing each departmental overhead, including its share of general expense, among the units of manufacture in the department, and adding this total overhead to the prime cost per unit.
