UNIT REVIEW Unit Mastery Test XII 

(Time, 35 minutes) 255 Arrange your work clearly on your answer paper. Number the answers to correspond to the numbering in the test. 

1. The Mutual Trading Company began business with a stock of goods valued at $27,390.46; during the year its net purchases amounted to $89,392.16; its net sales during the same period amounted to $147,362.29; and it closed business with an inventory of $31,562.90. What per cent on the net cost of sales was the gross trading profit? 

2. A manufacturer listed a piece of machinery in a catalog at $75. He now allows discounts of 25, 20, and 10% from the list price and still makes a profit of 50% upon the cost of production. 

What does it cost him to produce this article? 

3. A grinding machine was priced at a profit of 20% of the cost. If the profit amounted to $50.90, what was the selling price of the machine? 

4. Two horses were sold for $250 each. On one a profit of 

25% of cost was made and on the other 25% of cost was lost. 

What was the net gain or loss and the rate of gain or loss? 

5. At what price each must I mark goods that were purchased at $25 per dozen, that I may allow a discount of 20% from marked price and still gain 20% on cost? 

6. A man sold a house at a profit of 20% of cost. With the money received from this sale he purchased another house, which he later sold at a loss of 5% of cost. If his profit on the two transactions amounted to $562.80, what was the cost of the first house and the selling price of the second? 

7. Pens bought at 75¢ per gross are retailed at 6 for 5¢. What is the per cent of profit on cost? 

8. The profit and loss statement of a business, contained the following: Gross profit from trading, $4673.18; income from rentals, $750; selling expenses, $1321.60; general expenses, $2259.82. Find the net profit of the business. The expenses were what per cent of the gross trading profit?

UNIT REVIEW Thought Test VII 

 1. If you knew the cost of a dynamo in the power house and the length of time it has been in use, what other item or items of information would you need in order to compute its present value? 

2. If you know the price at which a certain article was sold and the fact that it was sold at a given per cent of loss based on the cost, how can you find the cost? 

3. If you know the price per ton your dealer charges you for coal delivered in your bin and the incidental expenses he has for freight, delivery; etc., what additional information do you need in order to compute the dealer's rate per cent of profit, based on gross cost? 

4. If there is a difference between a single discount of 20% and two successive discounts of 10% each, explain why there is a difference and show how much it is on a sale of a given amount. 

If there is no difference, state that as your opinion and prove it by an example. 

5. If you know the total amount of sales made by Rogers 

Bros. in their lumber business last year and know that this total represents an increase of a certain per cent over the preceding year, how can you compute the volume of their sales in the preceding year? 

6. Having at hand all the necessary items of information, how can you compute the total cost of a single unit of manufacture in a factory? Give items and explain procedure. 

7. Using the "direct labor hour method" what items of information must you have at hand in order to compute the overhead load that should enter into the cost of a certain job of work coming from the production department of an industrial corporation? Having these items of information, what arithmetical procedure will you follow? 

8. A dealer bought a box of glass marbles at a price equivalent to 96¢ a gross. He wishes to sell them at a certain number for 5¢. How can he find how many he can sell for 5¢ and make a profit of 50% on the cost?

Unit Eight FINANCING AND INVESTMENTS 

In this interesting Unit we take up the question of how money and credit operate in helping men to increase their wealth. We see something of the nature and value of banking, the institution that the financial world has developed as an agency by means of which the problems of finance can be met and credit can function. We also study briefly some of the ways in which money and credit function in the investments the people make. A broad knowledge of these questions will help you not only in your direct relation to business life but also in your discharge of the responsibilities of a citizen.

Write your answers to the questions in the Preview Test and make a list of the topics in which you are already well prepared and a list of those containing work that presents to you some difficulties. Follow these two lists carefully as you proceed in the work of the Unit from day to day. 257

WRITTEN TEST Preview Test in Financing and Investments 

Make your work sheet show the process by which you solve each problem. 

Number your answers to correspond to the numbers in the test. 

1. Compute the interest on $1,000 at 4% compounded semiannually for two years. What is the amount of principal and interest? 

2. A bank has made loans amounting to $2,450,000. In making a statement of the financial condition of the bank, should these loans be entered as resources or as liabilities? Why? 

3. If a bank that is capitalized at $2,000,000 has deposits amounting to $6,000,000 and has made loans for the equivalent of 75% of the deposits at an average rate of 5% per annum, what gross rate of income on the capital of the bank do these loans produce? 

4. What is the difference between paying an account by sending your personal check and paying it by sending a bank draft? 

5. What steps could you take through your banker to collect a delinquent account of a customer in another city? 

6. Explain one process by which you could pay an account in Berlin through your banker in America without actually shipping the money. 

7. A real estate improvement corporation owes a debt of $40,000 on an apartment building. The rate of interest on the debt is 5%, payable annually, and payment of the principal will be due in ten years. What will be the annual interest payment?

What sum of money should the company place at compound interest a 5%, compounded annually, to accumulate a sinking fund sufficient to meet the debt at maturity? (Refer to compound interest tables in solving this problem.) What sum of money should this company's budget set aside annually to cover the two items of the annual interest and the sinking fund appropriation? 

8. What is meant by "amortization of debts"?

CHAPTER XXI COMPOUND INTEREST 

Compound interest is interest computed at stated intervals upon principal and unpaid interest.

The interest periods, or periods of time at the end of which interest is computed, are usually annual, semi-annual, or quarterly, though other periods may be used.

Under a strict construction of the term "compound interest," it is assumed that interest accruals are credited at regular intervals, but that no interest is paid until the end of the time for which the obligation draws compound interest. The compound interest tables are all compiled upon this basis. 

Payments of part of the interest might be made, leaving a balance of unpaid interest to itself draw interest, but problems involving such transactions are not strictly within the range of compound interest as generally understood. 

Compound interest is generally paid by savings banks and other institutions accepting deposits of a reasonably permanent character.

There is no law against the payment of compound interest by the party who owes, but generally speaking the law will not lend its aid to the collection of compound interest. In most states penalties are imposed for attempting to collect it. 

EXAMPLE. What will $500 amount to in 2 yr. 4 mo., at 6% interest compounded semi-annually? Principal..... 

Int. for 6 mo. at 6%....... 

Int. for 2d 6 mo. at 6%..... 

Int. for 3d 6 mo. at 6%.... 

Int. for 4th 6 mo. at 6%...... 

Int. for last 4 mo. at 6%.... 

Amt. at end of 2 yr. 4 mo.. $500.00 15.00 $515.00 15.45 $530.45 15.91 $546.36 16.39 $562.75 11.26 $574.01 

The interest is the difference between the amount and the original principal. In this case $574.01-$500. = $74.01, compound interest. 259

COMPOUND INTEREST 

WRITTEN. Find the amounts in the following problems without the use of the table: 

1. $150 at 4% compounded quarterly for 2 yr. 

2. $300 at 5% compounded semi-annually for 1 yr. 6 mo. 

3. $500 at 5% compounded annually from Jan. 1, 1916, to July 1, 1919. 

4. $3000 at 8% compounded semi-annually from Mar. 16, 1918, to Dec. 5, 1921. 

5. George deposited $200 in a savings bank on Jan. 1, 1918, at 4% interest compounded semi-annually. How much did he withdraw if he took it all out on July 1, 1919? 

6. What will $1500 amount to in two years with interest at 6% compounded quarterly?

How to Use the Compound Interest Table 

First find the amount of $1.00 for the given number of periods, at the rate of interest for the period. Then multiply by the number of dollars in the principal.

If the interest is compounded annually, the number of periods (at left) is the number of years in the time, and the rate (at top) is the annual rate of interest.

If the interest is compounded semi-annually, the number of periods (at left) is double the number of years in the time, and the rate (at top) is half the annual rate of interest.

If the interest is compounded quarterly, the number of periods (at left) is four times the number of years in the time, and the rate (at top) is one fourth the annual rate of interest. 

If the number of periods exceeds 50, find the amount for 50 periods and with this as a new principal find the amount for the remainder of the time.

Problems in Use of the Table 

1. Find the interest on $570 at 8% per annum compounded quarterly for 6 years. 

2. What will be the amount of $2000 at the end of 10 years if interest is compounded semi-annually at 5% per annum? 

3. Find the interest on $10,000 at 4% compounded annually for 4 yr. 2 mo. 15 da.

CHAPTER XXII PARTIAL PAYMENTS 

A partial payment is a payment of a part of a note, account, or other obligation.

When a partial payment of a written obligation is made, the memorandum of the payment should also be in writing, usually on the back of the paper evidencing the original debt. Thus, the following might be indorsed on the back of a note by the creditor: 

Recd. $500.00 on this note 

Apr. 2, 1919. J. H. Williams.

The debtor should see that such indorsement is made in his presence at the time he makes the payment.

When a payment is made on an interest-bearing obligation the question arises whether it is to be applied to the payment of interest or of principal. In the absence of agreement between the parties as to how a payment shall be applied, use the following rule: 

United States Rule

The Supreme Court of the United States has held that when a partial payment is made upon an interest-bearing debt, the payment must be applied first to discharge the accrued interest. 

What remains of the payment must be applied upon the principal if the payment is larger than the accrued interest at the time the payment is made. If the payment is smaller than the accrued interest, the previous principal, and that only, continues to draw interest.

The object of this rule is to prevent compound interest. 

This rule, commonly called the United States Rule, is now the law in a majority of the states, having been adopted either by statute or by court decision. 

EXAMPLE. A note dated March 1, 1927, for $1800, with interest at 6%, had the following payments indorsed upon it: Sept. 27, 1927, $500; Mar. 15, 1928, $25; June 1, 1928, $700. What amount should be paid to discharge the note on Sept. 1, 1928? 267

PARTIAL PAYMENTS 

$1800.00 

Interest on $1800 at 6%, Mar. 1 to Sept. 27 (6 mo. 

26 da.). 61.80 

Amount of obligation Sept. 27, 1927... $1861.80 

Payment on Sept. 27, 1927. 

Balance of obligation after 1st payment.. [Int. on $1361.80 at 6% Sept. 27, 1927, to Mar. 15, 1928 (5 mo. 18 da.), was $38.13, but the payment of $25.00 did not cover this.] 

Int. on $1361.80 at 6% Sept. 27, 1927, to June 1, 1928 500.00 $1361.80 (8 mo. 4 da.).. 

Amount of obligation on June 1, 1928. 55.38 $1417.18 

Payment Mar. 15, 1928-$25.00 

Payment June 1, 1928 -700.00 $ 725.00

Amount due on Sept. 1, 1928...

Sometimes when a partial payment is made the old note is destroyed and a new note issued for the balance then due. This is a simple method, but is objectionable for two reasons: First, because all transactions relating to the obligation are not evidenced on the same paper; second, because whenever the payment is less than the accrued interest the purpose of the U. S. rule (to prevent compounding) is defeated.

Mortgage notes usually bear coupons for interest due. Each coupon bears its own due date, and each interest item bears simple interest from its date. 

When by the terms of the loan interest items are payable on specified dates, and bear simple interest if unpaid, it is customary to allow the borrower interest on any payments made before any interest date. 

The rules for figuring partial payments apply to partial payments on noninterest-bearing notes after maturity, as well as to interest-bearing notes at any time.

PARTIAL PAYMENTS Merchants' Rule in Partial Payments The Merchants' Rule is: Find the amount of the note or other obliga tion, including interest to the date of final payment. Then find the amounts due on the various partial payments, including interest, from the time each was made until the date of final payment, and find the total of these. The difference between the two totals is the amount due. 

Turn to the illustrative example under Partial Payments, United States Rule, and solve that example by the merchants' rule stated above. What is the difference in result, and why?

Installments 

Houses, lots, farms, and other properties are frequently paid for by installments, or partial payments, after an initial payment has been made.

In purchasing property on this plan the balance of the principal, after the first payment, is usually interest-bearing.

NATIONAL BANKS IN THE UNITED STATES 1914 TO 1933 

Functions of a Bank

A bank is a corporation organized for the purpose of dealing in money and the various forms of its equivalent in negotiable paper, receiving deposits, paying out money as authorized, making loans, and issuing currency. National banks operate under 

Federal law and are regulated by the Federal government. State banks are chartered by the states in which they are located. 

The various forms of banking activity are regulated by laws defining the conditions under which each activity may be carried on. Some of the ways in which banks serve the business interests of their communities have been described in Chapter II. 

The Federal Reserve System of banks constitutes the principal agency by which the Federal government makes its functional connection with private banking in the United States. 

All the national banks and many state banks are members of this system. Through its activities credit may be expanded or restricted in accordance with the fluctuating requirements of trade.

NO. OF BANKS 

7161 1915 1916 1917 1918 6161 1920 1921 

CHAPTER XXIII BANKS AND BANKING

BANKS AND BANKING 

The capital of a bank is obtained by selling shares of capital stock, as in other chartered corporations. The funds needed in conducting a bank consist of the proceeds arising from the sale of shares of its stock and from using part of the deposits as loans. 

From time to time, banks are required to publish statements showing their resources and liabilities. The resources show what the banks have with which to meet their obligations. The liabilities show what those obligations are.

The following is a condensed form of a statement made by a bank to show its condition on a given date:

Give the result to the nearest tenth of one per cent.) (b) What is the approximate ratio of Deposits to the total of 

Capital Stock, Surplus, and Undivided Profits? 

Under a law enacted by Congress in 1933, bank deposits are insured against loss arising from the closing of banks. All deposits not in excess of $5000 are covered by this insurance. It gives protection to a large per cent of all the depositors in banks in the United States.

From available funds, not in excess of a specified per cent of the deposits, a bank may make loans to responsible parties. The bank collects the interest in advance on such loans. Interest collected in this manner is called bank discount. Because bank discount is one of the chief sources of income for the bank, the subject has special importance and is treated in a separate chapter in this book.

BANKING PROBLEMS Banks of Issue 

275 National banks have authority to issue bank notes which circulate as currency. The amount of currency issued by a bank is limited by Federal law, and must be protected by United States bonds deposited as security with the national treasury.

Banking Problems 

1. If 80% of the deposits shown in the bank statement on the preceding page are used in loans at 5% interest, what gross rate of income on the capital stock of the bank will this produce? 

2. A bank is capitalized at $200,000. Its loans and discounts amount to $315,000, and it owns bonds and securities to the amount of $24,000. On loans and discounts this bank averages 

3% net earnings, and on its bonds and securities 21% net. The income from these investments is what per cent of the capital? 

3. A bank owns $50,000 of registered U. S. bonds on which it receives 21% interest. These bonds are on deposit with the United 

States treasurer to secure circulation and the treasurer has issued $50,000 in national bank notes in exchange for them. Of these bank notes $40,000 are in circulation and subject to an annual tax of 1%, and are in use as loans bearing an average rate of 4% interest. What is the bank's profit on its original investment of $50,000 in the bonds? 

Savings Banks and Departments

Some banks, called savings banks, confine their deposit business to savings accounts. Most other banks have savings departments in which savings deposits are kept. The deposits in a savings department or a savings bank draw interest, if they are undisturbed throughout an interest bearing period, usually one half year. 

The following rules governing interest on deposits are followed by most savings banks, with slight local modifications: 

RULES GOVERNING INTEREST ON SAVINGS DEPOSITS 

1. On deposits made during the first ten days of January and July, and during the first five days of any other month, interest will be allowed from the first day of the month in which such deposit is made. On deposits made after the above named days of each month, interest will be allowed from the first day of the month following the deposit.

BANKS AND BANKING 

2. Interest will not be allowed on the average balances, nor for parts of months, nor for parts of dollars, nor on sums withdrawn between January 1 and June 30, or between July 1 and December 31. In computing interest, withdrawals between these periods will be deducted from the first deposits. 

Note that it is possible under these rules to have a large amount in the bank at all times and still be entitled to no interest. 

EXAMPLE. On Jan. 6, 1933, H. D. Avery deposited $100 in a savings bank at 4% interest, compounded semi-annually. On 

Mar. 7, 1933, he deposited $150.00. On Aug. 5, 1933, he deposited $75. On Oct. 26, 1933, he withdrew $45.50. On Feb. 8, 1934, he deposited $70.25. He withdrew the balance due him June 1, 1934. 

How much did he withdraw?

DATE DEPOSITS INTEREST WITHDRAWALS BALANCE

EXPLANATION. On July 1, 1933, Avery is credited with interest on $100.00 for 6 mo., and on $150 for 3 mo. (not including March). On Jan. 1, 1934, he is credited with interest on $253.50-$45.50 for 6 mo. (Note that the $45.50 is deducted from the July 1 balance rather than from the deposit made Aug. 5), and on $75 for 5 mo. The balance being withdrawn on June 1, no further interest is credited.

Had Avery allowed his savings to remain on deposit one more month he would have been entitled to interest on $288 (no interest on the 41¢) for 6 mo. and on $70 for 4 mo.

BANKS AND BANKING Clearing House Problems 

Banks located in the same town usually meet daily, through their representatives, for the exchange of checks received by each upon the others. The organization is called the Clearing House. 

The exchanging of checks is called "Clearing." 

Although the original and principal purpose of the Clearing House is the clearing of checks, this is by no means the sole purpose of the organization. 

The members of the Clearing House usually agree on certain policies, exchange information, and pass rules governing the policies of member banks, especially in their relations to each other. 

In the course of its business, a bank receives from its depositors or cashes for its customers many checks upon other banks which are members of the same clearing house. 

Each bank takes to the Clearing House, at a certain time each day, all checks held by it against member banks. These are all turned in to the Clearing House for credit. The Clearing House officers then proceed to charge each bank with all checks against it turned in by the other banks. Finally, the banks against which there are more charges than credits pay to the Clearing House officers the excess of the charges, and the Clearing House officers proceed to pay out the total so received to the banks whose credit exceed the charges.

CHAPTER XXIV EXCHANGE 

Exchange is a general term applied to various methods of making remittance from one place, city, or country to another without the transfer of money itself.

Domestic or inland exchange is exchange between points within this country. Foreign exchange is exchange between this country and another. A foreign draft is called a "bill of exchange." 

The mediums of domestic exchange principally used are checks, postal money orders, express money orders, and bank drafts. 

The fee for the collection of a check or for the issuance of a money order or bank draft, is also called exchange. 

Check. Banks will generally receive for deposit without charge checks on other banks with which they have reasonably direct connections, but exchange fees are usually charged on checks drawn on out-of-town banks, especially when this necessitates special correspondence.

Postal Money Order. The remitter pays to the postmaster the sum of money which he wishes to remit, plus an exchange fee, and receives in return an order upon the postmaster at the point to which the remittance is to be sent, for the amount of money to be remitted. He sends this order to the person to whom he is making remittance, who cashes it at his city. 

RATES OF EXCHANGE ON POSTAL MONEY ORDERS 

For $2.50 or less... 6¢ $20.01 to $40.00.. 15¢ 

2.51 to $5.00. 8¢ 

40.01 to 60.00. 18¢ 

5.01 to 10.00.. 

10.01 to 20.00.. 11¢ 

60.01 to 80.00. .20¢ 13¢ 

80.01 to 100.00.. .22¢ 

Express Money Order. This is an order issued by one office of an express company upon another office of the same company. 

The procedure followed in remitting by express money order is similar to that followed in remitting by postal money order. The 280

Remittance by Telegraph. The procedure in remitting by telegraph is the same as that in remitting by postal money order except that the remitter does not receive the order and send it. 

The telegraph company wires instructions to its manager at the point to which the money is to go, or to some bank there, and the money is paid at once to the party designated.

Bank Draft. This is a draft by one bank upon another. The procedure followed in remitting is similar to that followed in remitting by postal money order. The bank from which the draft is purchased may or may not charge a fee, depending upon its relations with the buyer of the draft. 

Money orders and bank drafts are not different from personal checks, as far as the buyer and payee are concerned. But from the standpoint of drawer and drawee there is this distinction: A check is always supposed to be drawn against a balance sufficient to cover it, but this is not true of a money order or a draft, these being paid upon demand, by pre-arrangement between the drawer and the drawee bank, adjustment between the banks being made later. 

Domestic Exchange. By means of domestic exchange trans fers of credit are made for the payment of debt between persons living in different cities in the same country, without sending the money from one city to the other. A service fee is usually charged by banks for making collections or transferring credits in this manner.

EXCHANGE 

Formerly the banks charged a premium or granted a discount on domestic exchange between two banking centers. The custom resulted from the fact that on a given date more credit was moving in one direction between the two cities than was moving in the opposite direction. This difference created a stronger demand for exchange in one city than in the other, and it was met by charging a premium, as stated. If the amount of credit moving in both directions on a given date was equal, exchange was at par. 

Domestic exchange is practically at par everywhere in the United States under present conditions, because the Federal Reserve banks act as equalizers of credit throughout the nation and member banks in the system make their clearance of credits principally through that channel. This function of the Federal 

Reserve system is similar to the function of the local clearing house association. It serves as a clearing agency for the member banks in all cities in the United States in much the same way as the local clearing house serves the group of banks in its city. 

Most of the domestic exchange is carried on by the banks by using banks in New York, Chicago, or San Francisco as centers of exchange. Exchange may be written on banks in other cities, however, and this is often done if the bank where a draft is issued and the bank where it is to be paid are comparatively near together.

If a man wishes to pay an account in a city where his personal check can not be used without a fee because it is an "outof-town" check, he may use a bank draft for this purpose, as 

John Gibbs did in the example below.

The Farmers' and Merchants' Bank 

Millersburg, Ohio 

July 1, 1934. 

Pay to the Order of John Gibbs $125.00 

One Hundred Twenty-five Dollars 

To Central National Bank, Louis Miley 

Cleveland, Ohio Cashier

BANK DRAFT

FOREIGN EXCHANGE 

283 John Gibbs is paying the Cleveland Milling Co. a bill amounting to $125 for mill feed. Since he has an account with the 

Millersburg bank, he is not charged a fee for drafts which he may wish to buy at this bank. 

1. Of what bank is Louis Miley cashier? 

2. Who is ordered to pay $125? Who pays $125 to the 

Millersburg bank?

Foreign Exchange. The mediums most used in foreign ex change are International Postal Money Orders, Letters of Credit, Travelers' Checks, Cable Transfers, and Bills of Exchange. 

International Postal Money Orders are similar in nature to domestic postal money orders, but payable in certain foreign countries where, by treaty with our country, they will be honored. 

Letters of Credit. A letter of credit is a circular letter issued by a bank in this country, enabling its holder to secure currency

EXCHANGE 

at various times and places, up to the total amount specified in it. It is addressed to a number of foreign banks with which the bank of issue has business connections, and instructs them to pay to the holder of it, on demand, such amounts as he may require, up to a specified total. Each partial payment is endorsed on the letter. A commission of 1% is charged by the bank of issue, and each payment is made at the rate of exchange on the city of issue at the time and place of such payments. 

Travelers' checks are checks issued by express companies and by many banks in fixed denominations of $10, $20, $50, etc., to be used by travelers for meeting expenses arising en route. Each check is detached and surrendered as it is cashed. A small commission is charged by the company issuing the checks. Books of travelers' checks are sold for use in traveling in the United

States and also for the convenience of persons traveling in foreign countries. Identification of the person using these checks is made by means of his signature. 

Cable transfers are accomplished in much the same way as telegraphic transfers of domestic exchange. The cost of sending money by cable includes the cost of the message and the exchange fee. The money is paid at the exchange rate current at the time, in the denominations of the country where payment is made. 

Money, or banking credit, may also be transferred by cable from a bank in the United States to a bank in a foreign country. It can then be used by the owner as needed in payment of expenses, as he might use travelers' checks.

Bills of Exchange. A bill of exchange is a draft drawn in one country and payable in another. It may be either a sight bill or a time bill.

A documentary bill of exchange is one which has some document attached to it, such as a bill of lading, invoice, governmental inspection certificate, or other document necessary to the drawee and possession of which he can get only by payment of the bill. 

The documentary bill of exchange is similar in this respect to a draft with bill of lading attached. A bill that has no document attached is called a 'clean" bill of exchange. 66 Bills of exchange are usually made payable in the currency of the country in which they are payable.

PAR OF EXCHANGE 

285 Par of Exchange. In foreign exchange it is difficult to maintain par of exchange, because the currencies of countries differ in value, and the degree of this difference varies. If you wish to pay an account of £1000 in London by buying exchange on London in New York, you need to know the relative value of the United States dollar and the English pound sterling. This relative value fluctuates on account of the amount of gold represented in a dollar and in one pound sterling. 

The demand for exchange of credit between New York and

London is influenced also by the volume of trade moving in each direction on a given date. If there is a strong demand for exchange on London to pay debts in England or elsewhere in 

Europe, your New York banker may inform you that London exchange is at a premium. This will increase the cost of your draft or bill of exchange.

For many years prior to 1933 the gold content of the American dollar and of the British pound sterling had been kept at a ratio which made the par value of £1 equal to $4.8665. When the gold content of the dollar was changed by law and by executive order in 1933, the relation of the dollar to the pound sterling also changed. On April 3, 1934, the pound was quoted on exchange at $5.185, and only one week earlier it has been quoted at $5.10. 

On February 27, it was $5.065. A fair average for the first half year was $5.1075.

Some fluctuation has taken place in the par of exchange on Paris, Berlin, and other European money centers. The following table shows the quoted values of foreign exchange in various

1. Rogers, a New York importer of woolen goods from England, wished to pay an account of £1000 in London on May 14, 1934. Par of exchange on that date was $5.1075. Allowing nothing for premium, his bill of exchange for £1000 cost him 

1000 x $5.1075=$5,107.50

Formerly the same amount of London exchange cost Rogers 

1000 x $4.8665= $4,866.50

If we consider a man in England wishing to buy exchange on New York to pay for $10,000 worth of cotton, the situation is reversed. 

2. Benson, a Manchester manufacturer, goes to his banker to buy exchange on New York for $10,000 to pay for cotton he has bought. Exchange on the date being as stated in problem 1 above he must pay as many pounds sterling as the quotient of $10,000 $5.1075-1957.905

Formerly the same amount of New York exchange cost Benson as many pounds sterling as the quotient of $10,000 $4.8665-2054.8649

Under the changed condition of par Benson has saved in his payment the difference between £2054.8649 and £1957.905, or £96.9599, which is £96 19s. 2d.

From the foregoing we can see that when our dollar has a lower gold content than it formerly had in relation to foreign currency, it is more expensive for us to buy goods in foreign markets; but it is easier for us to sell our surplus cotton and other products in Europe, because the European purchaser is able to buy more with a given amount of his money.

CHAPTER XXV STOCKS 

A corporation is an organization formed under authority of the state, endowed with a continuous legal existence irrespective of that of the individuals composing it at any time, and authorized to transact business as a single individual. 

The capital stock of a corporation is the amount authorized to be paid in to its treasury for the conduct of its business and the benefit of its creditors.

Capital stock must be distinguished from capital. The capital stock is a fixed amount prescribed by the charter. The capital is the amount of net assets on hand at any time. The first business transaction of the corporation may occasion a profit (or a loss), and thereupon the capital will be more (or less) than the capital stock.

A CERTIFICATE OF STOCK 

Shares. The capital stock of a corporation is divided into shares, usually of $100 each. These shares, represented by certificates of stock, are sold to individuals. The owners of shares are known as shareholders, and as such participate in the profits of the corporation. 288

KINDS OF STOCK 

289 The extent of a shareholder's participation depends upon the number and kind of shares which he holds.

Common and Preferred Stock. There are many different kinds of stock issued. The kinds usually issued are common stock and preferred stock. Preferred stock is stock which is sold under an agreement that the holders will be given preference over holders of common stock in the distribution of profits, or of assets, or both. 

Common stock is ordinary stock which carries with it no such preference. "6% preferred" stock means stock that is issued under an agreement that $6.00 a share will be paid to its holders before holders of common stock shall receive any dividends. Cumulative preferred stock is stock sold under an agreement that if at any dividend period the dividend at the agreed rate cannot be paid, the holder shall continue to be entitled to it until it can be paid. Non-participating preferred stock is stock that does not so participate.. 

It frequently occurs, when profits have been large, that greater dividends are paid on common stock than on non-participating preferred stock. 

A dividend is an amount of money paid by a corporation to its stockholders, each stockholder receiving the part to which he is entitled by the number of shares which he holds. 

Dividends are usually either annual, semi-annual, or quarterly. The directors of the corporation decide how much in dividends shall be paid, and when. Dividends must be paid out of profits. It is unlawful to impair the capital of a company by the payment of dividends. 

When a sale of stock is made, the dividend goes to the purchaser unless the sale is made "ex-dividend." This term means "without dividend," the dividend being reserved by the seller.

The par value of stock is its face value. 

The market value of stock is the price at which it can be bought. 

The book value of a share of stock is that part of the net assets of the corporation, as shown by its books, which corresponds to the part of the capital stock which one share represents. 

Since the capital, surplus, and undivided profits represent the net assets of a corporation, the quickest method of finding book value is to divide the total of these three accounts by the number of shares of stock. 

The price of stock may be quoted in dollars per share, as a per cent, or in cents on the dollar. 

Thus, $95.50 per share, 95%, or 95 (meaning 95 cents on the dollar) all mean the same thing, in case of stock at $100 par.

Broker's Fees

A stock broker is one who acts as an agent for others in buying and selling stocks. He charges for this service a fee, usually 

121¢ per share (of 1% of the par value of stock at $100 a share).

Listed Stocks. The stock exchange publishes a list of stocks which may be bought and sold on the floor of the exchange. To get a stock listed, those promoting it must meet certain requirements of the stock exchange as to publication of profits, etc.

Unlisted Stocks. These are stocks which are not listed as above. They may be dealt in by members of the stock exchange, but not on the floor. A separate list of these is published under the head of unlisted stocks. As they may not be dealt in on the floor of the stock exchange, they have come to be called street or "curb'' stocks.

How to Compute the Rate of Income 

In the case of stocks the amount of the dividend on each share is also the income on a share. 

The dividend = par value rate of dividend. 

The rate of income is the per cent which the income is of the purchase price (market value at the time the stock was bought).

This formula is helpful in solving certain stock problems. 

Par value rate of dividend=market value × rate of income. 

PROBLEM.—When 6% stock is selling at 120, find the rate of income. $6 is the income produced by each share. To get this income the buyer paid $120. The problem then becomes an easy one in percentage. "$6 is what per cent of $120?'' 

PROOF.-$100 (Par) × 6% (Rate of dividend) (Rate of income). $120 (Market) X 5%

The formula also helps to solve this type of problem: "What may be paid for a 6% stock to make 8% on the purchase price (market value) ?"

SOLUTION.-A 6% stock yields $6 income on each share. The problem then becomes "$6 is 8% of what amount?" $6 is 8% of $75 (market value). 

PROOF.-$100 (Par) × 6% (Rate of dividend) = $75 (Market) × 8% (Rate of income).

CHAPTER XXVI BONDS 

A bond in a financial sense, is a written promise, under seal, to pay a specified sum of money at a fixed time, and to pay interest thereon at a stipulated rate at fixed intervals. 

Broadly speaking, the issuance of bonds is a device for borrowing from many investors small amounts which in the aggregate make a large sum. 

Suppose that X, a corporation, wants to borrow $100,000. It issues 1000 bonds for $100 each, which are sold to the public. 

The bonds in a given issue may be so written that all will mature on the same date; or they may be made to mature in series, becoming due in successive years.

A coupon bond is a bond to which are attached interest coupons, one coupon for each item of interest to be paid. Each coupon shows the date and amount of interest due, and as each falls due it is detached by the bondholder and presented for payment.

A registered bond is one which is payable only to the order of a bondholder specifically named (registered) on the books of the person, firm, or corporation issuing the bonds. 

It may be registered as to principal only, in which case the interest would be represented by coupons payable to bearer; or it may be registered as to principal and interest, in which case the interest as well as the principal would be payable only to the order of the person in whose name it was registered.

It is customary to list a bond in such a way as to show by whom it was issued, when it is due, the rate of interest it bears, and some other important fact or facts. Thus U. S. 48, 1925 registered are 4% registered bonds issued by the United States, payable in 1925. Westinghouse convertible 5s, 1920 coupons, are 5% coupon bonds issued by the Westinghouse Company, payable in 1920 but convertible into stock, if desired, under stipulated conditions. 

First Mortgage bonds are bonds secured by a first mortgage. Refunding bonds are bonds issued to raise money for the redemption of bonds previously issued. Extension bonds are bonds issued to secure money for extensions of properties. Sinking Fund bonds are bonds providing under the terms of their issue, for the creation of a sinking fund for their retirement. 296

PRICES OF BONDS 

297 Debentures are unsecured bonds. Income bonds are bonds which pay interest only if justified by the income of the business for which the bonds were issued. Consolidated bonds (as U. S. Consol. 2s, 1930) are usually bonds issued in place of several previous issues, which have been consolidated in the one issue.

DOLLARS PER BOND 

85 80 75 70 65 50 60 55 1933 1932 1933 1932 50 Jan.

Feb. March April May June July Aug. Sept. Oct. Nov. Dec. 

VARIATIONS IN PRICES OF BONDS, 1932 AND 1933 Market Prices of Bonds 

The prices of bonds fluctuate constantly in response to the demand for opportunities to invest capital in them in comparison with some other forms of investment. Legislation and the value of the monetary unit also influence the prices of corporation bonds, as quoted on the exchanges. Bonds bear a fixed rate of interest, and therefore the desirability of bonds as investments depends largely upon the opportunity to buy them at favorable prices. Study the above graph and see how greatly the prices of bonds fluctuated in 1932 and 1933. 

To find the cost of a bond.

Since bonds draw interest, it is understood that the purchaser must pay the quoted price plus interest, accrued since the last payment of interest. 

Thus, in case of a $100.00 bond, interest payable Jan. 1 and July 1, and sold on Mar. 1 at 973, the price would be found by adding to $97.50 the interest on the par value of the bond ($100.00) for two months.

BONDS

88 and interest, brokerage. If interest is payable Jan. 1 and 

July 1, what did they cost? $5000 × (.883.00) = $4450. 

Int. on $5000 July 1 to Sept. 10 (2 mo. 9 da.) = $43.13. $4450+$43.13=$4493.13, cost.

To find the present value of a bond. 

EXAMPLE. What can I afford to pay for a 6% bond, par value $100.00, interest payable semi-annually, if it matures in 15 years, to earn 4%?

To find the present value of a bond, two elements must be considered: (1) The relation of the rate of interest on the bond to the rate it is desired to earn; (2) the time the bond has to run. 

If the first element only were considered, I would be willing to pay $150 for such a bond ($3.00.02=$150) because the interest on $150 at 4% for a half year ($3.00) is the same as the interest on the bond for the same period (3% of $100-$3.00).

But suppose I should pay $150. In 15 years the bond would be redeemed for $100 and I should lose $50.00. It is evident that I must pay less than $150, by an amount which in 15 years will amount to $50, at 4% interest compounded semi-annually (semi-annual interest is used for consistency, since the interest on the bonds is semi-annual). The compound interest tables show that $1.00 will amount, compounded 31 times at 2%, to $1.84758882. $50÷1.84758882=$27.06. $150-$27.06=$122.94, the present value of the bond.

BOND TABLES 

299 NOTE. The present value of a bond is the total of the present worths of all future returns of interest and principal. Calculations may be made from present worth tables.

NOTE. The present value, plus compound interest to maturity at the rate of earning desired, must equal the total of all returns, each plus interest at the rate the bond bears, from the time it is made until maturity. Compound interest tables are needed to make this calculation.

Bond Tables. To facilitate calculations of bond values, tables are used, of which a specimen is shown herewith. 

Since interest on bonds is usually payable semi-annually, these tables are compiled on this basis.

CHAPTER XXVII APPLICATIONS OF COMPOUND INTEREST 

Annuities

An annuity is an amount of money payable at stated intervals (usually yearly) through a given number of years.*

An insurance company, upon the death of B, who was insured for $10,000, offered to pay his widow $2310 a year for five years. This payment of $2310 a year was an annuity.

The word annuity is loosely used to mean any sort of an annual payment, as a pension, an allowance, or a contribution to a sinking fund. Its use here, however, is confined strictly to equal periodical installments necessary to retire an interest-bearing debt.

NOTE. Sinking funds, as hereafter more fully treated, usually are created for the retirement at some future date of a debt of a specified amount, which is either non-interest bearing or the interest on which is paid from year to year so that the principal does not increase.

EXAMPLE. An insurance company owes Mrs. B. $6000. Instead of paying this at once the company offers to make five equal annual payments, the first payable at once. What is the amount of this annuity, on a 5% basis?

The last payment will be made in 4 years. 

Installments plus interest must equal debt plus interest. $6000. at 5% compounded annually for 4 years will amount to (see compound interest table) 6000×1.2155063=$7293.04.

Installments of $1.00 a year paid annually for 5 years will at the end of 4 yr., at 5% compounded annually, amount to $4.525631 (see compound interest amount table). Adding $1.00 paid at beginning of 5th yr. gives $5.525631. $7293.04÷5.525631 = $1319.86, amount of annuity. 

EXAMPLE. I borrowed $6000 for 5 yr., at 5%. I desire to pay this back in five payments. What annual payment is required? 

The first payment will be made in one year; the last, in five years. $6000. at 5% compounded annually for 5 years will amount to (see compound interest table) 6000×$1.2762816= $7657.69

Installments of $1.00 a year paid annually for 4 years (one each at beginning of 2d, 3d, 4th, and 5th yr.) will amount to $4.525631 (see compound interest amount table). Adding $1.00 paid at end of 5th yr. gives... .$5.525631 $7657.69÷5.525631-$1385.85, amount of annuity. * The number of years, in an insurance annuity, is often the number of years shown by the table of mortality to be the beneficiary's expectation of life. 301

SINKING FUNDS 

Sinking Funds 303 A sinking fund is the amount accumulated by setting aside a sum of money each year for a fixed period of years, so that, with compound interest, the sums set aside will amount to a certain debt at the end of the fixed period of time. 

Naturally those who lend money wish assurance of its repayment. The creation of a sinking fund to pay the debt at maturity gives them this assurance. 

This is of importance to the borrower also, as the sinking fund is a guaranty against the eventual impairment of capital.

The amount of debt is paid to the creditor at maturity. The debt itself is usually no larger at maturity than at its origin, as interest has probably been paid periodically throughout its life.

The amount of the annual contribution is frequently spoken of as a sinking fund, but this is not strictly correct, as the sinking fund is the result of these contributions and their accumulations of interest. 

EXAMPLE. The Sprague Water Power Co. has issued $1,000,000 of 51% bonds due in 5 years. What amount should the company annually set aside in a sinking fund at 5% to equal the principal debt at maturity?

Naturally no appropriation to the sinking fund is made at the beginning of the first year. The presumption must be that the money borrowed was all needed. The first appropriation was made at the beginning of the second year. 

Two methods of solution are given below. Study both carefully and use the method which seems the easier to you.

Using Compound Interest Amount Table 

$1.00 invested at the beginning of each year for 4 years (the 2d, 3d, 4th, and 

5th year of the loan) at 5% amounts to $4.525631.

Adding $1.00 appropriated at the end of the 5th year of the loan gives $5.525631. $1,000,000÷5.525631=$180,974.82, amount of annual appropriation.

Using Compound Interest Tables 

Each $1.00 of 1st payment compounded for 4 yr. 

Each 1.00 of 2d payment compounded for 3 yr. 

Each 1.00 of 3d payment compounded for 2 yr. 

Each 1.00 of 4th payment compounded for 1 yr. 

Each 1.00 of 5th payment compounded for 0 yr...

For each $1.00 paid annually for 5 years, the amount of the debt cancelled will be.. $1,000,000÷$5.525631 = $180,974.82, amount of the payment. $1.215506 1.157625 1.1025 1.05 1.00 $5.525631

APPLICATIONS OF COMPOUND INTEREST 

It is the custom for corporations, states, and cities to buy back their own bonds with their sinking fund annuities when the interest rate on the bonds they wish to retire is higher than can be realized on the sinking fund securities and the bonds can be purchased for an amount near par. 

WRITTEN 

1. The Eastern Construction Co. issued $100,000 in bonds payable in 20 years, and created a sinking fund to pay the bonds at maturity. On a 4% basis, how large an annual appropriation to the sinking fund was made? 

2. A railroad company borrows $1,000,000 by issuing sinking fund bonds payable in 15 years. If it can net 4% on its sinking fund investments, how large an annual appropriation should be made? 

3. A city issued $350,000 of 20-year bonds to raise money for constructing a municipal electric lighting plant. If the city can realize 4% on its sinking fund securities, how much must be the annual payment to the fund? 

4. A corporation, trying to borrow money on its sinking fund bonds, advertises that it will set aside each year $6,000.00 for this fund. The bonds it is trying to sell amount to $250,000, and are payable in 20 years. How much more or less than sufficient to pay this indebtedness in 20 years will be the fund so established if the corporation can realize 5% upon its investments? 

5. Harding leased a lot for 20 years at an annual rental of $2500. 

He erected on it a building at a cost of $50,000. What must be the annual income from the building in order to cover: (a) The annual rent of the land; (b) interest of 8% per annum on the cash investment; and (c) an annual appropriation to a sinking fund which at 4% would equal the cost of the building at the expiration of the land lease? 

6. A city issued $250,000 of 20 yr., 5% bonds to erect a city hospital. It was agreed that the city should provide a sinking fund at 3% sufficient to retire the entire issue at maturity. How much did the city appropriate annually? If the taxable value of all property within the city at the time this issue of bonds was made amounted to $325,560,000, how many mills per dollar of valuation would be necessary to raise the first contribution to the sinking fund?

AMORTIZATION OF DEBTS Amortization of Debts 

305 Amortization is the payment of a debt by a series of equal payments, at regular intervals, the payments covering both principal and interest.

These payments are made to the creditor, and are not mere appropriations. Each payment covers the interest accrued at the time it was made, the balance being applied on principal. Each successive payment covers more principal than the preceding payment, and less interest. 

EXAMPLE. On Apr. 1, 1934, a farmer borrowed $1000 for 4 years at 4%, under an agreement that principal and interest were to be paid in 4 equal payments. How much should the farmer pay back each year?

The question to be decided is, what periodical payment will in 4 years at 

4% equal a debt of $1000 bearing interest at 4%?

Consulting the compound interest amount table we find that $1.00 invested at the beginning of the 2d, 3d, and 4th year (3 payments will amount to $3.246464. This, plus a final payment of $1.00 on which no interest is figured, gives $4.246464 as the amount realized in 4 years from a series of annual investments of $1.00 at 4% compound interest.

Consulting the compound interest table, we find that $1 compounded for 4 periods at 4% amounts to $1.16985856. Multiplying by 1000 gives $1169.85856 as the amount of $1000 in 4 years at 4% compound interest. $1169.858564.246464—$275.49, the annual payment.

The foregoing is proved by the following schedule, which also shows how much of each payment is applied to principal and how much to interest. Accept $275.49 as the correct amount, and work out each step for yourself. 

SCHEDULE OF AMORTIZATION DATE PAYMENT INTEREST ON BALANCE AMORTIZA- TION OF PRINCIPAL BALANCE AFTER EACH PAYMENT 

April 1, 1934. $1000.00

April 1, 1935. 275.49 40.00 235.49 764.51 April 1, 1936. 275.49 30.58 244.91 519.60 April 1, 1937. 275.49 20.78 254.71 264.89

April 1, 1938. 275.49 10.60 264.89 00.00

Notice that the amount applied on the principal increases as the interest decreases.

APPLICATIONS OF COMPOUND INTEREST WRITTEN 

1. A farmer bought 160 acres of land at $90 per acre. He had enough cash to pay one third of the purchase price, and borrowed the remaining two thirds. He agreed to return the loan with interest at 5% in 5 equal annual payments. What should be the amount of each annual payment? 

Prepare a schedule of amortization. 

2. The Vinton Mfg. Co. installed machinery at a cost of $70,000. They paid $20,000 cash and agreed to pay the balance and interest at 6% in 5 equal annual payments. 

Prepare a schedule of amortization. 

3. Samuel Wilkins borrowed $3000, agreeing to pay back the loan and interest at 4% in 10 equal annual payments. He made the first five payments and then sold his property, the purchaser agreeing to assume the unpaid portion of the debt. How much did Mr. Wilkins pay on the principal and how much did the buyer assume? 

Prepare a schedule of amortization. 

4. A farmer borrowed $3000 and agreed to repay the principal and interest at 6% in 10 years by making 20 equal semiannual payments. Find the amount of each payment and prepare a schedule of amortization.

The principal of arithmetic that is used in computing the amortization of debts has various applications in financial transactions. Local taxing units, such as city governments and school districts, often use it in scheduling their annual payments to cancel the debt arising from the issuance of bonds payable in series. This action makes the amortization plan take the place of a sinking fund. The term sinking fund is sometimes loosely used with this meaning, but this use of the term is not strictly accurate.

PUBLIC WORKS FINANCED BY BONDS How Bond Premiums and Discounts Affect the Income Tax 

Premiums and discounts in buying bonds represent losses and gains that should be spread over the years which they cover. 

Thus, if a profit which should cover ten years is entered in the books as of one year, the income tax that year will be too heavy. The principle of amortization may be used to correct such inequalities.

A premium on a bond is the amount by which its market value is in excess of par. As the bond will eventually be redeemed at par, it is clear that the premium which is included in the value of the bond will gradually disappear. This gradual disappear-

APPLICATIONS OF COMPOUND INTEREST 

ance is computed by a plan of amortization which is developed in the two examples following:

Bonds sell at a premium when the rate of interest they bear is greater than the rate of interest which is to be realized from the investment. The longer the life of the bond the greater the premium. 

EXAMPLE. What can a bank afford to pay in cash on Jan. 1, 1934, for a $100,000 issue of 5% bonds, interest payable semiannually, in order to realize upon its investment the equivalent of 4% interest compounded semi-annually, if the bonds are payable in 2 years?

The returns to the bank will be the four interest payments, due in yr., 1 yr., 1 yr., and 2 yr., respectively, and the principal, due in 2 yr. It can afford to pay the present worth of these future returns calculated on a basis of 4% compounded semi-annually. Consulting the compound interest table for our rates, we find:

A discount on a bond is the amount by which its market value is less than its par value. 

Bonds sell at a discount when the rate of discount they bear is less than the rate of interest which is to be realized from the investment. The longer the life of the bond the greater the discount. 

Suppose a bond to be sold at a discount. As it will eventually be redeemed at par, its worth gradually approaches par. It is clear that the discount should not be considered a lump sum discount (or a profit made in one day) but should be distributed uniformly throughout the period covered by the bonds. How this distribution is accomplished through a sinking fund, is developed in the two examples following:

EXAMPLE. On Jan. 1, 1934, the Simplex Manufacturing Co. 

issued $100,000 of 2 yr. 4% bonds, interest payable on Jan. 1 and July 1 of each year. What could the purchaser pay for the issue, to net 6% compounded semi-annually on his investment? 

The returns to the purchaser will be the four interest payments and the payment of principal when due. To net 6% on his investment, he can pay the present worth of these items as follows:

The purchaser's investment is shown above as increased by the amount of each of these sinking fund appropriations until it reaches $100,000, while the discount is shown as decreased by each of these amounts until it reaches $00.00.

GENERAL REVIEW PROBLEMS Conversion of Bonds 

311 Convertible bonds are bonds which may be exchanged, under specified conditions, for stock or for other bonds. The prices at which such conversion will be made are specified, as to both the bonds traded and the stock or bonds traded for. 

Thus, bonds convertible at 95 into stock at 105 means that the bonds will be accepted at 95 and the stock will be sold for 105, when the exchange is made. 

WRITTEN 

1. The Santa Fe Railroad has issued bonds convertible at par into common stock at $110. How much common stock will be received in exchange for $23,100 worth of bonds? 

2. If 5% bonds can be converted at $105 into 6% stock at $110, will the holder of such a bond increase or decrease his income by making the exchange, and how much? 

3. I own bonds of the Goodyear T. and R. Company which are now due and the par value of which is $5000. One condition of the bond is that it is convertible into a like amount of the Company's 6% preferred stock at par, and in addition common stock of the Company of the par value of 30% of the bonds. The preferred stock is selling at 93 and the common at 70. What will be my gain if I convert the bonds and sell the stock, brokerage on the stock 1%? 

4. If the purchaser of the stock held it as an investment, and the preferred paid 6% and the common 4%, what would be his annual income from his investment, including brokerage, and his per cent of income? 

5. Mr. Griffith has $10,000 worth of 5% bonds convertible at market price, 102, into common stock of the same company at par. The common stock is expected to yield 7%. If Mr. Griffith makes the exchange will he increase or decrease his income and how much?

UNIT REVIEW Unit Mastery Test XIV 

(Time, 45 minutes) 313 1. Which is better and how much, $5500 in cash or $1500 at the end of each year for 4 years, if money is worth 4% ? 

2. A woman's husband dies leaving her a life interest in a part of a farm, which pays her annually $900. The heirs owning the remaining interests in the property insist on a sale and the property cannot be divided. If her expectation of life is 6 years, and money is worth 6%, what sum should she be paid for her interest? 

3. Ames has an endowment insurance policy for $1000 payable at once. He also has the option of taking $211.99 in cash and 4 additional payments of a like sum in 1, 2, 3, and 4 years, respectively. If in either case he gets 4% interest compounded semi-annually in a savings bank, which plan is better and how much, at the end of the fourth year? 

4. A man owing $500 due in 6 mo., $700 in 1 yr., $900 in 2 yrs. and $1000 in 3 yrs., all without interest, wishes to settle these debts by making 3 equal payments, in 1, 2, and 3 years, respectively. What must each payment be if money is worth 5% ?

4 years with interest at 5% on deferred payments. How much money does he pay, including interest payments? 

6. A railroad pays $720 a year to each of two watchmen to guard a crossing. How much can it afford to spend for elevating the tracks over that crossing if money is worth 41% ? 

7. A man owes a mortgage, due in 5 yr., on an apartment house. When he renews the mortgage he wishes to reduce the loan $6000. If money is worth 5% what amount must he set aside at the end of each year to equal $6000? 

8. What is a coal mine worth which will yield $10,000 annually for 10 yr. before it is exhausted, if money is worth 5% ? 

9. A man buys a piano for $600. He pays $100 in cash and agrees to pay the balance in 5 bi-monthly payments of $100.00 each, each payment to include 6% on the unpaid balance. What is the amount of each payment?

Unit Nine

HOW THINGS ARE MEASURED 

Practical arithmetic consists largely of computing in the many applications of standards of measurement that are used in life. In Unit 

Nine we study some of the most common of these standards of measurement and learn how they are applied in determining the quantity of a given substance, the dimensions of a limited space, the capacity of boxes and bins, the relative weight of objects, the length of time a machine has been in use, and many other items of information that we need in everyday affairs. The Unit will present to the student many topics that he has had in earlier study of arithmetic, but he will take up these topics in the light of his added experience and broader appreciation of their vocational value. 

A brief Preview Test furnishes the opportunity to check the ability and skill of the class in the work of this Unit. Each student should take this test, and the results should be kept as a basis for providing for differences in individual ability as the study of the Unit progresses. 315

CHAPTER XXVIII PRACTICAL MEASUREMENTS SECTION OF U. S. STANDARD METER 

Fundamental Units of Measurement. The fundamental units of measurement are units of numerical quantity, length, weight, and time. Other units, such as units of value, surface, volume, and capacity, are derived from these fundamental units. Direction is an essential factor in some calculations. 

The fundamental unit of numerical quantity is the number 1. 

All other numerical quantities, integral or fractional, are based upon it. וויי 

U. S. STANDard KilograM 

The fundamental unit of length is the meter. Our foot of 12 inches is .3068 meter. Our linear, square, and cubic measures, are all based upon this fundamental unit.

The fundamental unit of weight is the kilogram. Our pound (Troy) is equivalent to .373267 kilogram. Our various tables of weight are all based upon this fundamental unit. 

The fundamental unit of time is the day.

The solution of the problems of this chapter requires the use of the tables of measurement found in the appendix. All these tables are based directly or indirectly on one of the fundamental units mentioned above. 317

PRACTICAL MEASUREMENTS Denominate Numbers 

Denominate numbers are numbers that are used to indicate a definite quantity of some specific thing; as, 3 hours; $4. 

Compound denominate numbers are denominate numbers that refer to two or more items in the same kind of table of measure; as 3 hr. 45 min.; 6 ft. 9 in.

Reduction of denominate numbers is the process of changing them from the denominations in which they are expressed to other denominations in the table of measures that applies to them. It is often desirable to do this in computing in such numbers. They may be changed to higher or lower denominations. 

A denominate number is changed to a higher denomination by dividing it by the number of units of its order required to make one unit of the higher order to which it is to be changed. 

EXAMPLES: (a) Change 16 qt. to gallons. 

16÷4=4 16 qt.=4 gal. (b) Change ÷12= in. to feet. in.ft. (c) Change 1.6 rd. to miles. 1.6320.005 1.6 rd.-.005 mi. 

A denominate number is changed to a lower denomination by reversing the above process and multiplying the number by the number of units of the desired lower order required to make one unit of the given order.

EXAMPLES: (a) Change $2 to cents. 

100 X2 200 $2=200¢ (b) Change 60×30 hr. to minutes. hr. 30 min. (c) Change 2.5 mi. to rods. 

320 x 2.5800 2.5 mi. 800 rd. 

It is sometimes desirable to use both of the above processes in changing a compound denominate number so as to express it in any one of the series of denominations occurring in its table of measures. 

EXAMPLE: Change 22 rd., 2 yd., 2 ft., 3 in., to yards in decimal form. 22 rd. 

2 yd. 5×22 121 2= 2 2 ft. 3 in. 21 ft. 2.25÷ 3= .75 

22 rd., 2 yd., 2 ft., 3 in. =123.75 yd.

PRACTICAL MEASUREMENTS 

Compound denominate numbers can be added, subtracted, multiplied, and divided, when necessary in working problems. 

If the numbers to be added, subtracted, multiplied, or divided have been reduced to the same denomination in the table of measures that applies to them, and fractional parts have been expressed as decimals, these operations may then be performed in the usual way. Such examples need no further explanation. 

But compound denominate numbers can also be added, subtracted, multiplied, and divided (into parts) without reducing before the operation is performed. The principles of the four fundamental processes are applied in the usual way; but it is necessary to use the scale of the table of measures appropriate to the problem material instead of using the decimal scale, when "carrying" and "borrowing" in the work of computation. Examples of these four kinds of operations in denomiate numbers follow to make the method clear.

In solving problems the method of work should be selected in such a way as to give the student the greatest saving of time and effort. In some problems the numbers that are involved in computations and the tables of measures that apply in solving the problems are such as to make the use of reduction easiest and quickest; in others it is simplest to proceed without first reducing the compound numbers. Another way to divide in denominate numbers is to reduce the entire expression to one denomination and divide. 

Thus in the above case the entire expression could have been written as gallons, or as quarts, or as pints, and then divided by 9. 

277 gal. 3 qt. 1 pt. =1111 qt. 

1111 qt.÷9=123 qt. or 30 gal. 3 qt. 1 pt.

To divide a compound denominate number by another, express both in terms of the same denomination and divide.

CHAPTER XXIX POWERS AND ROOTS OF NUMBERS 

Powers A power of a number is the product obtained by using the number two or more times as a constant factor. 

Thus, 4, 8, and 16 are powers of 2. 4 is the second power of 2 because 2 is used twice as a factor to give 4. 8 is the third power of 2 because 2 is used three times as a factor to give 8.

The second power of a number is called the square of the number. 

The third power of a number is called the cube of the number. 

Consideration of the rules for finding the area of a square and the volume of a cube will make apparent the meanings of the above terms. 

Involution is the process of raising a number to one of its powers. 

The power to which a number is to be raised is often indicated by writing a small figure to the right and slightly above the number to be raised. This small figure is called the exponent. 

Thus, 52 means that 5 is to be squared; 63 means that the cube of 6 is to be found; 24 means that 2 is to be raised to the 4th power; etc.

POWERS AND ROOTS OF NUMBERS 

Roots The root of a number is that number which was used two or more times as a constant factor to give the number. 

Thus 2 is the square root of 4; 2 is the cube root of 8; 2 is the 4th root of 16; 

5 is the square root of 25.

Evolution is the process of finding what number was used as a constant factor to produce a certain power. Evolution is the opposite of involution.

The radical sign is the character (√) placed above a number, considered as a power, to indicate that the root is to be extracted. 

When no number is used within the radical, square root is indicated.

Thus: 256 indicates that the square root is to be found. 

1728 indicates that the cube root is to be found. 

891 indicates that the fourth root is to be found.

A perfect power is a number whose root can be extracted leaving no remainder. 

25 is a perfect square whose root is 5. 

An imperfect power is a number whose root cannot be found exactly. 

10 is an improper power, as its square root is 3.1622+, and no exact cube root or other root can be found. 

To find the square root of a number.

EXAMPLE. What is one side of a square the area of which is 1225 sq. in? 12'25. 30 9.00 30 x 30 

Trial div., 30×2= 60 3 25 3853 30 5 300 52= 25 325 3 25 30 x 5 5x5 1. Point off the number into periods of two places, beginning at the decimal point.

SQUARE ROOT 

327 2. Determine the largest perfect square which may be subtracted from the left hand period (9). Write the root of this square, followed by a cipher or ciphers, as a part of the result (30), and subtract the square of this partial result from the number. 

3. For a trial divisor, multiply by 2 the part of the result already formed (giving 60). Try this (60) into the remainder and add the largest integral number that can be so obtained (5), to the partial result already formed. 

Multiply the trial divisor (60) by this number (5) and to the result add the square of this number (5² = 25). 

4. Deduct from the remainder the total so secured. If the total is larger than the remainder, a smaller number will have to be selected for the second part of the result, and step 3 performed over again. 

If there are more periods, continue the solution by the same process. 

The method of solution shown above is a formula. See if you can reason the process out by consulting this diagram as you follow the steps of the formula. 

When the number of which the square root is to be extracted contains decimals, be sure there are an even number of decimal places, adding a cipher if necessary. Solve as before, pointing off as many decimal places in the root as there are periods in the decimal part of the number.

Thus the square root of 9'.92′25 will have two decimal places. The square root of .00'05'76 will have three decimal places.

To find the square root of a common fraction find the square root of its numerator and of its denominator and use these roots as the numerator and denominator, respectively, of the result.

CHAPTER XXX VOCATIONAL PROBLEMS Areas of Surfaces Preliminary Definitions and Rules 

It is assumed that the student is familiar with most of the terms used in measurements, and that he knows the elementary rules. No attempt is made here to give a complete or connected list. A few definitions that the student may have forgotten, are given. These are accompanied and followed by some rules that will be needed in the work in practical measurements which follows. 

For tables, the student is referred to the appendix. 

A line is perpendicular to another when the two meet at right angles, thus forming two angles of 90° each. 

Thus a vertical and horizontal line are perpendicular to each other. Do not confuse vertical and perpendicular. A line may be perpendicular to another without being vertical.

The hypotenuse of a right triangle is the line which is opposite the right angle.

TRIANGLES EQUILATERAL ISOSCELES SCALENE RIGHT OBTUSE ACUTE EQUIANGULAR 

The perimeter of a surface is the combined length of its sides. 

The area of a rectangle is the product of its length and width. 

The area of a triangle is half the product of its base and altitude. 

The area of a triangle the altitude of which is unknown is found as follows: From half the perimeter subtract each side separately; 328

AREAS OF SURFACES QUADRILATERALS TRAPEZEUM TRAPEZOID RECTANGLE PARALLELOGRAMS SQUARE 

RHOMBOID 

PARALLELOGRAM RHOMBUS 

329 multiply the product of these three results by half the perimeter; extract the square root of the result. 

The square of the hypotenuse of a right triangle is equal to the sum of the squares of the other two sides.

From this we can determine the length of any side of a right triangle if the other two sides are known. 

The circumference of a circle is 3.1416 times the diameter. 

For approximate measurements 34 is sometimes used instead of 3.1416. 

Use 34 only when instructed to do so. 

The area of a circle is 

3.1416 (approx. 34) www the square of the radius, or .7854 (3.1416÷4) X the square of the diameter, or the circumference X one fourth the diameter (or of radius)

The square of the diameter is four times the square of the radius. Therefore if 3.1416X square of radius = area, then .7854 (3.1416÷4) X square of diameter

Again, if 3.1416÷4×D² = area, then 3.1416×D×D÷4=area, then (3.1416×D)×(D÷4)= area, and since (3.1416×D) is the circumference, then circumference X one fourth of diameter = area. area.

Knowing the relations of radius, diameter, circumference, and area, each to the others, any one of them can be found if any other one is known.

Surfaces and Volumes of Solids A prism is a solid the bases of which are equal, similar, parallel figures, and the sides of which are parallelograms. 

The slant height of a regular pyramid or regular cone is the shortest distance from apex to base, measured on the side of the figure (as contrasted with the altitude, or perpendicular height through the body of the figure).

The lateral area of a cylinder is the product of its circumference by its height; of a prism, its perimeter by its height.

VOCATIONAL PROBLEMS SOLIDS 

TRIANGULAR RECTANGULAR PENTAGONAL 

CUBE 

PRISM PRISM PRISM 

CYLINDER SPHERE QUADRANGULAR PRISM PYRAMID 

CONE

FRUSTUM OF PYRAMID FRUSTUM OF CONE 

The lateral area of a regular pyramid is half the product of its slant height and its perimeter; of a regular cone, half its slant height by the circumference of its base. 

The volume of a prism or of a cylinder is the product of its area on one base and its height.

The volume of a pyramid or of a cone is the product of the area of its base, and its altitude. 

The area of the surface of a sphere is four times the square of its radius multiplied by 3.1416.

The volume of a sphere is of the area of its surface multiplied by its radius; or 

X3.1416X the third power of its radius.

The volume of a cylinder is .7854 (approximately, to) times the volume of a cube into which it will exactly fit. The volume of a sphere is approximately that of a cylinder into which it will exactly fit. The volume of a ball is therefore about that of a cubic box into which it will exactly fit.

VOCATIONAL PROBLEMS 

Measuring Lumber

The unit of lumber measure is the board foot, which is 1 foot long, 12 inches wide, and 1 inch thick. A board that is less than 

1 inch thick is measured as though it were 1 inch thick.

Certain kinds of finishing lumber, such as moldings and quarter rounds, are sold by the linear foot, but with these exceptions all lumber is sold by the board foot. 

EXAMPLE. How many board feet in 80 joists each 2″ x 6″ x 18'? 2X6X18X80 12 = 1440 bd. ft. 

To find the number of board feet in one piece 1 inch thick, multiply the length in feet by the width in inches and divide by 12.* To find the number of board feet in 80 pieces 2 in. thick, include the 

80 and the 2 as factors, as shown in the solution given. The problem is solved by cancellation.

PLANNING A ROOF Wood Measure 

Wood in the pile is measured by the cord of 128 cu. ft. The solid contents of the pile, in cu. ft., divided by 128, gives the number of cords.

A convenient plan in measuring cordwood is to think of each cord as being 

HIGH 8 FT. LONG A CORD OF WOOD 

335 

4FT.WIDE 

4' x 4' x 8'. If a pile is 4' high and the pieces are 4' long, its length divided by 

8 is the number of cords in the pile. If the pile is 6' high and the pieces are 4' long, divide the length by 8 and increase the result. And so on.

Planning a Roof 

Gable, Shed, and Gambrel Roofs

A roof is supported by timbers called rafters. These are of various sizes. 

The lower ends of the rafters rest on horizontal timbers, called plates, which lie on the wall or on the posts which support the wall. 

The upper ends rest on the ridgepole. 

32 FT.

GABLE ROOF, 12-16 PITCH 

The slant of a rafter is known as the pitch of the roof. The pitch is the perpendicular distance between the ridge and the plates divided by the width of the building. 

Thus, if the width of a building is 36 ft. and the ridge is 12 ft. higher than the plates, the pitch would be. Carpenters also indicate the pitch of a roof

VOCATIONAL PROBLEMS 

by numbers representing the height of the ridge and half of the width of the building, in feet. Thus the pitch in the preceding case would be given as 12-18. 20 36SHED ROOF GAMBREL ROOF 

In a true gambrel roof the rafters on the lower slant are the same length as the rafters on the upper slant. The upper and lower rafters come together at the purline, which is the board on which the upper rafters rest. There are many variations of the gambrel roof, however.

337 Robert Adams is building a frame house on his farm. The house is oblong in shape, being 28' x 48′ on the foundation with the roof plates 22' from the foundation. Many kinds of work are involved in building a house. A few of these are explained here, and problems are given relating to them. Study the examples that show how the different kinds of building problems are to be solved. Then work the problems that follow these examples. Shingling

Shingles are estimated as having a uniform width of 4 in. They are usually 16 in. long, but their length is not material in figuring the number required to cover a given space, since a large part of the surface of each shingle is 4 in. covered by other shingles, with only 4, 4, 5, or 5½ inches exposed to the weather. 

11½ inches Covered -16 inches- A shingle laid 4 in. to the weather has 4 x 4 in. or 16 sq. in. of exposed surface. It would take 900 of such shingles to exactly cover a square (10 ft. x 10 ft.), but in practical calculations 1000 are estimated, to allow for waste.

Shingles are sold in bundles of 250 each. Only whole bundles can be purchased.

Extra allowance should be made for waste in shingling a hip roof, the opposite edges of which are not parallel.

The amount of exposure depends upon the pitch of the roof-the steeper the roof, the more exposure.

Flooring and Siding

Matched lumber is generally used for flooring. Each board has a tongue along one long edge and a groove along the other long edge. The tongue of one board fits into the groove of the next. 

Siding may be matched, like flooring, or may be lapped, each board lapping over the one below it.

Whether boards are matched together with tongue and groove, or lapped, there is a waste that must be accounted for, for the full width of a board is measured, and some of this width is lost in matching or lapping.

In solving the problems of this kind, allow as follows: 

One fifth (over actual measurement) for 4-inch matched flooring. 

One fourth (over actual measurement) for 3-inch matched flooring. 

One sixth (over actual measurement) for matched siding. 

One sixth (over actual measurement) for ship lap siding. 

One inch of the width of each board, in ordinary lap siding. 

EXAMPLE. How much 3-inch flooring is required for a room 

18' x 15'? 

1815 2771, no. of board ft. in floor. 

1 of 2771698, no. of board ft. allowed for matching. 

27769347, total board ft. required, counting the fractional part of a foot as a whole foot.
