Plastering 

The cost of plastering is generally estimated by the square yard. The square (100 sq. ft.) is sometimes used, but not often. 

Estimates generally include labor and materials. The estimator must know the number of coats desired; whether lath is to be used, and what kind; and whether furring is necessary.

Furring is attaching to brick or tile walls wooden strips to which the lath can be nailed. Furring and metal lath are usually charged separately. 

Methods of calculation vary. Follow the process in the solution below.

VOCATIONAL PROBLEMS 

Papering

The unit in wall paper measure is the roll, which is 18 in. wide and 8 yd. long. American papers come in double rolls, or bolts 

18 in. wide and 16 yd. long. These bolts are cut into whole strips each as long as the height of the wall to be papered, the remainder of each bolt, if any, being wasted. A half of a double roll can be bought. French and English papers come in bolts 12 yd. long, equivalent to a roll and a half. 

The prices quoted on wall paper include putting the paper on the walls.

In figuring the allowance for an opening, in an ordinary room in a residence, it is customary to multiply the entire height of the wall from floor to ceiling by the width of the opening. 

There are usually some spaces above doors, to be papered, but the waste ends of rolls can be utilized for this purpose in many cases. In case of an unusually high ceiling, the spaces above doors and windows are figured. 

In figuring ceilings it is customary to run the paper either the long way or the short way of the ceiling, as may be most economical. 

When the length of a wall is not an even multiple of 18", one strip has to be split lengthwise, and only a part of it used. In this case a whole strip is figured. 

Waste occurs from matching patterns, papering around irregular woodwork, etc. The student will not be required to figure this. 

EXAMPLE. How many double rolls of paper will be required for the walls of a room 11' 6" x 14' x 9', with 3 windows each 

3' 6" wide, and 2 doors each 4' wide? 

51′ (perim.) — 18′ 6″ (openings) =32′ 6″, net perim. 

32′ 6″÷18″ 213, or 22, no. of strips for walls 48' 

9'5, no. of strips from 1 double roll 

22÷5=4, no. of double rolls, or 4 double rolls and 1 single roll required. 

EXAMPLE. How many double rolls of paper will be required for a ceiling 11' 6" x 14'? 

Running the paper the short way: 

48'11' 4, no. of strips from 1 double roll. 

14'÷1′ = 10, no. of strips across ceiling. 

10÷4=22, no. double rolls, or 2 double rolls and 1 single roll required. 

Figure for yourself the number of double rolls that would be required if paper were run the long way of the room.

Figure the carpet as running the long way of the room unless otherwise instructed.

CHAPTER XXXI VOCATIONAL PROBLEMS (Continued) Brick Work 

Bricks vary in size. A "common brick," however, is generally 

8" x 4" x 2". Customs in estimating brick work vary in different localities. Usually, 22 bricks are allowed for each cubic foot of brick masonry. Figure this as follows: Add 1" to each dimension of a common brick to allow for mortar, and find how many times the product of these dimensions is contained in 1728 cu. in. 

In estimating the number of bricks required for a given job, actual measurements are taken, with full allowance for all openings, and with corners counted only once. If the full outside measurement of a foundation were taken, it would result in counting each corner twice. In estimating the labor of laying brick, however, full outside measurements are taken, and deductions are not allowed for openings unless so specified. 

WRITTEN 

1. How many bricks are required for a wall 40 ft. long, 5 ft. high, and 1 ft. thick? 

2. What will be the cost for brick for an 8-in. wall 28′ x 36', and 12' high, with 120 sq. ft. of openings, at $8.50 per M, if full thousands only can be bought? 

3. What would be the cost of brick for a wall as in the preceding problem, if for the facing the specifications called for a pressed brick that cost $14 per M and these could be bought in the exact quantity required?

The two 30-foot walls and the rear wall, each with 45 sq. ft. of openings, are to be faced with common brick; and the front wall, which has four openings of 18 sq. ft. each, is to be faced with pressed brick having the same dimensions as the common brick. USING CONCRETE 

Concrete 343 Concrete is a mixture of cement and sand, or of cement, sand, and crushed stone or gravel.

Cement is bought by the barrel, estimated at 400 lb. gross and 

376 lb. net. It is shipped in bags holding bbl. each. A bag contains approximately 1 cu. ft. 

Sand and stone are bought by the cubic yard. 

Mixtures. The materials to make concrete are mixed in varying proportions, depending upon the strength desired.

A mixture is referred to by naming in turn the number of parts of cement, sand, and gravel required to make it. Thus a "1, 2, 4 mixture'' is a mixture containing 1 part cement, 2 parts sand, and 4 parts gravel or stone. This is the mixture commonly used in work requiring no extraordinary strength to meet special strain. In sidewalk construction a 1-3-5 mixture is often used for the base and a 1-2-0 for the topping.

Voids. In mixing concrete the small particles fill in the voids or spaces between the larger pieces. It has been estimated that 

1.45 cu. yd. of unmixed materials must be provided to make 1 cu. yd. of mixture. When one of the materials used is gravel or stone, an extra 8% or 10% of the whole must be provided. 

Assuming that for the case in hand it is proper to figure on 45% voids, and to add 8% to total material because gravel is used, note the following solution.

EXAMPLE. How much cement shall be used in making 1 cu. yd. of a 1, 2, 4 mixture?

In a 1, 2, 4 mixture there are 1+2+4 parts, or 7 parts, of which 1 is cement. 

Multiplying of 1.45 by 27 (the no. of cu. ft. in 1 cu. yd.), then dividing by 

4 (the no. of cu. ft. in 1 bbl.), and increasing the total quantity 8%, we have 1 1.45 27 1 1.08 1 4 1 = 1.51, the no. of bbl. cement required.

WRITTEN. Using the same basis as in the above solution (45% voids and 8% added to total when stone or gravel is used), solve the following problems. 

1. How many barrels of cement are needed to make one cubic yard of a 1, 22, 5 mixture of concrete? 

2. How many barrels of cement are needed to make one cubic yard of a 1, 3, 5 mixture of concrete? 

3. How many barrels of cement are needed to make one cubic yard of a 1, 3, 6 mixture of concrete?

Good Roads 

It was officially estimated that in one year 42,743,500 tons of certain farm products were hauled to shipping points in the U. S. 

(Bulletin 49, U. S. Office of Public Roads). The average cost of hauling was 25¢ per ton per mile. The average distance was 

9 miles. The cost over concrete roads was 8¢ per ton per mile; over ordinary stone roads, 11.09¢; and over dirt roads, 39¢. Use the foregoing data in solving the problems following.

WRITTEN 

1. How much would the farmers of the United States have saved that year if they had used concrete roads in hauling all their crops to market? What per cent of the money paid was loss if all costs above 8¢ per mile are considered losses? 

2. If the farmers of Brown County hauled 150,000 tons of farm products to market last year at an average cost of 25¢ per ton per mile, and an average distance of 7 miles, what did they lose by using poor roads, if all costs above 8¢ per ton per mile are figured as losses? What per cent was their cost for hauling in excess of the cost required to haul over concrete roads? 

3. It costs a farmer $2.25 per ton to haul his wheat to a shipping point 9 miles from the farm. A commission merchant in New York ships a ton of wheat to Liverpool, 3100 miles, at $1.75 per ton. How much more per mile does it cost the farmer to haul a ton of wheat on the farm road than the commission merchant pays in shipping from New York to Liverpool? What per cent is the cost of shipping on the water of the cost of hauling on the farm roads?

HEATING 

Heating 347 Steam. A steam radiator consists of sections (coils) through which the steam passes. Heat is radiated from the coils in proportion to the number of sq. ft. in their total surface. A square foot of radiation is one square foot of coil surface.

The quantity of radiation required for heating a room depends upon the construction of the building, the amount of exposed wall surface of the room, the number and size of outside openings (glass), the outside temperature, the degree of heat desired, and many other factors. 

Contractors have various methods of estimating the quantity of radiation. In solving the problems of this section, however, you will use the following schedule, which is recognized in building as a standard for computing radiation. Schedule for computing minimum quantities of steam radiation at 

70° F. with outside temperature at minus 10° F. (a) 1 foot of radiation for every 300 cu. ft. of air space, plus (b) 1 foot of radiation for every 15 sq. ft. net exposed wall surface, plus (c) 1 foot of radiation for every 2 sq. ft. of glass surface.

In measuring glass surface the full opening in the wall shall be figured. Outside door openings shall be taken as glass.

For hot water radiation add 60% to quantity necessary for steam radiation.

For vapor systems add 20% to quantity necessary for steam. 

For heating to 65°, estimate as for 70° and multiply by .94. 

For heating to 60°, estimate as for 70° and multiply by .87. 

For heating to 55°, estimate as for 70° and multiply by .81. 

For heating to 50°, estimate as for 70° and multiply by .75. 

EXAMPLE. How much steam radiation is necessary for heating to 70° a room 16 ft. x 12 ft. x 9 ft., with the front and one side wall exposed, if the room has 2 windows each 3 ft. x 6 ft. and one window 5 ft. x 6 ft.?

VOCATIONAL PROBLEMS 

16X12X9=1728, no. of cu. ft., air space. 

1728÷300=5.76, or 6, no. of sq. ft. radiation (a) (16+12) X9=252, no. of sq. ft., total exposed wall surface. 

2×(3×6)+(5×6)=72, no. of sq. ft., glass. 

252 sq. ft.-72 sq. ft. 180 sq. ft., net exposed wall surface. 

180 sq. ft. 15 sq. ft. = 12, no. sq. ft. radiation (b). 

72 sq. ft.÷2 sq. ft. 36, no. sq. ft. radiation (c). 

6+12+36=54, no. of sq. ft. total radiation.

NOTE. In all solutions compute on the basis of 10° below zero, outside temperature.

Standard Time 

The earth makes one rotation in 24 hours. Since there are 360° in its circumference, a point anywhere on its surface must pass through 15° of longitude in one hour of time.

The circumference of the earth at the equator is approximately 25,000 miles. A point near the equator would therefore pass through about 1000 miles of space in an hour.

The meridians of longitude approach each other gradually as they extend farther from the equator and nearer to the poles, but for practical purposes we may consider that one hour of time corresponds to 1000 miles (east and west) in all parts of the United States.

In order to facilitate the management of time schedules for train service in the 

United States and Canada, the railroads in 1883 agreed upon a plan for making four standardized time belts, each having near its center one of the following meridians of west longitude: 75°, 90°, 105°, and 120°. The time adopted for each belt is sun time on the meridian for that belt. This is called Standard Time.

The time belts and the meridians are shown on the accompanying map. The edges of the time belts are very irregular, because each railroad changes time one hour at the most convenient division point in its own system. 140 160 180° 160°

351 live. Sun time and standard time are the same in that city. 

Points located in the same time belt but west of the meridian find standard time "faster" than sun time. Points located in that belt but east of its meridian find standard time "slower" than sun time. This disadvantage is more than overcome by the benefits, unless a place is near the eastern edge of its time belt.

LAND SURVEYS 

Land Surveys 353 Originally, farm lands of the United States had no regularity as regards boundaries or size. This is largely true today of the eastern states; that is, the states east of the Appalachian mountains and parts of the states adjacent to the western slopes of these mountains.

When Virginia ceded her claims to the great Northwest territory, the federal government instituted a method of regular surveys for all government land. This method has been followed generally throughout the country ever since. The plan of surveying as it now exists follows: 

A principal meridian is a true north and south line established as a basis for government surveys. There are in the United States twenty-four principal meridians.

The meridians established for the purpose of government survey must not be confounded with meridians of longitude used by astronomers and navigators, as they are entirely different. The principal meridians used in surveying land are usually located with reference to some natural landmark, such as the mouth of a river, and are not equi-distant from each other. 

The first principal meridian begins at the junction of the Miami and Ohio rivers, and forms the boundary between Indiana and Ohio; the second begins at a point two and a half miles west of the junction of the Little Blue and Ohio rivers; the third begins at the mouth of the Ohio and runs north to the northern boundary of Illinois; the fourth begins at the mouth of the Illinois river and extends north through Wisconsin and Minnesota, governing surveys in Wisconsin, Northern Minnesota and Western Illinois; the fifth begins at the mouth of the Arkansas and governs surveys in Arkansas, Missouri, the greater part of Minnesota, and Dakota east of the Missouri river; the sixth is the meridian of 97 deg., 22 min. west, extending south to latitude 37 deg. and north to the Missouri, and controlling the surveys in Kansas, Nebraska, the Dakotas south and west of the Missouri, Wyoming, and all of Colorado except a portion along the Rio Grande, the survey of which is governed by the New Mexico meridian.

Besides these there are eighteen principal meridians in the United States survey known by names instead of numbers.

A base line is a line which crosses the principal meridians at right angles to them, and is used as a basis of measurements north or south.

Intersections of meridians and base lines are marked by stone monuments.

VOCATIONAL PROBLEMS 

Across the principal meridians and base lines at right angles, other lines are run six miles apart, which divide the territory into square tracts six miles square. 

These tracts are called townships.

Correction Lines.

R3W T4N R3E surface of the earth is convex, all meridians converge as the latitude increases. Townships being exactly retangular, their east and west boundaries are

not exactly parallel with the IV. principal meridian by which they are governed. This creates a necessity for occasional adjustments, which are made upon correction lines, as shown by the diagram.

bered north or south from the base line. Thus "town 1 north," "town 1 south," etc.

A range is a line of townships running north and south, and is designated by its number east or west from the principal meridian. 

Thus the range of townships lying next a prime meridian is known as range 1, east or west, the next range 2, east or west, etc.

A complete description of the location of a township states both its north and south location and its east and west location, thus: 1 N, R 2 E, to which is added in legal descriptions "of the third principal meridian, county of Morgan."

The above would be read, in full, "Township one north, range two east of the third principal meridian, county of Morgan."

Sections. Each township is divided into thirty-six sections, each 1 mile square and containing 640 acres. 

These sections are numbered serially from 1 to 36 inclusive, beginning at the N. E. cor. and running W. in the first tier, E. in the second, etc. 

All fractional sections except those due to lakes, rivers, and other natural boundaries are thrown into the northern and western rows. Sections are further divided into halves, quarters, and eighths. An eighth section is also called a "forty," because it contains 40 acres.

What is Ashbrook's net income on the 20 acres if he produces 17 bales averaging 508 lb., the price of cotton being 14.20¢ per lb.?

761.10, all of which were considered additional costs of the cotton. General and administrative expenses of the agency amounted to $3,257.62 during this month. What was the net gain by buying its cotton through its own agency instead of buying it through brokers at 12.75¢ per lb., delivered at the mill?

842,000 bales. The United States produced 11,506,000 bales. What per cent of the world's cotton did the United States produce!

CHAPTER XXXII SHIPPING AND TRANSPORTATION Shipping by Rail 

In shipping merchandise, the question which most frequently presents itself is whether it is cheapest to ship by freight, express, or parcel post.

Each method of shipment has its advantages and disadvantages from the standpoints of speed, security, and other points relating to the service given by the carrier, and sometimes one of these will be the principal factor in deciding how to ship.

Parcel Post. The parcel post has been treated at the end of 

Chapter III. The student should review Postal Rates at this time. 

Freight. Commodities are classified as First Class, Second Class, 

Third Class, etc., and different rates given for each. Different rates are given for carload lots (C.L.) and for less than carload lots (L.C.L.). Each railroad publishes lists, called tariffs, of rates from each of the cities on its line to every other city. To determine in advance what the freight charges will be, it is necessary to know, first, to what class the goods shipped belong; second, the rate on that class from shipping point to destination. Except for coal, and other commodities shipped by the ton, the rate is quoted per 100 lb. Less than 100 lb. is charged as 100 lb. 

In problem 1 following, all merchandise is first class, L.C.L., and the rate is stated for each package.

Express. There are many different scales of express rates in use in the United States, each designated by a number. To find what the express charges will be, the shipper must know: 1st, whether his goods are first, second, or third class; 2d, what scale of rates applies between the point of shipment and the destination (determined by consulting a Directory of Express Stations); and 3d, what is the rate, on this scale, for packages of the weight and class of his shipment. The express rate is also affected by the valuation. A shipper can sometimes get a reduced rate by agreeing to a low valuation in case of loss.

In the problem following, all packages are first class. The weight and scale number are given, and the student will consult the table herewith to find the rate. 359

Shipping by Motor Truck. An important industry has developed in hauling freight by motor truck. Many producers, also, take their live stock and agricultural products to market in privately owned trucks. Freight for shipping by truck is classified in four classes, as it is in shipments by rail. 

Freight is handled by motor truck companies in two ways. A load may be taken to a specified destination by contract, or shipments of freight may be carried by a trucking company operating as a common carrier. If the shipment is made by contract, the charges are based upon cost of operation plus overhead expense. In carrying freight when operating as a common carrier, making regular trips over a specified route, the trucking company uses the classified schedules of freight and bases the charges on the rates applicable to different kinds of merchandise. 

WRITTEN

2. Two brothers drove their own truck loaded with beef cattle from their ranch in Wyoming to the stock yards in Chicago, a distance of approximately 1200 miles. On the return trip they took a load of 5000 lb. of merchandise to the ranch. 

Their expense for operating the truck, not allowing for their labor, averaged 10¢ a mile. If the two loads were equal in weight, what was the cost per ton per mile?

SHIPPING AND TRANSPORTATION Air Transport 

During recent years the development of transportation by air has been very rapid. The number of passengers carried by air transport increased from 5,872 in 1926 to 550,000 in 1933. The increase in the transportation of express packages was equally great. The record shows 6,467 pounds in 1926 and 1,660,000 pounds in 1933. The mail service has been explained briefly in Chapter III. The highest record was made in 1931, when airplanes carried more than 9,000,000 pounds of mail. In 1934 the scheduled time for Coast to Coast trips was 22 hours westbound and 19 hours eastbound. The newer air liners render much of their service at a speed approximating 180 miles an hour.

A combination of air-rail transportation is provided by means of which passengers and mail or express packages are carried from any starting point to the desired destination in much shorter time than was formerly possible.

CHAPTER XXXIII BUILDING AND LOAN ASSOCIATIONS 

A Building and Loan Association is a form of corporation organized to promote home ownership by men and women who have moderate but regular incomes but who have not sufficient free capital with which to finance the building of homes. The building and loan association makes loans to these persons, and the loans are repaid in small regular payments with interest. 

In this way the borrower is enabled to own his home and to pay for it in a reasonable length of time in payments not much larger than his previous expense for rent. A fund for this purpose is provided by persons who buy shares in the association as an investment. This plan is advantageous to the home builder and profitable to the investor.

Charters incorporating building and loan associations are issued by the various state governments and also by the federal government of the United States. The charters of the local associations differ in details, and the exact method of doing business in each association is regulated by its charter and its by-laws and rules. 

Capital for use in making loans to borrowers, as stated above, is provided by selling shares of stock in the corporation. Shares of stock may be (a) installment shares, which are to be paid for in stated dues each month or other comparatively short period of time, (b) investment or full paid income shares, which are fully paid for when purchased or have been matured by dues and are later used by the shareholder as a source of income, (c) accumulative or prepaid shares, which are shares that mature automatically by the addition of dividends from time to time after the payment of a lump sum (usually $50 or $75) when the shares are purchased, or (d) optional savings shares, which are shares upon which either 369

BUILDING AND LOAN ASSOCIATIONS 

the amount of the stated payment may vary or the period for the payments may be irregular or both of these conditions may be used. 

A building and loan association has other sources of income besides the dues on installment shares of stock and the interest on loans. It may charge membership fees and impose fines for delinquent payment of dues. It also realizes some profit on the withdrawal or repurchase value of the shares of members who for any reason withdraw from the association, for there is usually a difference between the book value and the withdrawal value.

A shareholder who withdraws from the association before he finishes paying for his shares, does not, as a rule, receive a cash withdrawal payment equal to the full book value of the shares. 

The plan contemplates that this payment returns to the shareholder all the money he has paid in dues and pays him a fixed rate of participation in the profits. The exact rate of participation is determined by the rules of the association. 

If the building industry is inactive and but few requests for loans are received, an association may desire to repurchase some of the shares that are held by its members. Under these circumstances or any others making the association desire to repurchase shares, the members whose shares are repurchased receive the full book value.

The book value of shares in a building and loan association can be found in the records of the association by adding to the total amount paid to date on each share the amount of profits that have been apportioned. As the profits arise from the sources mentioned above, each share participates in them. 

The matured value of shares varies in different associations. 

It may be as small as $25 a share or as large as $500, but nearly all associations make the matured value of their shares either $100 or $200.

The plans used by building and loan associations in issuing and selling shares have minor differences, but they may be grouped in two large classes. In one of these, the shares are sold in series, all the shares in a given series being of the same date and maturing on the same future date. If a purchaser buys shares at some time after the issue, it is necessary for him to

WITHDRAWAL OR REPURCHASE VALUE 

371 pay all back dues to the date of his purchase or to pay a sufficient premium to cover the back dues. A second series may be issued by the association at a date somewhat later, then a third series, and so on, if the association meets with a strong demand for loans justifying each additional series, and if capital finds this form of investment more inviting than other forms. 

In the other class of building and loan associations, a purchaser may buy shares at any time, and he is not required to pay back dues and thereby make the stock effective as of a given past date. It will be seen at once that this general plan is a liberal modification of the one described above. Each sale of shares virtually becomes a series in itself and will run its course to maturity without reference to the maturity dates of other holdings of shares in the association. Under this plan a person whose application has been passed upon favorably may buy shares and secure a loan at such time as may be most convenient. 

EXAMPLE 

Harry Austin subscribed for 20 installment shares in a building and loan association. Under his agreement, he has paid $1 per share each month for five years. He now wishes to withdraw. What is the withdrawal value of his shares?

We will assume that the withdrawal value is based upon average earnings of 41% on the payments. Austin has made sixty monthly payments of $20 each, or a total payment of $1200. 

The first payment has been earning 41% for 60 months; the second payment, for 59 months; the third, for 58 months; and so on to the end of the series. The last payment has earned dividends one month.

The process of computing the dividends on these payments separately would be long and laborious. It is customary to treat them as a descending arithmetical series, and to equate the time and compute simple interest for the equated period on the entire amount paid.

We need to find the sum of all the numbers of months Austin's payments have been earning dividends. The sum of all the terms

BUILDING AND LOAN ASSOCIATIONS 

of such a series may be found as follows: Multiply the sum of the first term and the last term by one half the number of terms. 

First term 60+ last term 1=61 

One half the number of terms of 60=30

Sum of all the terms 

30 x 61=1830 As the payments were all equal and the periods were equal, we can now find the equated time by dividing the sum of all the terms by the number of payments made. 

1830÷÷60=301, the number of months the whole amount paid in should be credited with earning dividends. 

Dividends on $1200 at 41% for 30 mo. 

Total payments made 

Withdrawal value of Austin's shares $ 137.25 1200.00 $1337.25 

Find the withdrawal value of the following holdings of shares. of building and loan stock: 

1. 10 shares, monthly payments 36 mo. at $1 per share, 4%. 

2. 20 shares, monthly payments 48 mo. at $1 per share, 5%. 

3. 50 shares, monthly payments 24 mo. at $1 per share, 3%. 

Distribution of Profits

Building and loan profits are ordinarily distributed on the basis of the book value of the shares. The profits of the association are usually determined semi-annually and are credited to the shares or are distributed in the same manner as dividends in other corporations. In installment shares, not yet fully paid for, the dividends are usually credited to the shares as so much additional payment. They serve to hasten the date of the maturity of the shares.

Each building and loan association, continuing an indefinite number of series of shares, has at all times several series not yet matured. If the association sells shares continuously and allows each sale to be virtually a series in itself, the computation of the distribution of profits must take this fact into account. It must take into account also all causes of variation in the book value of the different kinds of shares as affected by the rules of the association. An example is given to make the principle clear.

DISTRIBUTION OF PROFITS 

373 

EXAMPLE 

On July 1, 1933, Martin Robinson subscribed for 20 building and loan shares, on which he paid $1 per share on the first day of each month. The matured value of each share was to be $100. 

The association contained many other shareholders, who held various amounts issued to them at different past dates. It is required to know the value of Robinson's shares at the end of the first semi-annual accounting period.

What investment for one month is equivalent to Robinson's investment in one share for the six months of the accounting period?

The first month one share represented an investment of $1; the second month it represented an investment of $2; the third month, $3; the fourth month, $4; the fifth month, $5; and the sixth month, $6. Adding these monthly investments, we find that one share represented the same as an investment of $21 for one month.

As Robinson had subscribed for 50 shares, and each share for the six months period represented an investment equivalent to $21 for one month, his entire holding represented the equivalent of the investment of fifty times $21, or $1050, for one month. 

As stated above, there were many other shareholders in the association, and they held different amounts and different kinds of shares issued to them on various past dates. Robinson's holding and each of the other holdings may now be considered as separate series of shares. The investment in each of these series must be reduced to its equivalent in terms of a single investment for one month.

Let us assume that all these holdings, when reduced to terms of an equivalent one-month investment, show that the total investment in the association for the accounting period was equivalent to a one-month investment of $29,280. Many of the shares included in this total were subscribed for previous to the present accounting period. If dividends have been accumulated on some of these, their book value as of the beginning of the accounting period is taken into consideration. It is of course greater than the book value of Robinson's shares. Other shares may have been subscribed for during the six months of the accounting period. These will participate in the distribution of

BUILDING AND LOAN ASSOCIATIONS 

profits with a book value based only upon the number of months they have been in effect. All these differences are taken into account.

If we know that the profit to be distributed is $439.20, and we find as stated above that the entire investment for the accounting period is equivalent to a one-month investment of $29,280, we can divide the amount of profit by the total onemonth investment and find the rate of profit per dollar per month. $29,280) $439.20 (.015 

Rate of gain per dollar per month= .015

We have already learned that Robinson's investment in each share for the six months of the accounting period is equivalent to a one-month investment of $21. His profit, therefore, may be found as follows: 

Profit on one share=.015 times $21-$00.315 

Profit on fifty shares 50 times $00.315=$15.75

Given the rate of gain per dollar per month, the one-month investment equivalent to each share, and the number of shares held by a shareholder, the various dividends of the shareholders may be determined in a similar manner.

THE BORROWER 

The Borrower 375 As a rule, the borrower must be a member of the association. 

He becomes a member by subscribing for shares, as already explained. The total matured value of his shares must be as large as the loan he secures.

The borrower pays interest on his loan, the same as he would do if he secured a loan from a bank or an individual. At the same time, in his capacity as a member of the association, he holds shares that participate in the profits.

Building and loan associations have two methods of computing credit for the payments made on loans by borrowers. One of these methods is based upon the principle that underlies the accumulation of a sinking fund, with which you are already familiar. The other method is an application of the principle seen in amortization of debts by the payment of a stated sum, which is separated into two parts, one part being used to pay accrued interest and the other part being applied to the direct reduction of the debt. The latter is called the direct reduction method. 

A borrower who is paying dues on the shares for which he has subscribed and is also paying interest on the loan he has secured, may pay more than the required amount each period if he desires to mature the shares rapidly. A condition of this kind is shown in the page of a borrower's pass book below.

CHAPTER XXXIV HOME BUDGETING 

The general objective of all home budgets it to make the financial life of the family happy. Happiness depends upon the state of mind. If one keeps his desires within his income, the actual amount of the income, above the provision for necessities, will be of less consequence. 

Home budgeting is simply the planned spending of the family income in such a way as to derive the greatest value for the money expended and to avoid financial straits. A budget should be flexible and should be adapted to the special needs of the family using it. It should not be the arbitrary setting aside of a certain sum for each type of expense. It constitutes facing facts by the family spenders.

There is danger in making the family budget so difficult and complicated as to discourage its use. The budget should make family spending intelligent. The simpler it is, the more likely it is to be used from year to year. 

SAMPLE BUDGETS FOR DISCUSSION A Family of Four INCOME (YR.) 

$1,200 $1,800 $2,400 $3,000 $6,000 $10,000 1. Food.... 336 420 520 600 900 1,200 2. 

Shelter, rented or owned.. 240 408 480 600 1,080 1,500 3. 

Clothing.

BUDGET DISTRIBUTION 

377 The sample budgets shown on the preceding page are intended to suggest a good apportionment of income in meeting expenses. 

Families having the specified incomes will modify these budgets in many particulars, but a wise and careful consideration of the future needs as well as the present desires of the members of the family should govern all such modifications.

In the discussion of these sample budgets, it will be helpful to keep in mind the subordinate items included in each major subdivision. 

1. Various estimates of the cost of food for a family have been made. These estimates run from an average of $3 or $4 a person per week, in families having moderate incomes, to higher amounts. By careful spending the expense per week for each person may be reduced slightly without sacrificing real food values. There should be a sufficient quantity of the right kinds of food to insure good health and proper nourishment for the family. 

2. The sum set aside for shelter may be a fixed amount if the family is renting. It will probably run from 20% to 25% of the income. A family owning its home but owing a debt on the property, should set aside in the budget a similar amount to cover taxes, insurance, and up-keep and to provide for payments on the debt. 

3. The allowance for clothing will be from 121% to 15% of the income. It will be governed by the ages and the occupations of the members of the family. 

4. Operating expenses will include such items as heat, light, water, ice, telephone, laundry, cleaning and pressing clothing, miscellaneous household supplies, wages of employed help, and countless minor items connected with running a home. All these combined will take from 121% to 15% of the income. 

5. Health, Recreation, and Advancement together constitute one of the most important subdivisions of the budget. The amount devoted to these items may profitably be increased rather freely as the income increases.

This subdivision includes the education of the children, the expenses for vacation outings and for travel, all payments

HOME BUDGETING 

toward the support of churches and welfare organizations in general, the systematic pursuit of open-air recreation and of club membership, the cost of books and magazines for the family, direct expense for services of physicians and dentists, and other items of expense logically falling under physical, mental, and spiritual betterment.

In families not having members in college or university these items of expense will require from 10% to 15% of the income; if members of the family are away from home in college or university, the expense will be increased. 

6. Savings and Reserve should have a place in every budget, however small the income. A considerable degree of sacrifice of present wishes should be made in order to set aside something as a protection against unforeseen reverses, serious periods of illness or of unemployment, and the needs that will arise in old age. This part of the budget will also make it possible for the family to accumulate funds to pay such current debts as may have resulted from financial reverses now past, and thereby to balance the budget.

In many instances it will be helpful to divide the funds allotted to Savings and Reserve into two parts, one for building up a permanent estate and one to be used as an emergency fund in meeting critical current situations in family finance. In incomes of more than $3000 a year, the amount apportioned to Savings and Reserve should increase rapidly in proportion to other parts of the budget. This point is especially important in families whose income increases rapidly from any cause or who receive large amounts suddenly by inheritance or gift. 

7. Automobile expenses are listed separately in the table of budgets suggested for discussion. Most families will find this plan of budgeting desirable, but the item may be classified under Operating Expenses of the home. The financial effect of owning and operating an automobile has such far-reaching results in relation to all other parts of the budget, that its proper control is a matter of vital concern if budgeting is expected to be successful. A separate place in the budget helps to keep this

BUDGET DISTRIBUTION 

379 item of expense under constant inspection. In moderate incomes it will need an allowance of 8% to 10% of the total.

Planning and Administering the Budget The planning of the budget is very important. If it is well planned, the administration of it will be efficient and enjoyable. 

Someone has aptly said that "planning the family budget is doing your worrying before you have spent your money instead of afterwards." In administering the budget the expense of certain items comes in relatively large sums and is not distributed evenly over the pay periods of income. Such expenses as life insurance premiums, the taxes on property or income, payments on a large debt on the home, vacation costs, or the cost of a new automobile, may be provided for by allowing the budget fund under the appropriate head to accumulate temporarily; or these expenses may be met by special additions to an emergency fund included in Savings and Reserve. In either case, expenses such as these should be met without seriously disarranging the fixed monthly needs of the family.

In planning the budget for a year, all the expenses of the preceding year, as shown by actual records or estimated carefully,

HOME BUDGETING 

should be listed; and the list should be analyzed to locate sources of waste and to correct imperfect allotment of funds. The budget may then be administered by any one of the following plans: (a) Using a dole system, under which the pay received at the end of each pay period is divided and placed in seperate envelopes corresponding to the parts of the budget; (b) Using the allowance plan, which is a record-keeping system giving on paper the amount allowed for each kind of expense and charging against that sum the items of expense under it from week to week; (c) Using the charge account system, in which goods are bought on credit, and a record of invoices is used in checking the budget; or (d) Using the joint bank account system, in which a man and wife both write checks on their joint account in the bank to pay for articles purchased and to meet other expenses, and the stubs in the check books serve in posting expenditures on the budget record from time to time.

All these plans are in use, and each has its special advantages under conditions to which it is peculiarly adapted. In general, the least satisfactory plan is that of running open accounts at the stores where members of the family buy goods. In many instances the greatest need in a family, when expenses are running high, is to pay in cash or bank checks whenever purchases are made or other kinds of expense incurred. The faithful use of this one device in administering the home budget will straighten out many financial difficulties in the home. 

One practical objection to the joint bank account checking system is the additional time it requires for writing the checks wherever and whenever purchases are made in stores or elsewhere. It is often true that many other customers are waiting their turn at the counter or the cashier's desk, and the interests and attitude of all these must be kept in mind by a considerate person in expediting business. This delay and consequent interference with others may be overcome by writing a check and cashing it at the bank before starting to make the purchases.

PREPARATION OF FAMILY BUDGET 

381 The dole plan and the allowance plan are well suited for use in families that do not have a bank account in any bank, and they are very helpful as subordinate features in connection with budgeting even when a single bank account or a joint bank account is used as the method of control of the funds arising from the family income. Each family will choose the method or the combination of methods most satisfactory in its own situation in meeting expenses systematically and keeping expenses within income. 

PREPARATION OF FAMILY BUDGET Illustrative Example 

The study of an illustrative example will help to show the steps by which a family may build up its budgeting practice on a solid basis instead of trying to start by setting arbitrary standards in the various parts of the budget. Such an example is shown in tabulated form on page 383. It represents the experience of Mr. and Mrs. Henry Jones, who wished to systematize their expenditures of money and to regulate their financial affairs in accordance with a budget so prepared as to fit their own resources and their needs. 

By examining the available data Mr. and Mrs. Jones discovered that their only saving to date had been the item of life insurance premium, and that even in this important matter Mr. Jones was not carrying sufficient insurance.

HOME BUDGETING 

Their food bill was higher than it should have been, and they think that this was due to entertaining and to the lack of sufficient attention to buying carefully. Their rent expense has been $45 a month, and this is about right for their income. They have been spending too much for clothing. The item of expense under the heading of Health was high last year, due principally to the serious illness of their son, Henry Jr. They decided that they had not been spending too much for recreation, nor on their car. Notwithstanding some of these expenses were not out of proportion to the income, they believe that certain items can be reduced to make possible a wiser distribution of the income. 

The analysis of expenditures shows that Mr. and Mrs. Jones owe the hospital $200 on account of the illness of Henry Jr., $40 to Hale's Men's Store on open account, and about $83 in various smaller amounts to other stores where they have been buying on credit. They have decided to correct this condition, if possible, in next year's budget.

The first step in their retrenchment program will be to move to a house that they can rent for $40 a month. They will also reduce cost of new clothing and of the care of their clothing in cleaning and pressing. They will watch for little leaks in expenditures, and will stop as many of these as possible. Mr. 

Jones will save some of the automobile expense by walking to his work, and Mrs. Jones will get along with less help in her household work from week to week. By these measures and other minor savings they expect to be able to begin the second year with a well balanced budget.

Mr. Jones receives $200 a month, payable in semi-monthly payments of $100 each. The Joneses have two small children. 

They live in a city, own and use an automobile, and employ help in the home at irregular intervals. As the basis for planning their budget, they have collected data showing the amounts they spent for various purposes during the preceding year. Study the charted list of these expenditures and the list of estimates made by Mr. and Mrs. Jones for use during the year for which they are planning a budget.

Mar. 1 Balances 44 39 120 The table above shows how the budget of Mr. and Mrs. Jones worked in actual use for two months. They decided to use the allowance plan. At the beginning of each pay period, in this case two weeks, the pay received is recorded in the proper column. The expenditures for each week are recorded as distributed under the budget headings, and the total amount expended each week is recorded in the column for totals. 

Study this table carefully, and notice particularly what happens at the end of each month. In several of the budgetary sections the expenditures during the month prove to be less than the corresponding allowances. This saving causes a surplus in those sections and in the section of totals. 670 800 1259 11 10 2860 2750 17 30 2850 2850 3500 3500 195 13 55 800 149 110 1730 195 20 25 13 55 20 25 800 800 1259 1259 2860 2860 800 149 110 that these surpluses are carried forward into the record of the following month. At the end of a month a surplus in any section of the budget record stands in the column of expenditures, but in being carried to the next month it is transferred to the allowance column and increases the allowance for that month. 

If this process is continued from month to month in any given section of the budget record, a special reserve fund is accumulated in that particular section of the budget. In this way larger expenditures in that section may be made later. 

This point appears clearly in the sections on Clothing and Health-Recreation-Advancement. The record indicates that the Jones family may be planning a vacation trip later.

1. Analyze the data on this page, and then prepare a oneyear budget for the Reynolds household. Make the arrangement of material similar to that on page 383. 

Data for working out a budget for the Reynolds family: (1) The family consists of Mr. and Mrs. Reynolds and their son, who is a junior in high school. (2) Mr. Reynolds receives a weekly salary of $40. (3) Mr. and Mrs. Reynolds bought a home several years ago, on which they have been paying $250 a year, with interest at 6% on the unpaid balance payable each year at the time of the payment on the principal. The unpaid balance of this debt at the time of beginning the budget is $1250, with the annual payment due in May. 

The budget begins to control expenditures on July 1. (4) The family does not keep a car; and there are no outstanding debts, except as stated above. (5) Taxes for the year now ending were $72; insurance was $24; and repairs on the house were $60. (6) Mr. Reynolds must be prepared to pay his life insurance premiums in quarterly installments of $18 each, due on the 20th of September, December, March, and June. (7) Last year Mr. Reynolds bought one share of stock in the corporation for which he works. He paid $60, and he wishes to make the same payment on stock each year for a term of years.
